CHAPTER XL

T H E CENSUS OF T H E PROVINCE.
PART I.-PRELIMINARY OPERATIONS.
834. Territorial sub-division into Census units.—The unit adopted for Census purposes was the
area of which the Census was to be taken by a single enumerator.
This was called the block. The
blocks were grouped into circles, each of which was under a supervisor, and the circles were again
grouped into charges, with a superintendent
over each. These charges were arranged by tahsils, a
gazetted officer being placed, wherever possible, in charge of each tahsil. A map of the tahsil showing
the village boundaries was taken, and the population of each village according to the Census of 1868
marked upon it. With the help of this map it was easy to fix all blocks which consisted of integral
villages to decide roughly how many blocks each of the larger villages should be divided into, and to mark
out the rural circles and charges. In fixing these, established boundaries, such as those of police and
patwlri's circles, zaildaVs circuits, wards in towns and the like, were followed as far as possible. Patwiris' circles were found especially convenient, the patwari being often appointed supervisor of his circle.
The sub-division was strictly one of area ; that is to say, wherever a village was divided into two
or more blocks, the lands attached to it were also divided. Thus the collective blocks of a tahsil included
the whole of its area ; and it was carefully explained to each enumerator that he was responsible for the
enumeration, not only of persons present in the portion of the village site that was under his charge, but
also of all such as might be present in the fields or other area attached to it. The directions laid down
that in making this division, well-defined lines, such as roads, canals, streams, and the like, should be
followed. This was one of the points to which too little attention was paid, the boundary not unfrequently
consisting of an imaginary line drawn across the area, which had no tangible existence.
835. T h e index-register and circle-map.—As the blocks were roughly marked off, an index-register
(Census Form A, General Letter A, Appendix D) was drawn up which served as an index to the Censussub-divisions of the tahsil, showing the relation which they bore to the various villages, the number of
houses, the population by last Census for each, the names of the Census staff, and so forth. Perhaps the
most important columns in this form, and the ones which needed most insistance to ensure their being properly filled up, were columns 5 and 6 headed " Subsidiary parts.'' In these columns were shown ail the
minor hamlets and groups of houses or temporary huts which are so often scattered about the village area,
and which, without some such record, were so likely to escape enumeration. The Deputy Commissioner
of Ambila took upon himself to omit these columns as useless. But it seems to me that thev are absolutely essential; and especially so in the Multin and Derajat divisions and the hill tracts, where tinv
hamlets are dotted about all over the district. Some suggestions for facilitating the preparation of this form
will be found in the section on Difficulties and Suggestions.
The register, when the rough entry of blocks was complete, was compared with the list of survey villages
which is kept up in the tahsils, to ensure that no portion of the area had escaped inclusion in some one
block or other. Each supervisor was then furnished with a copy of so much of the register as referred to
his circle, together with a rough sketch-map of the circle showing the village boundaries and the rough
division into blocks; and he was then sent into his circle to conduct the house numbering, and to rina'llv
demarcate the blocks where these latter consisted of portions of villages or towns.
836. House-lists and block-maps.—The next step was to prepare a rough sketch-map of each block.
showing its boundaries and the position of every house included within them. Together with this was
prepared a house-list (Census Form B, Appendix D), the two standing to each other in the relation of the
settlement field map and register. In this house-list was entered a detail, not only of the houses of the
block, but also of the families dwelling in each house. This detail enabled the supervisor to complete the
demarcation of blocks, finally fixing their limits in towns and in those villages which were too large for a
single enumerator to deal with. Each house then had a separate number allotted to it, the numbering being
consecutive within each block, and following the order in which it would be most convenient for the enu"
merator to visit the houses on the night of the Census. In Kulu the houses were numbered downhill
beginning from the highest point, to which the enumerator climbed up during the dav, and so had no uphill
work after he had once begun his enumeration. This was a capital device which should be followed in all
hilly tracts.
It was laid down that each homestead (aiddi) should have a separate series of house numbers, except
in the case of towns, in which each muhallah was to be numbered separately. These directions did not
work well. The small scattered hamlets of which in some parts of the Province the village consists
often include only four or five houses ; while in many towns the muhattahs are not wards, but streets or
quarters consisting of dwelling-houses as opposed to shops, and do not include the bazaars at all ; and
even where there are true muhallahs, they are often too large for the purpose, as onlv one numbering
can work at a time in the unit which is to bear a continuous series of numbers. The best plan
1 probably be to keep to one series of numbers throughout the whole of a village or Census circle.
whichever might in each case be smaller.

The number allotted to each house was marked on it in a conspicuous manner, so as to bo easily
visible by the enumerator when he made his rounds on the Census nights
The method of affixing the
numbers varied from place to place. Quite the worst plan I know of was to write the numbers on pieces
of paper stuck up on the wall. In Talagang wooden pegs were driven into the wall, the ends whitewashed, and the numbers painted on them. But unless the pegs were extraordinarily large the numbers.
would be too small to be conspicuous. The plan of marking the numbers on boards hung up on the
houses is probably the best for towns; for though the boards cost something (less than one anna each in
Rohtak) yet they can be kept up as permanent numbers, and can be shifted as new houses are built or
old ones pulled down. For sheds and gipsies' huts boards must be used ; but in villages the be>t plan i> to
paint a white square on the wall, near the door-post and under the shelter of the eaves, and to paiht th<

It must be remembered that what is wanted is, not a map to scale, but the merest diagram of r e s tive position. Thus I found that some of these maps were made on enormous sheets of paper in nrd< r
to show the outer boundaries of the block area. This was quite unnecessary. The limiting lines ot tl<
block external to the homestead should either be represented by lines marked with the name 01 tin
road or other feature for which they stand without regard to scale, or, perhaps a better plan, be si paral<-'.\
shown as a small sketch-map drawn in the corner of the house-map of the homestead.
838. Definition of a h0USe.~This was a much disputed point; and l think that the dehrlttorfinally adopted caused a great deal of difficulty and confusion, and led to the inclusion of some M rv
rheaningless figures in our Census tables. We find live units in existence in the Panj.ih —
(1) The enclosure, including one or more buildings communicating with the public road h\ .,
common door or gateway :
(2) The building or edifice, a structural unit which is generally easv of identification , ) m j t -. in
be shown on a map :
(3) The quarters, consisting of any such portion of an edifice as ha- a separate mean- 01
access from without. For instance, in towns the ground floor of a building U often .i
shop open to the street ; the first floor occupied by prostitutes with a stflirea^e ot its ow n
the second floor occupied by artisans with another and separate street-door and Mair,.iM
perhaps leading into an entirely different street, and so forth :
(4) The household, consisting of several families nearly related to each other, and occupying * ithc*
common or adjacent rooms or buildings, but not being in commensal it y with ea. h other
(5) The family, consisting of a commensal group, denoted by a separate hearth, and o c n : p \ - r ^
rooms, a room, or a portion of a room.

THE CENSUS OF THE PROVINCE.

Part I.—Preliminary Operations.
With respect to these five units, of which the first three are structural and the two last social, two
points have to be determined :—
{a) Which is the unit to which a separate number can most conveniently be attached for the
purposes of enumeration, and as a guide to the enumerator ;
{&) Which units should be separately recorded for statistical purposes.
It by no means follows that the answer to these two questions is one and the same.
T h e Committee appointed in 1877-78 to consider the arrangements for the late Census recommended
that the enclosures should be numbered, and that within the enclosures, which they called houses, families
should be enumerated separately. T h e Government of India at first decided not to recognize enclosures, " t h e experience of the last Census having shown that the use of the term was either confusing o r
u
impracticable/'
and adopted the house at its unit, defining it sometimes as "indicating the homestead
" w h e r e members of a family live in commensality, and as comprising also the resident servants of such
" family," and sometimes as " the several dwelling-rooms which go to make up the homestead, whether such
" rooms are occupied by one or more joint families." But in its final instructions it abandoned commensality
as a test, and decided that " house " should simply mean " a building to which a separate number has been
"affixed," suggesting at the same time that each "dwelling-place of one or more families with their
" servants, having a separate principal entrance from the public way," should be separately numbered. This
was practically equivalent to adopting the " e n c l o s u r e " as its unit, but retaining the term " h o u s e " by
which to describe i t ; and much confusion resulted.
In the Panjab the staff were told to number as a separate house (translated by dhata, which really
means enclosure) " a n y group of dwelling-houses, whether one only or many, which have a common
" s e p a r a t e principal entrance from the road or street, however many families it may include ;" while the
family (translated by ghar) was defined as " including all the people, whether one only or many, who eat
" together at one common table (chu/ka), together with such of their servants as reside with them and any
" visitors who may be staying with them." At the same time it was pointed out to the superior staff that,
while the outer door leading into the street would ordinarily be the sign of a house, which would include
all the dwellings to which that door might lead, yet there were in many towns large caravanserais, courts,
or collections of quarters entered by a common gateway, which it would be impossible to treat as single
houses. The letter continued as follows 1 :—
" On the one hand the house, as now defined, no longer possesses any gveat statistical importance ; and is, in fact, little more
" than a convenient unit to serve as a basis of enumeration. On the other hand, just because it is that unit, and because tlie
" enumerator will be told that the house is a building separately numbered, it is extremely important that each house so separately
" numbered and recorded in the house-list should be very clearly demarcated. Thus, where the definition iu the rule loaves it open
" to doubt what should be taken to constitute a house, or where the letter of the definition would give an unmanageably large house,
" the following are the chief considerations by which you must be guided.
(1) " T h a t the house should be clearly defined by obvious existing boundaries, so that there may be no possibility
" of mistake as to what it does or does not include;
(2) " T h a t it should not include an unwieldy number of families, as its doing so might lead to great confusion
" o r error in the actual enumeration,"

It will be observed that these instructions drew no distinction between the edifice and the quarters ; in fact I was not then aware how often the same building has several street-doors, leading respectively to its separate floors or quarters.
839. The result was that the house in the Panjab Census had a signification which was by
no means constant.- In the villages of the greater part of the Province, where the enclosure generally coincides with the dwelling-place of a household including not more than two or three families, this
was the unit most often numbered as a separate house.
In parts of the Multin division and in
the hills, where the rule is that each family lives in an isolated building, this latter was what
was numbered. In the towns the building or edifice was in some places adopted as the unit, in others the
quarters, less frequently the enclosure. In all cases, the limits of size beyond which a single enclosure
was broken up and its included buildings treated as separate houses, varied immensely from place
to place. But I regard this result as at once unavoidable and unobjectionable, so far as the unit
to b e adopted for the guidance of the enumerator is concerned. In this connection the only question is
one of practical convenience ; and the variety and complexity of the conditions met with is so
infinite, especially in towns, that it is impossible to lay down any uniform rule which shall work well.
If the staff once understand that the one object is to number as a separate house what will be most
obvious and unmistakeable to the enumerator and most convenient as a unit for his enumeration, they will
have little difficulty in dealing with any conditions they may meet with.
In fact, I believe the great fault in the Panjab instructions to have been that they did not leave
sufficient discretion in the matter. In those huge courts or quarters {katra or kiichabandi)
often
found in large towns, where scores or even hundreds of separate houses are surrounded bv a
common wall and h a v e ' o n l y a single gateway as the means of access, the mere necessity of the case
compelled the supervisor to number each building as a separate house. But the use of the word ahata in
the translated instructions, and the fact that the definition given in the rule made the common entrance
the index of a house, led the staff to feel far too great hesitation in splitting up enclosures into
several houses; and I often found, more especially in towns, courts containing many detached
dwelling-houses numbered as single houses; while, in order to distinguish the several dwelling-houses,
and especially in order to provide for the entry of such of them as might be temporarily vacant, and
which would otherwise, not being " h o u s e s " and including no family, have found no place whatever
in the house-Hst, a series of subordinate numbers had been affixed to the buildings within the socalled house, thus causing unnecessary-complication and possibility of confusion and error. So in
the villages, where the supervision was necessarily less intelligent, I often found that quarters inhabited by

the whole menial population of the village had been treated as single houses, simply because they were
separated from the village homestead by and included within walls with only one gateway.
840. S u g g e s t i o n s for t h e future.—If I had to frame rules again on the point, I should adopt the
''enclosure or h o u s e " as my unit to be numbered, translate it by " ahata, makdn, yd ghar" (taking
chulha as my translation of family 1 ), and explain that what was wanted was, to ensure that the enumerator should easily find the number and should be certain what was covered by it, and that these were the
only points to be considered. This plan would of course leave the staff at liberty to select in each case
that one of the three structural units mentioned in section 838 which might seem most convenient. But
this is exactly what is wanted. T h e o t h e r t w o o f the five units, the household and the family, are obviously unsuitable ; for, depending on social relations and not on physical structure, they cannot have numbers painted on them as such, though they may of course, and often do coincide with one or other of the
structural units. And of the three structural units, the enclosure is the most convenient in the case of a
family homestead, the building in the case of separate houses opening upon a common courtyard or of
isolated houses, and the quarters with its separate street-door in the case of town houses let in flats. At
any rate I see no third alternative but, either to allow the discretion I propose, or to adopt the old
double classification of enclosure and house which is said not to have worked well on former occasions.
I think that something approaching to this double classification might still be usefully employed in the case
of those small courtyards often found in towns, where the separate buildings included in them are let on
hire to tenants who are constantly changing, and where, while it may be convenient to number the court
as a single house, the entry of families in the house-list is but an unsatisfactory guide to the details. Here
I would use subordinate or simmani numbers; and, the court being house No. 154, would call the included
buildings '\* ' £ * ' | * and so forth. But to prescribe a similar complication in all, because it happens to be
the simplest alternative in a few cases, would I think be a mistake.
84J. Occupied and unoccupied houses.—There is another aspect of the house question which gave
rise to more difficulty than I could have supposed possible ; and that is, the question of the degree of human occupation or of the probability of human occupation on the night of the Census, which should qualify a building to rank as a house. The instructions were that " buildings used for human occupation,
whether by day only or by both day and night, and even though they be but huts or sheds," and temporarily
vacant dwelling-houses, should be reckoned as houses ; but not " r u i n e d buildings, cattle pens, stalls, outhouses, godowns, and other buildings not occupied by man." But in many parts of the Province the
buildings occupied by the family and by the cattle respectively are very similar in style ; and if guests
arrive the cattle are turned out of one and the guests take their place, while somebody always sleeps
with the cattle in their shed as a precaution against thieves. Again, small sheds are scattered all over the
fields and at the wells, which serve as a protection against the sun in the daytime, and are almost always
occupied at night by the m e n who watch the crops to guard them against wild animals. Both these classes
of buildings were very commonly entered as houses. Both were certainly used for human occupation,
by night at any rate ; and probably the entry was technically correct under the rules. But as the men
sleeping in them at night would, under section 6 of the instructions to enumerators, be enumerated as if they
were at home with their families and not in these buildings, there was no real need to enter the buildings ;
and it would have been better to have excluded them, not only because to include them in the statistics for
houses was misleading, but also because there, was a danger that the men sleeping in them might be enumerated twice over. Perhaps in a future Census the existence of a hearth (ckiilha) would be the best test
of human habitation in doubtful cases. But exceptional cases will occur for which no rules can provide.
I reproached one Tahsildar for entering a tank as an inhabited house ; but he justified himself successfully by explaining that a faqir was sitting in the middle of it up to his waist in water, who avowed his
intention of remaining there till after the Census.

842. Halting-places and camping grounds entered in the house-lists.—As the house-list formed
the basis of the enumerator's'schedules, the only method of securing that any spot likely to contain human
beings on the night of the Census should be visited by him was to enter and treat it as a house. Almost
every village has a well or grove close to it at which way-farers commonly encamp at night ; while cartmen or men travelling with animals usually encamp in the bare space immediately surrounding the
homestead. Accordingly I directed that all such spots, all encamping-grounds and recognised halting
places for travellers, and such ghats and mooring-places for boats as were not large enough to be treated
as separate blocks, should be added at the end of the house-list and treated in every way as houses. In
one tahsil I found that almost every well in each village had been entered as a separate house, lest somebody should be sleeping at it. This misunderstanding might be guarded against in a future Census.
Thus the figures for houses include a good many buildings to which the term would hardiv apply ;
but they are almost all included among "unoccupied houses," so that even if these figures possessed
greater statistical value than in my opinion they do, the error would not be very important.
843. Entry of families.—The definition of family given in section 83S is so familiar to the native mind,
that no mistake should have arisen in this respect. But the difficulty described in section 839, which was
felt chiefly in towns, sometimes led the Census staff into error. They had enclosures, buildings, and families
to deal with. The house-list showed two units only, houses and families. Following the rules, they entered
the enclosure in the house column. Where the several buildings were permanently occupied by members
of the same household the entry of families was a sufficient guide. But where they were occupied by
very temporary tenants, wholly unconnected with each other, and who were not unlikely to have changed

before the Census night, and especially where some of these buildings were temporarily vacant, the
staff felt that a list of families would be no sufficient guide to the enumerator, and not infrequently
availed themselves of the column for families in which to distinguish the several buildings included within
the enclosure. And the use of the terms ghar for."famiIy " and ghar abad for " resident family " was unfortunate, as they do no doubt apply to the structure as well as to the family which inhabits it. 1 had
wished to use kirhi, which is certainly the word for the Eastern Panjab; but it is doubtful whether it would
b e generally understood, and I think chilha would b e the best word to use in future.
T h e result of the tendency I have just described was, that the entries in columns 7 and 8 of the
house-lists * sometimes referred to buildings rather than to families, dwelling-houses which were vacant
at Census being shown in them with their owner (not occupier) as the head of the family, while a building
which might contain several families had only one entry. So again the chief to whom a rest-house
(hujrah) in a frontier village belonged was often shown as the head of a family residing in it. Again I
found that people sleeping only in the building, as in the cattle sheds and field-huts mentioned in section
841, and shop-keepers who, while owning separate houses where their families lived and at which they
took their meals, slept in their shops for the greater security of their goods ( an apparently very common practice in towns), were often entered as families resident at their sheds or shops, whereas their
real families were separately entered in their homes. This not only meant a double entry of families,
but also danger of a double enumeration of persons. The use of the word chulha for family would
render such error unlikely,
844. -Statistical u n i t s t o b e recorded.—But after it has been decided what units are most convenient for purposes of enumeration, the .question remains whether it is worth while for statistical purposes
to separate any other units in our record; Of course the house, as defined above in section 840, possesses
no statistical value whatever. And I doubt much whether it is worth while collecting statistics for any
structural
unit in the Panjab.
In the villages the family homestead and in the towns the edifice coincides most nearly with the English house. But in both it is impossible to draw a line for practical purposes between the family-enclosure containing only small buildings in which the members of a household
live, and the larger enclosure which includes many houses occupied by independent households. And
the structural complexity which is often found in towns is most extraordinary and perplexing. It is
not uncommon to find one or more houses built inside another, the last having originally had a large
courtyard, but having fallen into the hands of owners too poor to be able to afford the convenience, and
who have allowed others to build within it.
As for the household, the figures would be interesting if they could be easily obtained, as they would give
the same statistics for the collective groups of families as the figures for the family give for the individual
families comprised in those groups. . But the difficulty of obtaining trustworthy figures seems insuperable. There is no outward visible sign by which the unit can be recognised; and it would be hopeless
to expect that the investigation into, social relations which alone could determine its limits should be
profitably carried out at Census. Mr. Elliott verbally proposed to me to attempt something of the sort,
by ruling that the buildings within an enclosure should be numbered as separate houses when they
were occupied by independent households, but that the enclosure should be treated as a single house
when the included buildings were occupied by the families of a single household. But I believe that the
mixed cases, in which some of the buildings would be occupied by allied and others by independent
families, would be, in towns at any rate,' so common as to destroy the value of the figures ; and I am convinced that the only unit that can be usefully prescribed for the purposes of numbering must be a purely
structural one.
T h e family is, of course, a unit of the highest statistical importance. And the cluUha or hearth affords
an easy and universally recognised means of distinguishing it. But I think that the house-list, and not the
schedule, is the paper from which the most valuable statistics of families could be obtained. I had,
indeed, intended to take my figures for families from this paper; but 1 abandoned this intention because,
as already pointed out, the defect in the definition and treatment of " h o u s e " led to the family column in
the Panjab house-lists being not unfrequently filled up incorrectly. But the definition I propose would remove this difficulty ; and nothing would then be easier than to obtain an absolutely correct record of resident families. If a family includes three sons, one of whom is going on a visit and is enumerated in a
hostelry, another is travelling with his cart and encamped by a village on the Census night, while a third
is in his boat at the time, the schedules will, unless special precautions are adopted, show four separate
families. This is not what is wanted. W h a t is wanted is a record of family foci or hearths ; and this the
house-list, and the house-list only, will give us.
845. Demarcation of urban limits.—Few expressions are more indefinite or cappble of more various
interpretations than " the town of so and so." There is the city, often included within walls; the houses
built immediately under and outside those walls ; the surrounding waste lands, which arc seldom wholly
free from buildings ; the outlying suburbs, often very extensive; the small hamlets located for convenience
of cultivation in the revenue-paying area attached to the town, and included within the boundaries of the
mauzah or survey village ; the Civil lines or cantonments, generally at some distance from the town itself;
while not uncommonly.the town is made up of two or more large villages or towns lying close to each
other, but still sufficiently distinct the one from th'ejjther. The papers of the Census'of °S6S do not enable us, except in the rarest instances, to say with certainty what the limits were which included the population returned for each town ; and it is therefore difficult to compare the town population of 186S with that
of 1881. I have attempted.^but not with complete success, to ensure that at the next Census a similar uncertainty shall not be felt with regard to the limits now adopted. In my first instructions (see sections 6
1

Form B, Appendix D.

425

tv

TjflE-CENSUS OF THE PROVINCE.

Part I.—Preliminary Operations.
and 7, General Letter A, Appendix D) I directed that limits were to be fixed for each town, cantonments,
and Civil lines, which should include all suburbs and areas in or upon which people found on the night of
the Census would properly be included in the population of the town. I pointed out that in the case
of municipalities, municipal limits would ordinarily suffice ; while in that of cantonments, cantonment
i mits should b e followed. I directed that where the town had arable land attached to it and consti~
tuted a revenue-paying estate, the area included within the urban limits just described should be treated
throughout as quite separate from the remaining village area, and from any hamlets that the latter might
contain. Subsequently, at Mr. Elliott's desire, I stated that the urban limits of municipalities should
correspond with municipal limits except under very special circumstances.
These instructions were, I am glad to say, not carried o u t ; at least not in the manner intended by me.
I had meant that limits jwere to be fixed for the urban area of the town of Dehli, for instance ; and
that everything within that area was to b e labelled " Town of Delhi " without further specification.
But this would have deprived us of all details of population for suburbs ; and thus, while we should
'have been saved an immensity of trouble in compilation, we should have lost much very valuable
information. It may be, even now, that the limits of certain towns and the population returned for them
may include suburbs for which it would have been well to have had separate figures. But a glance
at the Appendix to Table XIX for British Territory will .show that such a case, if it occurs at all, is at least
the exception.
846. The way in which I would treat the matter on a future occasion is this. T h e external
urban limits in each case would be governed by the principles to be presently laid down in the
section on Tabulation. Within those limits I would fix the boundaries of (1) the town proper, (2) each
suburb or group of suburbs for which it was desirable to have separate figures, separating as a
general rule every suburb that bore a distinct name, and which it was at all possible to separate.
T h e town proper might be taken by wards, if such divisions existed. The limits of each section should
include all encamping grounds, waste land, or other urban spaces which properly attached to it,
so that the area of all the sections taken together should include all that would be included within the external urban limits.
T would then make my Census blocks follow these sections, so that no block should as a rule contain
any part of more than one section. If a block contained two small sections I would have a separate book
of schedules for each, and cases where boundaries crossed would be similarly provided for.
For instance,
part of the Ambala Civil lines lies within and part without municipal limits; and, as I have not
separate figures for the two parts, I have had to treat the population of the whole as part of the population
of the municipality in the present Census. In this particular case the error is absolutely insignificant ; but
it and similar cases would be met by having separate books of schedules for the intra and extramunicipal portions.
The arrangement above 'described would give us separate figures for every
separate group of houses which could possibly be considered as falling within the limits of the town.
T h e question which of these groups should be reckoned to form part of the town could then be dealt
with at leisure. I shall discuss the principles on which I think it should be decided when treating of the
classification of population as-urban and rural in the section on the Tabulation of the figures.
847. S i z e Of Census sub-divisions.—At the Panj£b Census of 1868 each block included 100 families
(then called houses), and each circle 20 blocks in towns and l o to 15 in scattered village areas. The
Committee of 1877-78 recommended that each block should not contain more than 60 families, or 300
souls; and that a circle should not contain more than 10 blocks, supervising agency being still further
strengthened on the night of the Census. I pointed out that the agency available and the distribution of
the population would vary . so greatly that no hard-and-fast rule could be laid down; but that the "limits
" for a block prescribed at the last Census should not ordinarily be exceeded ; while the supervisors should be
" appointed as nearly as possible on the scale recommended by the Committee." I also recommended that not
more than four circles should be placed under a single superintendent. Abstract No. 143 on the opposite page shows the average number of people included in each town and village block respectively, and
the average area of the latter in each district in the Panjab. Column 9 shows the area per block after
deducting the waste lands belonging to Government, which are not indeed wholly uninhabited, but of
which the nomad pastoral population is exceedingly scanty, and required special arrangements for their
Census.
I had hoped that district officers would avail themselves fully of the license I gave them in my circular, and would work up to the full limits of 500 souls per block. But I find that they almost universally speak of 300 souls as " t h e prescribed limit," while the table shows that they did not take me at my
word; and in one district I found small hamlets of only 8 or 10 houses made into separate blocks, and
villages of less than 400 inhabitants divided between two enumerators. This I had altered at once ; and
in fact the figures of- the Abstract, representing as they do the final number of blocks, do not adequately
represent the degree of sub-division which was at first attempted ; for blocks which had at first been
separated were in many cases thrown together before the Census, owing either to my representations or
to the impossibility of finding enumerators for them. Of course in hill districts like Simla, Kdngra, Hazara,
and Kohit, where the population is distributed over smalj hamlets lying at considerable distances from each
other, .and where travelling is exceedingly difficult, it is unavoidable that each block should include only a
small number of people. And generally, distance is a very important factor. But there seems no sufficient
reason why the average population of a village block should have been below 300 in Hushyarpur and Muzaffargarh, and below 350 in Dehli and Gurdaspur; or that of a town block below 300 in Ludhianah,
Hushyarpur, Gurdaspur, Multan and Dera Ismail Khan, and below 350 in Dehli, jalandhar, Sialkot,
Gujranwala Rawalpindi, Giijrat, Shahpur, Montgomery, Muzaffargarh, Derail Gh3zi Khan, Peshawar,
and Hazara.

848. Taking the Province as a whole, 1 think that the blocks were too small ; and more especially <:,
towns, where the average number of souls per block is even smaller than in villages, I attribute tin tact
partly to a common disinclination, which I found to exist in many districts, to include two villages m i.r.c
and the same block. It is of course absolutely necessary that trie block should be of such a size, both as
regards the number of people included within it and the distance between the points to he visited, that a
single man can easily visit and correct the entries for every house in it on the night of the Census. But
so long as this precaution is duly observed, the larger the blocks the better. An excessive number of
blocks increases the difficulty of finding enumerators, compels recourse to uneducated agency writing
strange and illegible characters, renders supervision more difficult and uniformity less easy of attainmi m.
increases the labour »nd expense of compilation, and obliges the Government to call upon a larg. r proportion than necessary of the non-official population to perform a very irksome duty. In many district? tInvery greatest difficulty was experienced in finding enumerators for the outlying tracts; ami in not a l< w
cases men unacquainted with the locality and its inhabitants had to be sent out from the towns, and tin ir
expenses paid from district funds. Yet in those very districts I not unfrcquentlv found that two blocks had
been made where one enumerator would have been amply sufficient. In the towns themselves c]ujii!i< d
agency existed in plenty; but here the houses lie so close together that it would have In . n we'd, by
increasing the size of the blocks, to reduce as much as possible the demand upon private individuals tor
assistance in the operations. I found that, in the City of Lahore, the enumerators in the portions which
I visited had generally finished their work by io° 30' or 1 i c on the night of the Census : and I believe that
where the houses lie in a compact mass, a single enumerator can easily- manage -;oo to 6e>o people, for Inhas not to take th^ir Census ai initio, but merely to check and correct a Census already taken at th. pr.•luninary enumeration. Where the honscs lie far apart, as in the case of scattered hamlets and village-. t V
distance to be travelled, and not the number of houses to be visited, is the most important fa, t

Stogdon of Lahore, Major Plowden of Kohat, and Colonel Parsons of Jahlam would increase the son 01 t! ,
block, Mr. Stogdon remarking that " it is difficult to get enumerators, and it is better to have int. Ibgc i t
enumerators and large blocks than enumerators of no capacity and small blocks.

To furnish so large an agency, every class of the community which had any pretensions to education i.r
intelligence was called upon to contribute. Almost every official in the district was employed in
Census duties, the supervising staff being chiefly drawn from this class; while private individuals formed
the great mass of the enumerating agency.
The superintendents were for the most part Minimis,
Naib Tahsildars, Qamjngos and their Assistants, Deputy Inspectors of Police, superior office staff
Chief Muharars
and head-masters of schools, Assistant Surgeons, Darogluts, members of committees,'
Government pensioners, Honorary Magistrates, merchants, Zaildars,
Javirdurs,
and rural notables
and men of influence. The supervisors were drawn from the classes mentioned above, supplemented
by officials from the district revenue, judicial, and municipal establishments, sergeants of police, district
school and post masters, native doctors, village accountants and headmen, j.igird.ir's factors, intelligent pupils from the higher schools, respectable traders, and land-owners and others of intelligence.
The class of enumerators comprised every clerk or similar official who could be spared and whose
services had not already been utilised in the higher grades ; and to these were added a vast body
of non-official agency, stamp-vendors, petition-writers, candidates for employment, pensioners of
sorts, retired Patwdris,
merchants' clerks, Jagirddrs'
servants, d/iurwais,
senior school-boys and
monitors, Pandits, Maulvis, village Mul/as and Qazis, shop-keepers, village headmen, and land-owners
who could read and write. The services of even illiterate but intelligent zaiiriuJars
were often
utilised by attaching to them school-boys as amanuenses.

THE CENSUS £F THE PROVINCE.

Part I.—Preliminary Operations.
85D. Aid afforded by independent Departments.—At the very commencement of operations the
P a n j i b Government issued a circular to all Heads of D e p a r t m e n t s 1 pointing out that " every available
" officer in every department of Government who can be relied on for efficiently conducting supervision
" will be expected to render assistance ; and Heads of Departments are requested to place the services of
" their staff unreservedly at the disposal of the District Officers for this purpose, so far as may be compatible
" with the conduct of necessary business." The manner in which these orders were carried out varied
much in various Departments and parts of the Province. In those districts where a settlement was in progress, vis., Sirsa, Ludhiina, Jalandhar, Hushydrpur, and Rawalpindi, the settlement establishment afforded
a ready-made supervising agency of the utmost value. The Deputy Commissioners and Settlement Officers
came to an agreement about the division of the work in each case ; and the most practical plan was that b>
which the latter undertook, in communication with the former, the whole of the purely village Census, leaving the remainder to the district staff proper. The Forest Officers rendered most valuable assistance in the difficult mountain tracts in which their duties lie ; and the Customs officials in Gurgaon worked
most zealously for us. The staff of the Western Jamna Canal was of great use in the final Census of ?omc
districts; while the postal, educational, and medical departments as a rule responded cordialh to the call
made upon them. But complaints of want of willingness to help in the work are not altogether absent
T h e Panjab Northern State Railway refused to assist; in Gurdaspur the Canal staff formed a solitary < xception to the willingness of other Departments ; in Gujrat the educational officers were obstructive ; while in
Jahlam little aid could be obtained outside the district staff. In Peshawar, the staff drawn from othti
Departments "did little, and never made any attempt to learn the working of the Census machinery,"
Major Nisbet writes from Simla :—
"One of the weakest points in the general arrangements was, the little assistance that could be obtained from utl.^r D-'j.^rt'' ments for Census work, notwithstanding the orders Government promised to issue on thi.- subject. The usunl (.'X.-ii-.* \y.\-. tin'.
" office hands were generally fully employed in their own special duties. On a future occasion it mit:lit be \\\-\\ lW <i<>v<.inh enT i.
" ascertain beforehand the assistance obtainable from ditfermit offices, and determine the sufficiency of excuse uileivd fur m^l* '--'in.
" to afford such aid."

I think it would have been well had Government issued a second circular early in December, again
insisting upon the importance of help being cordially rendered. And much can be done bv the D<. putv
Commissioner to make matters easy for other Departments. Mr. Benton of Karnal for insiano- u-od
the Canal staff as enumerators only. He required them to attend at their blocks from 26th Drcembt-r
to 2nd of January and again from 15th to 18th February, and he had the supervisors ready on th- spot
with the papers to explain their duties to them. In any case the brunt of the work must tall upon ih<Deputy Commissioner and his subordinates. I called for returns from the districts showing the amount
of assistance rendered by ©ther D e p a r t m e n t s ; but unfortunately the figures are rendered meaningle.-by the fact that most of the Deputy Commissioners have included under that heading the district staff
in the Police, Educational, Medical, Jail, Registration, and similar departments, which, being directK
subordinate to the Deputy Commissioner, should have been counted as District Staff. The Government of India forbade us to employ the rank and file of the police on Census duty ; and though the exception deprived us of the services of a large staff distributed all over the country, yet I think it was
absolutely necessary.

851. Preparation of the preliminary record by village accountants.—It will be noticed that of the
village accountants, nearly half were utilised in supervision. As a fact the great mass of the preliminarv
records in the villages was I believe made by these men, whether as supervisors or as enumerators
At an early stage of the operations I pointed o u t 2 that two classes of agency would be employed fur
the work of filling up the schedules, as it would sometimes be impossible to obtain for the preliminary
record the services of the man who was to conduct the final enumeration on the Census night, and the
staff which prepared the former would have to be supplemented for the latter operation. I "had wished
to put the point more strongly, and to say that in villages this would very generally be the case ; but
the Panjdb Government was of opinion that the objections to having the preliminary record prepared by
one man and the final Census taken by another were so great that, though such a course would doubtless
be unavoidable in some cases, yet it was well to treat it as a quite exceptional and deplorable necessitv.
In the towns it is not only necessary but easy to arrange that the same man shall perform both
operations. But in the villages this is not the case ; and everything I have seen and heard in connection
with this Census has convinced me that it is better to prepare the preliminary record by the best available
agency, and where necessary to this end, to sacrifice the advantage of having the same man make and
finally correct the entries. Of course it is most advisable that, where a reallv competent agent can be
secured for the final enumeration, he shall also fill up his schedules in the first instance, as he thus obtainsan intimate knowledge of his block and its inhabitants. But in the villages it is too often simply impossibh
to obtain really good men in sufficient numbers to take the final Census. The fact that of 40,000 village
enumerators, 11,000 were village banyas, 3,000 village headmen, 3,000 school-boys, and 21,000 nonofficials resident (for the most part, at any rate) in'the villages, speaks for itself. For the preliminary
record on the other hand, which is made more or less at leisure, it is comparativelv easv to obtain a
sufficient and competent staff: in fact the village accountants might almost prepare it unaided. Now the
preliminary record is, in the villages at least, the very backbone of the Census ; in fact it might almost
be said to be the Census itself. I do not believe that the alterations made on the night of "the Census
affected 5 per cent, of the village population. But supposing the proportion to have been double this.
and that in a village of 500 souls, the enumerator cut out 25 as having died or gone away, and entered
25 new men as having been born or come to the place since the preliminary record, I would prefer to
have the 500 entries correctly made in the first instance, and to take the chance of the enumerator making a mess of his 25 new entries—the 25 excisions he could not well go wrong with—rather than have

the original 500 entries made by a bungling old banya in a hand-writing which he could not read himself,
much less anybody else, with all the attendant difficulty of supervision, alteration, recopying, and the
probability of error in the end: He would at any rate have correct entries to guide him in entering the
newcomers- and where they were members of families already entered he could hardly go wrong, even
in recording them. Of course I would insist that where any other than the final enumerator made the
preliminary record, he should take the enumerator over his block before the night of the final Census,
visit every house with him, and read out and explain to him each entry in the schedules. This was done
in the present Census; and, as I have said, from what I have heard from Deputy Commissioners and
others, as well as from the final reports from the districts, I believe that the village schedules wore very
generally filled up in the first instance by the village accountants. In a future Census I would have this
done wherever a thoroughly competent enumerator could not be found.

853. Non-official agency.—It will be seen from the table on page 429 that, including village headmen,
1,850 out of 8,150 superintendents and supervisors, and 45,000 out of 53,000 enumerators were non-official.
Of these last 3,950 were school-boys; excluding whom we employed on Census duty 36,500 out o{ a
literate population of 298,800 in the villages, or one in eight, and 4,700 out of a literate population
of 183,300 in the towns, or one in thirty-nine \
Of the non-official staff only 1 superintendent and 3
supervisors in the T e n j£gir of Kohat, and 37 supervisors and 390 enumerators in the city of Amritsar
were paid. In a few instances one or two rupees were given to defray actual expenses of men sent to a
distance from their homes for the final enumeration ; and in the city of Dehli and the Lambagraon jagir in
K i n g r a the house-lists weFe prepared by paid agency. W i t h these exceptions no payments of any sort
were made on account of Census operations. T h e district officers bear unanimous testimony to the
willing cheerfulness with whieh all classes assisted in the Census ; and in many districts the individuals
selected do not appear to have even attempted to escape from the duty. But that that duty was most irksome
is beyond dispute. Every man employed on the supervising staff had to devote to the work a not inconsiderable portion of his time for from two to three months ; every man employed as aft enumerator had to
learn his work, fill in his preliminary record, go over his block with inspecting and revising officers, and take
the final Census ; all had t o conduct an inquisition which, though made as little distasteful as possible,
exposed them in towns at any rate to the risk of insult and abuse. Practically no payment of any sort
was made for the service rendered ; and though the certificates issued after the work was over were much
valued, yet they were no real equivalent for the duties to the effective performance of which they testified.
I quote below the remarks of some district officers on the subject.
Further testimony to the willing help
afforded by the non-official classes will be found in abundance in the paragraphs on the attitude of the
people in the section on Subsidiary Matters:—
Dehli (Mr. T . W . S m y t h ) . — " I n fact the services of every available male adult who could write legibly and
"was willing tc assist were utilised, according to his fitness, either as a superintendent, supervisor, or enumerator: their
"services were as a rule willingly rendered, and all took an interest in the duties assigned to them. Although every
"effort was made to employ Census officers in the neighbourhood of their head-quarters, yet of necessity many were
"required to work at a distance from their houses and at great personal inconvenience to themselves. The operations,
"including the preliminary preparations, the rough and final enumerations, extended over a period of nearly three month*. The
" work was to some extent onerous, as the inspection of the superior officers was frequent and conducted occasionally at unexpected
" times, and every one employed was required to be constantly at his post."
Tbang (Captain Bartholomew).—"In conclusion as suggestions have been invited for future use, I would express the opinion
" t h a t in no country but India could such a thoroughly efficient Census be performed by a practically unpaid staff. Large
"numbers of private individuals were pressed into the service and a great portion of their time for months was used by Government
" without any remuneration."
Hushyarpur (Mr. Coldstream).—" There was a great deal of gratuitous work done, and done on the whole I believe won" derfully cheerfully and well. The wish of Government was sufficient, there were those found who could undertake the work, and
" I heard I think no repining of any sort as I went about the district.
" Of the total number of agents 1,292 were unpaid. On one occasion in the hills I called the enumerators of a circle together
" and found they were some 30 Brahmins who could write Hindi and who with wonderful cheerfulness were serving Government for
" nothing. The amount sent by Government for remuneration of these men is very small,2, and would give if equally/listributed but
" annas 14 pie I for each. This sum has been supplemented from District and Municipal Funds, and it seemed but justice that some
" recognition of their services should be made, and the sum which after these local contributions it was possible to give them was but
" a very scant remuneration for the great pains they took.
" I t was.undoubtedly a difficulty even in many parts of this district (in which preliminary education is perhaps more forward
" than in- most) to find competent enumerators,
"That the work had to be done practically without remuneration greatly increased the difficulty. The exigencies of the situation
" necessitated the employment of enumerators in a manner which differed in no respect from hegar labour except that the subjects had to
" labour with head and pen, and not with hand and spade. Tliis was a real difficulty and should iu fairness be mentioned here. It hag
"been very partially met by the apportionment by Government of a small cash allotment and by contributions towards payment of the
" enumerators from District and Municipal funds. I think it would have been well if Government had from the commencement dirtct" e d District Funds and Municipal Committees to set apart R5,ooo or Rio,ooo (the sum being regulated according to population and
" area) of the budget allowance for the year to defray the expenses of the Census in each district. This matter bus been alluded to by
" several of the supervising officers. The TahsfldaV and Munsih" of D.asuyah wrote ' the impressed enumerators considered the work
** as ' hegar, but they paid attention when admonished, and came to know that an Act had been passed.' "
Commissioner, Jalandhar (Mr. Tremlett).—"When the next Census occurs it may well be questioned whether larger sums
" ought not to be raised, probably from local sources, for the remuneration of those employed. If the people are being educated to
''regard it as discreditable and wrong when a private individual ' useth his neighbour's service without wages aud givcth him not
*' for his work,' surely the State itself should not set a bad example and be thought to exact ' bcgai'i' labour."

854. I do not for a moment agree with those officers who condemn the policy which demanded
these gratuitous services from the more or less educated classes. I think it is well for the people in general to have it brought home to them that they are liable to be called upon to assist Government on occasion, and that they themselves must take a share in operations which concern the public weal. But
these duties, while of a distasteful nature, carried with them no corresponding privileges or status ; and I
think that every effort should be made to keep within as narrow limits as possible the numbers from whom
they must necessarily be demanded. If the larger limits of blocks which I recommend in section 848, and
the scale of supervising staff laid down in the instructions were followed, we should have :—

which would release nearly half the non-officials employed in the villages and two-thirds of those employed in the towns from Census duty. Of course the real effect would not he quite so "Teat as this as many
of the officials cannot, consistently with their ordinary duties, be employed at any great distance from their
head-quarters. But the figures show that a good deal could be done without, as 1 believe, injury to tin'
work, to reduce the burden thrown upon the educated non-official population.
8S5- With regard to the composition of the non-official staff, there is not much to be said. There
are 34,000 villages in the Panjab, and probably at least 80,000 village headmen; and the fad that |e„ s
than 3,000 of these were utilised for Census purposes shows how few of them must be able to read and
write. The village shop-keepers probably formed as competent an agency as could be obtained in many
cases, as they at least know the village and the people. The figures of Abstract No. 145 show that at
least 7,450 out of the 11,300 village shop-keepers employed as enumerators wrote the Persian c har.u tor •
a result so surprising that I made special inquiries as to the accuracy of the figures. It appears that
the new generation of-shop-keepers very generally learn Persian in the Government schools. Where
this is the case there can be no possible objection to their employment. But where they do not write
Persian I think they should be avoided, even at the cost of sending out school-boys at some iitlle expense
In such cases they are wholly unable to read the instructions issued ; the character they are p i e > , ,|
to employ is often a mere system~of mnemonic symbols only moderately intelligible to the writer • w hile their
mental capacity is not seldom of the meanest. Shop-keepers ignorant ol Persian were most l.ir"< b
employed in Rohtak and K i n g r a , and somewhat less largely in Dchli, Ilissar, and I-Trozpur. In uTl•< r
districts they were not made much use of.
W h e r e school-boys were utilised in the villages, an intelligent headman or other person on the spot
was often made responsible for the Census, the school-boy practically acting merely as his amanueiiM^
But several district officers remark that a sharp school-boy made a better enumerator than almost anybody
else, as he was proud of his duties, active, and intelligent, and yet willing to learn and to obey orders
In many cases the more intelligent youths from the higher schools in the towns were sent out into the
villages and their expenses paid from district funds. They were most largely employed in Ambala where
they constituted one-fourth, and in Liidhiana where they formed one-sixth'of the whole staff of enumerators, and in Dehli, Derah Ismail Khan, Gurgion, Rohtak, Simla, Jalandhar, and Hushyarpur. In almtet
all these districts their use might have been, to some extent at least, avoided by raising the block to a
normal size. As regards the use of non-official agency in general, in K i n g r a only 73 out of 3.098 enumerators arid in Hazara only 43 out of 1,3d were official, while in Rawalpindi'and Jahlam some t'hirteen-fourteenths of the enumerators were non-official. In Simla, Gurdilspur, Si'alkot.GiijnU, Shiihpur, and Muzaffar-'arh
all with the exception of Simla districts where the blocks were unnecessarily small, the proportion of nonofficials is large ; while in Kohdt it sinks to less than half, and in Gurgilon, Karnad, Ludhianah, and Bann 11
it is only about two-thirds.

in Kdngra, where the proportion of Pahari
writers was necessarily exceedingly large,and
where a staff of patwdris who could read the character was available for employment in tabulation, no schedules
were allowed to be sent to the compiling offices in any one of these three characters. Every effort should
be made to avoid enumerators writing any other character than English or Persian. The Deputy Commissioners report very generally that the use of other characters caused the greatest difficulty, the enumerator
being unable to read the instructions and the supervisors to read the schedules, while the last were
generally exceedingly illegible even to the man who wrote them. By far the best plan, where the enumerator wrote an inadmissible character or one not understood by the supervisor, was to write the original
entries in the schedules very far apart, and to fill in an interlinear transliteration. The supervisor then repeated in the Persian text all alterations or additions made by the enumerator in his original, and the former alone was used for compilation ; the latter however being immediately available for comparison in case
of doubt. Where this plan was not followed it was necessary to make a duplicate copy of the book of
schedules in the Persian character, and this greatly increased not only labour but also risk of error.
The Pahari character was used in K^ngra alone ; the Gurmukhi in HushyaVpur, Fi'rozpur, Rawalpindi
and, curiously enough, Peshdwar. The Mahajani was chiefly made use of in Rohtak, Sirsa, the Bhakkar and Leiah tahsils of Derah Ismdil Khdn, and to a less extent in Kingra, Firozpur, and the Mailsi tahsil
of MufUn. Nagri was employed most largely in Hissa>; but generally to a considerable extent throughout
the Dehli and Hiss^r divisions and in Shahpur Kandi of Gurda'spur, and not at all in the western parts of
the Province. Here again characters other than Persian were often used in districts where the average
block was unnecessarily small; but in some cases no doubt, the two phenomena are both due to a scattered and illiterate population inhabiting an outlying tract difficult of access. It is not impossible that
some of the men entered as having used the Nigri character may really have written in Mahajani, as the
latter is sometimes, but incorrectly, called Na'gri. Schedules were printed in Persian, Nigri, and Gurmukhi
(the last at the Ludhiinah Mission Press). Such as were used in Mahajani and Pahari had to be prepared
by hand in the districts in which they were used, as the character varies from district to district.
The highly educated enumerator is a mistake ; and though it is necessary that the supervising staff
should be men of education, yet a great deal of trouble was given and inaccuracy caused by their pride
of learning. An educated Hindu or Musalman has strong theories, derived from the scriptural authorities of his religion, as to how things ought to be ; and is not content to record them as they are, or
to believe that each man himself knows better than any outsider, however greatly his superior in education, what are his caste, clan and religion. Mr. Stogdon of Lahore says :—
"A considerable amount of tact was needed in dealing with such a large number of voluntary enumerators, many of whom
" considered that they were conferring a favour on the authorities by undertaking the work, and looked upon themselves as abler
"men than their supervisors and superintendents. The most difficult kind of enumerator to deal with was the self-opinionated one
" who worked on a method of his own and was ab >ve instruction or correction. In a few instances it was necessary to dispense
"with the services of such men altogether. They were worse than useless."
A flagrant instance of the harm which educated men may do with the best intentions is quoted in
section 37.
857. Progress of the work.—I took up my appointment as Deputy Superintendent of Census on
the 4th August 1880, and the preliminary Government circular issued on the 7th of the same month.
My instructions for the demarcation of Census units and the selection of agency were dated the 31st of
August; while instructions for the preparation of house-lists were issued on the 15th, and for the preliminary record on the 23rd of September. The preliminary record began on the 27th of December 1880,
and was completed during January 1881. The instructions for the final enumeration, which could not be
issued till the Government of India had issued orders for the Railway and Military Census, were dated
the 20th January 1881, and the Census was taken on the night of the 17th of the following month. The
record of progress is continued at the end of Part II of the next Chapter.

As the Panjab schedules were roughly stitched up into books, the name of the district was transferred
from the schedule"to a label on the cover with the following headings : Name of District and Talisil, No. v\
Circle, Letter of Block, Name of Enumerator, No. of pages in the Book, Signature of Supervisor.
I think
that there was no need to repeat the names of the Tahsil and the enumerator on each schedule ; and tlvit
the name of the village should have been shown on the label as well as on each page.
859. The original proposal of the Census Committee was to devote a page to each family (or, as tliev called it. hntwi. Ii,
the Pauj^b, however, the schedules were filled in straight away, a short line being drawn below each family in the column • t
names to facilitate checking, and a space being left at the end of each house in which to enter any newcomer on the ni^ht of t!i,
Census. Under these circumstances I think the decision to write the No. and description of hou^e across tl'e middle of the [u^o
was a mistake. I t led. in some eases to the entry being omitted altogether for all houses save that at the top of each pa-v. ai.e.
in others to space being wasted by a whole opening being devoted to the single house entered in the top keauing. 1 would add .i
column at the beginning of the schedule, headed " No. and descripiion of House."
Column 2, Name,—In column 2, Name, the directions were to write simply the word " female " or '" infant " where there w.iv
any objection to giving the name, or where a child had not yet been named. It WHS found in Peshawar that this caused difficultv
in checking where there were several women so entered. I think it would be better to write " wife," " daughter," " daughtei-in-la\c.'"'
&c, as the case might be, giving the woman's relation to the head of the family. There would, 1 conceive, he no election to p \ ,
this information. ' Mr. T. W. Smyth of Uehli would record the relationship ot each person enumerated to the head of the i'umilv
He writes:—
" I t might he an improvement to have an additional column in the schedule showing the relationship of the several member
" to the head of the family, such as wife, son, daughter, mother, and so on. This would assist the enumerator when record in<j; tin
" names, and would prevent omissions which are likely to occur if the names are not taken down in some recognised order. TI10
" enumerator's first question should be 'Who is the head of this family?" This would be followed by • Is he married, and if so
" w h a t is the name of his wife?' ' H a s he any sous, if so what are their names ?' • lias he any daughters, if so what me tin ii

p Para. 860.
