Part II.—The Enumeration of the People.
_" names P' ' Are there any other relations residing in the family, mother, brother, sister, &c.; if so, what are their names ?' "Are there
" a n y visitors iu the house, if so what.are their names?' and so on. I f the family is a large one the names of some of the members
J" are likely to be omitted if they are simply taken down, without any questions being put by the enumerators similar to those
4
' noted above."
What he says is doubtless t r u e ; but I fancy there might be some difficulty in recording relationship generally, especially where
concubines are kept; it is on general grounds well to keep the number of columns to be filled up as low as possible; and I would
confine the entry of relationship to the caseB where the name was not given.
Column 3, Civil Condition.—In the column for civil condition we had to translate " Married or unmarried " by heard yd
kwari (virgin), and frequent references were made as to how unmarried prostitutes were to be entered. I cannot suggest any better
translation; but I think the point might he noticed in the instructions to enumerators. Captain Barttolomew suggest nd-bidln,
a mongrel word not in use. Mr. Perkins very justly remarks " here strict etymology yields to custom. The use of kwari for an un" married prostitute *s not more absurd than the usage whereby a married girl or 5 years old is not a hodrC"
Column 4, Sex.—The column for sex was split up into two columns headed male and female respectively. The entry was
made by simply writing the word eh (one) in the appropriate column. But I think it would have been better to have a single
column headed " male or female," and make the enumerator write the proper word opposite each entry. The former plan saved a
little trouble in compilation ; but we found that in many cases the word " one " had been written in the wrong column, as was
apparent from the name of the person. There would be much less risk of the wrong word being written. I t would have been well to
state in the instructions to enumerators that eunuchs were to he shown as males. The native does not apply the word mard to them,
aud much doubt was felt in the matter. The FatiaMa State added a third sub-column for them under the bead of sex.
Column S» Age.— By some extraordinary perversity of circumstances, we were instructed to record the number of years completed, by each person on the birthday immediately preceding the Census. In the first place this upset the understanding of
every villager of whom the question was asked ; for every native calls himself 22 years old directly after he has completed his 21st
year. In the second place we were directed, in the case of infants under one year of age, to enter their age in months, though no
detail of age involving months was tabulated. I n the Panja'b we ordered the number of months to be written iu words instead of
numbers, with the word " months " after them ; but it is no doubt possible lhat the number of months was in some cases entered
or read as years. The age on the next birthday should on another occasion be entered in the schedules; and if for instance it is
wished to make the figures in the column of the age tables beaded " 5 to 9 " include those who have finished their 5II1, but not completed their xoth year, all that would be needed would be to tabulate the schedule entries from 6 to 10 instead of from 5 to 9, as
would he done in England, and enter the result. This would enable children under one year of age to be entered as one year old, and
so prevent the possihlity of confusion between months and years.
Columns 6 and 7, Religion and Caste.—These are the columns in filling up which the Panja'b enlarged most widely the
Government of India scheme. The Government of India directed us to record the caste of Hindus and the sect of members of other
religions. But caste, used in any wider sense than the somewhat mythical and practically obsolete fourfold division of Mann, is
mainly a matter, not of religion, but of tribal descent or occupation. Under the instructions received, only 1,907,732 out of the
3,564,519 J a t s and 325,215 out of the 1,436,058 Kajpdts in the Hritish Panja'b, or just over half of the one and not one-quarter of the
others, would have been shown as J a t s or R£jpots, the remainder appearing as Musalma'iis of either theSunni or Sbiah sect, Accordingly we transferred sect to its proper place under religion. In another Census it should, I think, have a separate column or nubcolumn to itself. I t is a great mistake ever to enter two distinct facts in one and the same column, for it leads to difficulty and
error in tabulation, if not in enumeration.
Having thus left column 7 clear for caste or tribal divisions, we divided it into three sub-columns headed respectively " original
" caste or tribe " (asl gaum), " clan " (zdt ydjirqah), and "got or sept " (got yd shakh). The whole question ol the classification
thus adopted, its merits and defects, and the best course to be adopted in future, is discussed in the Chapter on Castes at pages 188 to
190 of the Report. I t was suggested that we might direct the enumerators to record only certain specified tribes and clans, selecting such
as were administratively important. But I believe that such a'course would be fatal to accuracy. I t is true that we may hope to
have before next Census classified tables of elans, from which it would be easy to select in each case those which alone appear of
sufficient importance to be worth recording. But such a list would be far too bulky for practical use. Besides, I am certain it would
be fatal to tell the enumerators to record clans in some instances and not in others. And a native who, if asked what his clan
was, would give a perfectly correct answer, would, if asked to which of a certain number of specified chins he belonged, almost
certainly name one of them, though he might really belong to none. The only practicable plan is that adopted a t the present Census,
namely to record everything, and to select and reject at the compilation and not at the enumeration stage.
Column 10, Occupation.—In the vernacular schedules this column was headed "Occupation of men, and of those boys and
" females whose work contributes to the family income " (Peshah mard bdligh kd, tea un larkon ica lark ion tea auraton kd it Jin
hi mihnat se dmdani hud harti hat.) This was in accordance with the original instructions issued by the Government of India,
but not with the amended form in which .Mr. Elliott framed them (see section 718 supra), and which, though followed in our instructions to enumerators, was received too late to alter the heading of the schedule. I am of opinion that it would be far better to
head the column with the single word " Occupation," leaving all details for the instructions. If it led to any error, it wouIJ only
be the very unimportant one of recording an occupation which was not wanted, and which could he rejected at compilation. There
is one strong objection to the use of the won! peshah for occupation ; namely that, where used with reference to females, it commonly means prostitution. But rozffdr, which seems almost the only alternative, also means income; and the answer to "What is
" y o u r rozgdr?" might be " R 3 0 a month." Perhaps the phrase " Guzdra his tarahxe hota hai?" would have been better, especially as
it was determined, after the schedules h a d been printed, to record source of income where the person had no occupation.
Column ir, Education.—In this column we had only two entries " Under instruction " (likhna parhna slhhta hai) and " Not
44
under instruction but can read and write," ylikh park jdnta hai). Where a person was neither literate nor under instruction the
column was simply left blank; a better plan, I think, than entering him as " N o t under instruction, and not able to read and
44
write," as proposed by the Governmeut of India. The entries in this column are unfortunately few ; and our plan saves much trouble
in tabulation,
- 860. T h e enumerators 1 b o o k s . — T h e schedules w e r e printed across half-sheets of royal Bally paper,
the half-sheets b e i n g doubled across the middle a n d bound up into books measuring \2\ by 10 inches,
the schedule b e i n g \2\ inches across, and the books opening like a b a n y a ' s account-book, with one set
of headings to each opening which ran parallel to the back of the binding. T h e bindings were simply two
separate limp boards made of glazed calico pasted over country paper, and were made up at the Central
Gaol, Lahore, at an inordinately high rate. This was a mistake. T h e y could have been made by contract
'in the districts for half the m o n e y ; a n d the heavy cost of carriage, often by post, would have been saved.
But in any case sample-books should be sent to each Tahsil, as was done in the Census, to show them
how to make up their b o o k s ; as incredible ingenuity is often displayed in misunderstanding written instructions. As for the.form of book, I think it could hardly be improved. T h e loose schedules and covers
w e r e supplied to the supervisors, and they stitched up the books according to the size of their respective
blocks. If it was found that there were not enough pages in the book, or that it contained spare schedules
which were w a n t e d elsewhere, the stitchin-g was easily undone, schedules added or taken out, and the book
stitched up again.
T h e plan adopted was to have one book for each block, the entries for different villages included in
the same block being separated by a blank leaf. I think it would be b e t t e r in another Census to have a
s e p a r a t e book for each of two or more villages in the same block. It would save much trouble in tabulation

437

B

THE CENSUS OF THE PROVINCE.

Part II.—The Enumeration of the People.
and render error less likely to have every village with a book or books of its own. The label outside
•the book, described in section 858, would then show the name of the village.
Each book of schedules contained at the beginning a copy of the instructions to enumerators, a set of
questions which the enumerator was to ask in each case, and three pages of sample schedules filled up by
way of specimen. At the end two or three blank pages were kept in case of need on the Census night.
861. Preliminary practice of enumerators.—The blocks having been finally marked out, the houses
numbered, the house-lists prepared, and the enumerators appointed, the next thing was to teach them their
duties. This was effected by each supervisor collecting his enumerators at his head-quarters about a
week before the date fixed for the preliminary record, and making them take a Census of a portion of the
town or village, each man taking his share of the work. As each man showed that he thoroughly understood the instructions and method of work, he was sent back to his block to await the day for beginning
the preliminary record. In some cases I found that the Tahsildar had caused the enumerators to take a
Census of the whole Tahsil a month or more beforehand by way of practice. This ought to be strictly
forbidden; both because it entails unnecessary labour upon the staff and unnecessary worry to the people,
and because there is a great danger of the enumerators using the papers so prepared for their preliminary
record instead of going over the ground again.
862. T h e preliminary record.—The instructions were that the preliminary record was to be begun on
the 27th December and finished by the 25th January. It was to be finally tested and declared correct and
complete by the 7th February, leaving 10 clear days for preparation before the Census night. I think the
record might have been begun later. The curious effect upon the age-statistics of separating the mass of
the entries from the date of the final Census by a considerable interval, has already been discussed in the
chapter on Age. But besides this, the larger the interval which separates the preliminary record from the
actual Census, the greater will be the number of alterations which have to be made on the latter date.
Thus while I am convinced that for an Indian Census, it is absolutely imperative that the entries should
in the first instance be made and tested at leisure, leaving for the night of the Census nothing more to do
than to bring them up to date,—and this, not only because the type of intelligence enjoyed by the average
enumerator is so low that most minute supervision and examination of the entries is necessary, but also
because it would be impossible to obtain a sufficiently numerous staff to make ihe record ab initio in a
single night,—yet it is on several grounds advisable to reduce the interval between the two operations as much as possible. Now a fairly active enumerator will complete the preliminary record of his
block in three or four days. Supposing that my plan of making the patwaris prepare the record was accepted (section 851, supra), the outside that a patwari could possibly have to do would be ten blocks;
and in that case, he being himself the supervisor, no time need be allowed for examination of the entries.
I think that the best form the instructions could have taken would have been the following: the preliminary record not to be begun in any case before the 7th January, and to be completely tested and corrected
in all cases by the 7th February; subject to this latter condition, the later it is begun the better. This
would leave a certain discretion as to the date of beginning, and would allow sufficient time for practising
the enumerators who had not themselves made the preliminary record. It is true that the preliminary
record in Hissar was begun on 25th December and not finished till 10th February; but I am sure so long
need not have been taken. In Sirsa it was all done within 18 days. Mr. Frizelle of Shahpur writes as
follows on this subj ect:—
"Indeed I may say that the great fault committed in this district was that the preliminary record and the testing of it by
" supervisors was over too soon, so that before the final day came the ardour of the great body of the staff and the interest they took
"in their duties had somewhat cooled down, they had probably forgotten a good many facts in the mean time, and more alterations
" were necessary in the entries in the schedules than would otherwise have been the case."
And Mr. Lawrence of Peshawar says:—
" I would suggest that the preliminary record be completed in 10 or 15 days. In this district during Census operations the
"drought continued. Had rain fallen in January the greater part of the preliminary record of the Hashtnagar, MardtCu, and Utmin
" BohXk Tahsfls would have had to be corrected, for the Gujar population would have left Hashtnagar for Yifsufzai. As it was the
" preliminary record had to be corrected, for in the month allotted to this work there were many changes."

When the enumerator began the preliminary record he went round his block with the house-list in his
hand, and entered all residents for the time being, whether permanent or visitors only (save in hotels,
hostelries, encamping grounds, and the like, where only permanent residents were entered) for each house,
and, within the house, for each family. I think it was a mistake to enter mere temporary visitors. The
preliminary record must necessarily be separated from the final Census by an interval of some weeks ; and
the rule followed caused a good deal of needless excision on the Census night, while it greatly increased
the danger of double enumeration. I would not enter visitors whose stay was not intended to exceed two
or three days. In fact I suspect such persons were very often not entered at all, the rule to the contrary
notwithstanding. Mr. Stogdon of Lahore notes that wedding processions present at the village at the
time of the preliminary record were not entered.
Every house, whether empty or not, was entered in the book, a note being recorded where necessary
that the house was unoccupied or only occupied by day, and a small space being left for possible inhabitants
on the night of the Census. So where the residents were to be allowed to give in their own schedules, the
house was entered and a note made to the effect that a private sehedule had been sanctioned for it. The
large number of shops, and the cattle-sheds and field-huts mentioned in section 841 wasted a good deal of
room; but unless every house had been entered the house-list would have been no check upon the enumerator, and I think the plan adopted was the right one. The method of entry of houses and families is
described in section 859.
While the preliminary record was in progress the supervisors were constantly moving about their
circles, inspecting and testing the entries. When the supervisor had satisfied himself of the correctness of
the entries on any page, he signed it at the top or foot. In some cases the enumerators were so incompetent that their schedules had to be wholly recopied after correction by the supervisor; and this was always

438

TME CENSUS OF THE PROVINCE.

[.Chip: X l '

P&rt IL^The Eriumeration of the People.
.'done with any schedule which was much, cut up by corrections, the stitching being opened and the
schedule replaced by a new one. When the preliminary record was complete and correct for any block,
the supervisor numbered each page above his signature, entered the total number of pages in the label on
the" cover, and signed the label.
- 863. Date of the final Census.—The precise dafe'of the final Census was fixed by the Government
of India with reference to the various conditions of the several provinces of the Empire. They determined
that it should be some time in February ; the necessity for a moonlight night confined the choice within
still narrower limits; and the consideration which dictated the choice of the 17th as the night of the Census
was the advisability of avoiding any days on which large masses
of people would be assembled at fairs, where their enumeration

would have been most difficult. In the Panjib the only fairs of

But it may be noted that, supposing it to be impossible to avoid a date on or about which large fairs
were held, it would probably be better to take the actual date of the fair itself than the day immediately
preceding or following it. The difficulties of enumerating a large number of people collected at a fair would
doubtless be great. But they would not be insuperable, and they would at least be known exactly, and
cpuld be provided ior beforehand, But on the night before or after a large fair there would be a great
number of travellers scattered about the villages and roads in the neighbourhood; it would be quite impossible to foretell where they would be found; and local agency might be insufficient to meet the unexpected
demand upon it, while there would be a danger of such travellers escaping enumeration altogether. Mr.
Hawkins of Amritsar writes.'—"A large local fair was to take place in the village of Khatra in the Ajnila
" Tahsil on the night of the 17th; but it was put off to the 28th by order of the Deputy Commissioner, and
" n o difficulty whatever was experienced in the matter, as timely notice had been sent out to the
" neighbouring'Districts of Lahore, Sialkot, and Gurd&spur, from which the people come to the fair."
This procedure might usefully be adopted in other cases if necessary.
But there is another consideration which apparently escaped notice altogether. Certain days are
lagan, or auspicious dates for the celebration of Hindu weddings. They vary much in the degree of preeminence attached to each. But it is evident that, were a first-class lagan chosen for the night of the
Census, a considerable proportion of the Hindu and Sikh population of the Province might be absent from
their houses at wedding processions; and it would be necessary to cut their names out of the preliminary
record, and add them in the schedules of the place where the wedding was taking place. This consideration is not so important in the Panjib as in the North-West Provinces, first because a much larger proportion of the population is sufficiently Islamized not to attach any importance to lagans, and secondly, because
weddings are forbidden for three only out of the twelve months in the greater part of the Panjdb, instead
of seven as in the North-West Provinces. But it is a point which should be borne in mind on future occasions. So again, Thursday is a usual day for Mahomedan marriages, and some Thursdays especially;
and the day before or after a lagan is almost as objectionable as the lagan itself, as wedding processions
often have to make more than a day's journey. In the Dehli territory a good deal of trouble was caused
by wedding processions on the Census night.
Mr.. Benton of Karnal remarks that in Pdnipat much of the work was finished before the moon rose
(8° 39/ P.M. local time); and that a day on which the moon rose earlier would have suited better.
It may be noted that, in the vernacular instructions, the time for the Census should be described as
" t h e 17th of February, after the lamps are lit," and not as " t h e night of the 17th of February," which
many natives would understand to mean what we call the night of the 16th.
864.. T h e final Census.—During the ten days which intervened between the 7th and the 17th February,
the Census staff were busy in making preparations for the final event. Enumerators who had not themselves made the preliminary record had to be taken over their blocks in detail; supervisors had to satisfy
themselves that the enumerators understood exactly what was required of them; red ink had to be distributed through the supervisors to the enumerators; proclamations had to be issued; and arrangements (to
be described hereafter) had to be made for the Census of nomad population, and of travellers by road and
river. The supervisors had to satisfy themselves, during the 15th and 16th and 17th, that every enumerator was at his post, and had his preliminary record complete and everything in readiness for the final
Census. Immediately before the Census a proclamation was issued requesting the people to go home
early and stay there as far as possible on the night of the 17th ; and directing the head of each family to
remain awake and keep a lamp burning till the enumerator had visited him. Village headmen and watchmen were also warned that they must accompany the enumerator on his rounds.
, On the 17th of February, as soon as the cattle had gone home from the fields and the lamps were lit,
the enumerator went out on his rounds to visit every house in his block. After he had finished them, he
examined the space round the village, and all spots where travellers were likely to be encamped. During
the day he had ascertained whether there were any gipsies or squatters camped in the area of his block ;
and if so he had to visit them also. The cardinal rule for his guidance was, that he was not to alter anv
entry made in the preliminary record save by scratching out the whole of the entries appertaining to
persons who had died or gone away. This was absolutely necessary, as all the entries had been carefully
tested by the supervising staff, and an ignorant and conceited enumerator might have played great havoc
with them. He was also, of course, to add anybody who had been born or come to the place since the
preliminary record was made. It would be interesting to know how many excisions and additions were
made on the night of the Census; but this information has not been collected.
Newcomers were added at the ebd of the house, when they were staying in a house already entered,
without reference to the family to which they belonged. To have left a blank space for their entry at the

439

a

Part IT.—The Enraneration of the People.

end of each family would have wasted much room and served no useful purpose. On this subject Mr,
Wilson of Sirsa writes :—
" I tbink that too maoh importance is given to the ' house,'which is often an accidental conglomeration of families, and too
"little to the 'family' which is bound together by natural ties of great force. For instance if a baby was born after the prelimi"nary CenBUS and before the 17th February, it was then entered at the end of the house, perhaps a long way from the entries
"relatiog^to its father and mother and below entries relating to a totally distinct family. I would clans individuals into families,
*' and for this district at least would not attempt to class them into houses."
I agree with him as to the relative statistical importance of houses and families. But the record of
families is taken from the entries of the preliminary record; and the addition of the baby at the end of
the house did not affect the figures. Moreover, I propose (in section 844) to take these figures in future
from ±he house-lists, and not from the schedules. If however it is desired to take statistics for families
from the schedules, it would be well to direct the enumerator, in cases where whole families had come to
the house since the preliminary record, and especially in hostelries and the like, to separate off such
families from isolated individuals in the new entries- made on the Census night by two lines across the
name column, just as families ace divided off at the preliminary record. This would make the record of
families more accurate; but the primary object of distinguishing families in the schedules is, not to obtain
statistics, but simply to facilitate checking.
All excisions and entries made on the night of the Census were made in red ink. Mr. Elliott feared
that some difficulty would be experienced in supplying this to the enumerators ; but this was not found
to be the case. The common magenta aniline dye (skishah id rang) is cheap, sufficiently permanent for
the purpose, goes a long way, and is procurable in the bazaar of any considerable town. One Deputy
Commissioner estimated for some hundreds of rupees worth, enough to dye all his enumerators scarlet ;
but this was unnecessary. The colour was distributed through Tahsildars to the supervisors and by them
to the enumerators. And the use of red ink on the Census night was exceedingly valuable, as it enabled
the supervisors to distinguish at once the entries of the preliminary record, which he had already tested,
from those of the final Census which he had still to test, and prevented enumerators from playing tricks
with the entries.
865. It was proposed, at a late stage in the operations, to instruct the enumerators to distinguish residents from visitors in the schedules. The late date of the proposal would of itself have prevented its adoption in the Panjab. But in any case I do not think it a good one. The permanent population of any
town or village=its population on any night-(-absentees on that night—visitors on that night. Now,
though it would be easy enough to distinguish the visitors, yet it would be practically impossible to obtain
any record of the number of absentees under any system compatible with a synchronous Census, in which
each person is enumerated wherever he may happen to be on a given night. In large towns and even in
villages, save under special circumstances such as the occurrence of a fair, the visitors and absentees
would be about equal in -number at any given time; and the numbers actually present on the Census night
would probably give a far more accurate idea of the normal population of the town than would those
numbers after the exclusion of visitors. If it were really desired to obtain an enumeration of the permanently resident population of each place, the proper plan would I think be, to aecept my proposal that
temporary visitors should not be entered at the preliminary record (see section 862), and further to direct
that all persons only temporarily absent should be entered as if at home in the preliminary record. These
last would of course be cut out again at the final Census if they had not returned meanwhile; but if the
number of excisions and additions made in red ink on the Census night were tabulated, they would give
by a very simple sum the permanent population as it stood at the time of the preliminary record, births
and deaths occurring between that time and the final Census not being taken into account. The only
danger would be that the names of persons temporarily absent at the preliminary enumeration might be
left standing at the final Census, although they had not returned meanwhile.
On the night of the Census the supervising staff were strengthened wherever possible (chiefly, I
suspect, in the towns) and each enumerator was visited while at work wherever distance would allow of
its being done. Where this was impossible, the supervisors were instructed to direct their attention to the
larger villages with miscellaneous populations, and to those on frequented roads where many travellers
might be expected to stop. Next morning they started off to go round their circles and test on the spot
every excision or entry that had been made on the Census night. They were allowed to cut out or add
any person who appeared from enquiry to have been wrongly left in or omitted; but they were not to
alter the details recorded concerning any person rightly entered, and who was not before them, unless they
could make perfectly sure of what should have been recorded.
This completed the operations, so far as they concerned the enumeration of the people.

Part IIl.--Speeial Measures.

PART III.-SPECIAL MEASURES.
866," Municipalities.—The Census of the Municipalities, which embrace almost all the towDs with
more than 5,000 souls, and many with smaller populations, was taken through the Municipal Committees.
I n "the large Municipalities the arrangements were most thorough and complete ; and in several cases
advantage was taken of the opportunity to prepare permanent maps and house-registers. The cost of
the Census up to the time that the books were sent to the tabulating offices was borne by the municipal
funds in all Municipalities of the first and second classes, and in such of those of the third class as had at
the Municipal Census of 1875 a population of not less than 10,000 souls; forms and instructions, however,
being supplied at Government expense. But even here, where funds were available, it was not found
necessary to employ paid agency save for the preparation of house-lists in the city of Dehli, and for (he
whole operations in the city of Amritsar. Agency was, of course, easy to procure; and the only respect
in which the Census of the large cities was not specially accurate was the entry of religion, caste, and
clan. Here the educated city enumerators knew far too much to record accurately ; and from what I saw
"and heard, I suspect that the poorer classes in cities do often lose in a far greater degree than do those in
the villages, their regard for and memory of caste distinctions. A not inconsiderable proportion of them
often consists of a miscellaneous collection of low-caste men from the North-West Provinces who are
indifferently classed as Purbias by the people of the Panjab ; and they themselves seenj in many cases to
have grown to consider the classification as sufficient.
867. Cantonments.—The Census of cantonments was taken through the Cantonment Magistrate;
and the cost was borne, as in Municipalities, by cantonment funds. The degree of success with which the
work was done varied immensely, and depended wholly upon the character of the Cantonment Magistrate.
Some of the very best work I saw was in cantonments. In other cases the Magistrate was lazy and indifferent, the greatest pressure had to be put upon him to induce him to move at all, and in one cantonment at least the Census would hardly have been taken at all if the district officers had not stepped in at
t h e last moment and done the work themselves. In others again no care was taken in selecting the agency,
no supervision of any sort was exercised, and the staff had to be threatened with criminal proceedings
before anything could be done. The position is a difficult one. The Cantonment Magistrate is subordinate to the district officer only in his judicial and not in his executive capacity, while the Cantonment
"Committee which control his action in the latter capacity is of course wholly independent. But, as the
appointment of the Magistrate rests with the Panjab Government, I think on a future occasion a lazy
and indifferent man might be given clearly to understand that the work had got to be done, and that
he must do it, and do it properly. In Jahlam, Talagang, and Naushahra the whole work, excluding of
course the Gensus of military limits, was done by the district staff with the consent of the cantonment
authorities, who placed the cantonment staff at their disposal. Where this arrangement is agreed to
nothing could be better. Messrs. Beckett and Lawrence of Peshawar both recommend that either the
work should b e made over absolutely to the civil authorities, or that they should be relieved of all responsibility for it. Probably the best way would be to empower the Deputy Commissioner, in case proper
arrangements are not made by the cantonment authorities, to do the work himself by paid agency and
charge the cost to cantonment funds. The population of cantonment bazaars is more mixed than any
other population in the Province, and its Census presents very peculiar difficulties as to the entries in some
of the columns. It is, however, insignificant in extent.
868. Military Census.—It was, of course, absolutely necessary to take the Census of troops through
their own officers. At the same time it was advisable to throw as small a burden as possible upon the
military authorities. It was therefore decided that the Census of only " regimental lines, quarters, barracks,
or other premises in which troops or others subject to military discipline are quartered " should be taken
by the military authorities; " that of regimental and sadr bazaars, and of all parts of cantonments except
" the quarters just mentioned, being taken by the civil authorities acting through the Cantonment Magis" trate." Civil officers were directed to fix, in consultation with the military authorities, the limits within
which each was respectively responsible for the Census. A proposal was made to define the several responsibilities of the two departments by making the military authorities answerable for the Census of certain specified classes of persons, and of them alone. And in the Railway Census I found a similar standard
proposed in one case, whereby to distribute the responsibility for the Census among the several departments into which the railway staff was divided. I am convinced that any such rule must result in confusion and error. Where two persons have to take a Census side by side, the only safe plan is to fix topographical boundaries, and to make each responsible for the enumeration of all persons of whatever description found within his limits. At the same time, to reduce the risk of error, the military authorities were
requested to dear all military limits, between sunset and sunrise of the Census night, of all persons not
temporarily or permanently living within them ; and this, I think, was useful.

S

. The general plan of preliminary operations was somewhat simplified for the military Census.
;s on the subject will be found in Appendix D. Military quarters had been treated as houses in the
general Census of cantonments, and entered as such in the house-lists 1 . This, as Mr. Elliott pointed out,
was a .mistake; and it would have been better to have treated them as blocks. It had not been decided,
When I issued the instructions, within what limits the military Census should be confined ; but in future it
might be explained to the civil authorities that the military Census would be confined within the narrow
limits already described, and that such limits should be treated as blocks for the Census of which the military
authorities would be responsible. No separate index-register was prepared by the Military Department;

Part III.—Speeial Measures.
no maps were needed ; and a simplified form of house-list was used. But of course the schedule .-ad
the instructions for filling it up were the same as those used elsewhere, and a preliminary record was x
made. As soon as the entries of the Census night had been finally examined and corrected, the schedules
were made over to the district office, and the military authorities had no further concern with them.
Troops on the march were censused by their officers, the schedules being sent, in the case of a detachment
to the regimental headquarters, and in the case of a regiment to the station at which they were last cantoned. The schedules of a regiment on its return from service beyond the frontier, which was censused at
Karnal, were included in the population in that place. This was done by order. I should have preferred
to include them in the population of the nearest cantonment.
A European civil officer was appointed in each case to advise the military authorities, and to exercise
such general supervision over their operations as was necessary to secure uniformity in the results. It was
found exceedingly difficult to get the authorities to move in time. It was not that they were indifferent or
neglectful; in fact as far as I can judge (and I have examined all the English schedules page by page) the
Census of military lines was carefully taken ; far more so than that of many cantonments. But the military
Census was such a simple matter that the whole of the work might have been done within the week immediately preceding the Census night. Thus the preliminary arrangements were put off to the last. But
printed forms and instructions had to be provided ; and in some cases the indents for these had not been
received by the beginning of February, and I had to estimate the probable requirements as well as I could,
and send off such a supply as I thought would certainly be sufficient. I at least, if no one else, should have
been saved much anxiety if the' operations had not been so delayed, that not till the day before the Census
did I receive news of the completion of the preliminary record for the Province.
870. T h e Khaibar troops.—At the last moment Mr. Elliott informed me that he had, in consultation
with the Government of India, decided to include the troops and camp-followers then lying in the Khaibar
Pass in the Census of the Indian Empire ; as, though situated beyond British Territory, they formed an
integral part of its population. I accordingly sent them schedules and instructions. When the schedules
were returned to the tabulating office it was found that for the most part only four columns, namely those
showing name, sex, religion, and occupation, had been filled in. The military authorities informed the
Deputy Commissioner that they had authority for thus curtailing the entries ; but, though I reported the
fact, I am unaware what orders had been issued on the subject. In my tables the Khaibar has been
treated as an eleventh division, and its people included in the population of the British Panjab whence
they were for the most part drawn. Where the entries in the schedules were left blank, the numbers are
shown as " unspecified."
871. Public institutions and gangs of workmen.—TheCensus of all public institutions,such as gaofc,
police-lines, dispensaries, hospitals, asylums, and the like, and of gangs of workmen employed on railways,
canals, & c , was taken through the officers in charge. They were treated as single houses in the house-lists '.
Mr. Elliott was of opinion that they would better have been treated as blocks ; but I think that except where
very large, they were best treated as houses of which the occupier filled up his own private schedule. Schools
and other similar institutions, and police, canal, forest, customs, and other outposts were treated in a similar manner. Gaols 2, outposts, and similar buildings were entered as houses in the general house-list of the
blocks in which they were situated, and their resident families recorded therein. Separate house-lists were
prepared for gangs of workmen on large works. In both cases the preliminary record was deferred to
within a few days of the Census on account of the unstable nature of the population.
872. Private schedules.—The Government of India directed that private schedules, to be filled up
by the householder himself and not by the enumerator, were to be given only to " highly-educated people
and members of the European community," as it had been found at previous Censuses that they were
often filled in most correctly. They were also distributed to all schools, outposts, and similar houses mentioned in the preceding paragraph. They had instructions for filling them up printed on the back, and a
specimen will be found in Appendix D. The number of houses to which they were given in each district is
shown in Abstract No. 146 on the opposite page ; but the figures are somewhat misleading, as in some districts the schedules given to police posts and the like have, contrary to my express instructions, been
included in the return.
I found that Deputy Commissioners had not in all cases observed the direction to prepare lists of the
houses for which private schedules were sanctioned at an early stage of the operations. This was a mistake ;
as till the enumerator has an extract from this list for his block, he cannot tell what houses he is to exclude
from his enumeration. The schedules were distributed two or three days before the Census night, the headings
having been first filled up in the district office. On the morning after the Census the enumerator collected
all the private schedules for his block, carefully examining and correcting and completing the entries in
each. He then stitched them into the end of his book, and the supervisor, after testing and signing them,
added the number of new pages on the outside label. It was originally proposed that the householder
should enter in his private schedule all persons residing in his compound. But at a late stage of the proceedings I succeeded in procuring sanction to the rulethat he should enter only the residents of his bungalow, the native servants being enumerated in their outhouses by the ordinary enumerator of the block.
This was, I am convinced, the right rule to work upon. It would be hopeless to expect an officer of a
Queen's regiment or a European tradesman to enter correctly the particulars regarding his native servants.
but of course the distinction should in future be drawn in the first instructions issued to enumerators.
Several Deputy Commissioners bear testimony to the good effect which the rule had upon the correctness
with which the schedules were filled up, and point out that the chief difficulty experienced was where officers
in camp, for instance, had to fill up the schedules for Natives as well as Europeans. In many cases these had
to be returned for correction or completion.

Part III.—Special Measures.
• 87S European Census in general.—I believe it is the experience of all Indian Censuses that the
Census of the European community is much worse done than that of any other portion of the population^*
and certainly the Panjdb Census of 1881 was no exception to the rule. The mere enumeration was no
doubt accurate enough. But it appears hopeless to expect the average European house-holder to read the
instructions for filling up the schedules ; and the proportion of Native to European population in any given
block is so overwhelming that the enumerators and supervisors are necessarily chosen for their knowledge
of the natives.
I examined every entry in every European schedule during the course of tabulation. I found few instances of silly or wilfully incorrect entries. But I found —
(1) that the sect, and the fact of being a European British subject were very generally omitted:
(2) that the nationality was often not rightly entered :
(3) that the name of some obscure English village was often given in the birthplace column :
(4) that the occupation was very generally insufficiently described.
The schedules returned by women were generally far more accurately filled up than those prepared by
men. The ages of women were of course stated incorrectly. One lady returned her age as " over 30."
I have no reason whatever to doubt the correctness of the entry; but it was not so precise as I could have
wished.
" I do not think it would be impossible to have, as suggested by Colonel Jenkins (section 873), all the
European schedules examined immediately after the Census by a properly qualified European officer, and
returned for correction where necessary. The largest number of Christians other than Native Christians
in any single district is 4,018, and the greater part of these are soldiers, whose schedules can be run
through very rapidly.
I would also slightly modify the schedule for Europeans and
6
Eurasians, substituting for columns 6 and 7 the columns shown in
|
i
margin. As the Government of India schedule stood, the nationNationality E u r o p e a n
Religion . Sect
ality and. the fact of being a Eurasian or a European British Subject
BritishSubject.
were to be entered in the birthplace column, which not unnaturally
1
confused such people as read the foot-notes directing the entry.
876. Railway Census.—All Railway premises were from the first excluded from the block-system.
It would I think have been better to have treated and entered the Railway premises in each Tahsil as a
separate circle, for the enumeration of which the Railway authorities were responsible. The Railway Census
was divided into two distinct portions (1) that of the resident Railway population ; (2) that of passengers
by rail on the night of the Census. The rules on the subject will be found in Appendix D.
The Railway premises, for the Census of all persons residing within which the Railway authorities
were responsible, included the' roadway, all stations and signallers' and gatesmen's huts, all large quarters
occupied solely by Railway employes such as-exist at Lahore and Philaur, and all colonies which owe their
existence entirely to the railway, like those at Adamwahah and Sher Shah Bandar. In some cases it was
found moreconvenient to enumerate isolated signallers and gatemen with the village near which their huts
were situated; but in many parts, and especially where the Multah line passes through the bar, the distances were too great for this to be possible, and these men and their families were enumerated by an
official who trollied down the line on the day of the Census ; and they were assumed to be present at
night.
The scheme followed was identical with that in general use, save that no house-lists were prepared.
They were omitted under orders. But I think it was a mistake, as the Railway quarters at Lahore for
instance include a very considerable number of houses; and in such places at any rate they should have
been drawn up. Each station was treated as a block for the enumeration of which the station-master was
. made responsible, and at small stations a house-list was undoubtedly unnecessary. At large stations the
premises were divided off into blocks, the responsibility for which was distributed among the various Departments. The schedules of each block, when completely tested after the Census, were sent to the
Deputy Commissioner of the district in which the block lay.
The passenger Census was conducted on this wise. Between 9 0 P.M. on the 17th and 6° A.M. on the
iSth, each traveller by rail was enumerated at the station at which he alighted. On the morning of the
18th all passengers found in the train, and who did not appear to have been already enumerated, were
counted at the first considerable station at which the train stopped at or about 6° A.M. Private schedules
were used for first and second class passengers, and for Europeans travelling third class. The few passengers who alighted at wayside stations were easily dealt with by the local staff. Where trains arrived
at a terminus during the night, the clerks employed at the terminus during the day were kept on the spot
to take the Census. Where the six o'clock Census had to be taken at a station which was not a terminus,
sufficient clerks were sent there for the purpose. I saw the Census of the 11° mail taken at Lahore. Pairs
of clerks, accompanied by a porter bearing a lamp, were standing on the platform as the train drew up.
Each pair took a third class compartment, one taking one side and one the other, and beginning at opposite ends. As soon as they had finished their carriage its occupants were allowed to get out; and the
clerks moved down the train to the nearest carriage which had no enumerators at it. The door of each
carriage was kept locked till its enumerators were ready to begin work. In this way the Census of the
passengers was taken quickly and well. The passenger schedules were kept entirely separate from those
of the resident railway population, and were treated separately at tabulation.

i

877. The instructions for the Railway Census were not issued by the Government of India till the end
of December 1880, which left us very scant time to make the necessary arrangements; and I did not hear
of the completion of the preliminary record for the whole Panjah railway population till the day of the
Census itself. The arrangements on the Scinde, Panjab and Dehli and Indus Valley Railways were admirable, and the Census of the small part of the Rajputaha State Railway lying within Panjab limits was

£f&en punctually and well. I cannot say from personal knowledge what arrangements were made on the
Panjab Northern State Railway, as the Chief Engineer neglected to report them notwithstanding constant
applications on my part, and was reported to the Government of India for his neglect; but Mr. Gardiner,
the Deputy Commissioner of-Gujranwala, writes as follows:—
"The Railway Census was not.^satisfactorily performed, the necessary particulars having been omitted wholesale in very many
"cases; and I was tpU that the special office which had been created to superintend the Censns was abolished immediately after the
" night of the Census itself, so that no -arrangements were made to ensure that the schedules were promptly submitted nor was any

" The arrangements for the Railway Census should certainly be improved, and if no other more efficient supervision can be
" found than that in force on the present occasion, District Officers should be authorised to control all Railway arrangements. In
" many cases inefficient enumerators were appointed, and travellers detained for hours on arrival at their destination before they were
" enumerated and allowed to leave the Railway premises."
The authorities of the Panjab Northern State Railway seem to have been negligent and obstructive
throughout the Census operations.
878. Boat Census.—At the original demarcation of blocks, every ferry, ghat, and mooring-place
which was not so insignificant and so near a village that the enumerator could easily include it in his rounds,
was marked off as a separate block. Where there was a bridge across the river or a mere ferry without a
mooring place, the Census of both ends was taken by a single enumerator posted at the end where the
bridge or ferry establishment was stationed; and this even where the ends lay in different districts or
provinces, the population concerned being quite insignificant. In Dehli tickets were, at the request of the
North-West Provinces authorities, given to all persons enumerated as they crossed the Jamna; but the
precaution seems to me unnecessary, as the number of travellers must have been inappreciable. But where
there were mooring places on both sides of the river, and no bridge, the enumerators were posted on each
-side. Each such enumerator went to his post at 9° P.M. on the 17th and enumerated all persons spending
the night at the ghat, whether in boats or encamped. Where there was a bridge or a ferry, he stayed at
his post'all night and enumerated all travellers who did not appear from their statements to have been
already counted at their homes. In any case he remained at the ghat during the whole of the next day,
and enumerated all persons arriving in boats (but not any travellers by land who might cross over the ferry)
"unless they had a boat ticket or appeared to have been counted at their homes on the previous night.
T o each boat which he enumerated he gave a boat-ticket on red paper signed by him, and specifying the
No. of the boat, the name of its master, the number of people enumerated, and the date and place of enumeration.
879. River turnpikes for boat Census.—The object of keeping the enumerator at his post on the
18th was to pick up any boats that might have escaped enumeration during the night by being moored or
_ stranded at a distance from uny.ghdt.
This precaution was sufficient for the local traffic. But on the
lower course of the Indus, Chanab, and Satluj, a very considerable through traffic is carried on, the boats
engaged in which carry a store of provisions and often do not put in at any mooring-place for several days.
In fact on the Bahawalpur and Derah Ghazi Khan portion of the Indus there is often no such place for
many miles together. This traffic is smaller in the cold weather than at other times ; but still sufficient to
"necessitate special arrangements being made, otherwise the boats engaged in it might have altogether
escaped enumeration by being but in the main stream on the night of the 17th and during the whole of the
18th. The boat bridges at Kalabagh on the Indus, at Firozpuron the Satluj, and near Jhang at the
junction of the Chanab and Jahlam, fixed the limits beyond which such traffic was impossible. Below these
points the following stations were selected to serve as river turnpikes :—
On the Indus.—Mithankot,
Koreshi, a n d Derah Ismail Khan :
On the Chanab.—Sher Shah:
On the .?«//«/.—Bin drewala and Bhila Ghat:
each station being arranged for by the Deputy Commissioner who managed the ferry at the spot. The
Deputy "Commissioner of Muzaffargarh proposed Khangarh Doma, below the junction of the five rivers, the
Deputy Commissioner of Multan proposed Bandar ghat on the Chanab, and the Deputy Commissioner of
Montgomery-proposed. Chichawatni on the Ravi as additional stations ; but I informed them that, while there
was BO objection to their extending the special arrangements to these places if they thought it advisable, it
did not seem necessary to do so ; and as a fact Bandar ghat was the only one of them at which a turnpike
was established. At the stations thus selected, enumerators were posted and instructed to question all
boats which passed their station up to the evening of the 28th February, and to enumerate all persons in
them who did not appear to have been already enumerated elsewhere.
880. Travellers.—All travellers moving along main roads during the night of the 17th were enumerated at fixed points on those roads, turnpikes and police posts being utilised for the purpose. In this
instance special permission was given to utilise the rank and file of the police for purposes of enumeration. People merely coming home from the fields along the road were of course left to be enumerated at
their houses. The enumerators were especially instructed to visit all likely resting-places for travellers
within their blocks after going the round of their houses, and to revisit all hotels, rest-houses, hostelries,
encamping grounds and the like early on the morning of the 18th, to find out whether any unenumerated
traveller had come in after they had gone their rounds. In the case of large city hostelries and encamping'grounds it was ascertained early on the Census night whether an unusual number of travellers had
arrived; and ah additional enumerator was put on where necessary.
881. The numbers of people travelling by road in a February night is ordinarily so small that it was
not thought worth while to organise a system of check-tickets for travellers ; but the word of each man
was to be taken, where he was not obviously answering falsely, as to whether he had already been enumerated elsewhere or not. The Deputy Commissioner of Jahlam, indeed, reports that above 3,000 travellers were enumerated on the Grand Trunk Road in the Jahlam Tahsil alone; but it seems almost impossible that these figures should be accurate, as they would give one traveller to about every 20 yards of

Part III.—Special Measures.
road. In the Dehli district check-tickets were used ; but I doubt the necessity, though I do not knc--..
that there is any objection to the system beyond the additional complication it introduces. In the city of
Amfitsar every person coming out of the city after dark had to satisfy clerks stationed at the gates that
he had been already enumerated ; while persons entering the city were enumerated in the gate-schedule
and given a ticket to prevent their being counted again. I think this last was a mistake, as persons absent
from their houses for a few hours only were to be enumerated as if present there, and the plan adopted
probably led to a good deal of double enumeration. In Simla the Census of people travelling by tonga
or bullock train was taken before they started, and a special mark placed on the vehicle ; and this was
again checked at an intermediate point in the journey within the district. In Peshawar one jamadt'ir
travelled up the road from Attak and another down from Peshawar, taking the Census of the roadside
posts, and at the same time enumerating travellers by dak.
882. Nomad population.—The great grazing grounds of the Western Plains, known as the bar tracts,
are inhabited by nomad pastoral tribes who settle on a spot till the grazing within reach is exhausted,
and then move their encampments to new grounds. These camp-villages (//wis or ralinas) were allotted
to enumerators, generally drawn from the establishment in charge of the grazing preserves, who made the
preliminary record. A day or two before the Census the exact position of the camps was ascertained
from the headmen or forest officials (who usually acted as supervisors), and the same enumerators, mounted on horses or camels, took the Census of their blocks on the night of the 17th without difficulty. The
Deputy Commissioner of Montgomery proposed that mounted enumerators should sweep the bar during
the daytime of the 17th, but this seemed unnecessary. The exact position of the camps can easily be ascertained, and in February the whole population is to be found within them at night. The similar camps
of the Pawinda/is in Derah Ismail Khan called Kim's appear to be fixed and not moveable, and to haveneeded no special measures.
The enumerators were instructed to make particular inquiries from the village headmen as to the
existence and locality of any temporary squatters, such as gipsies, hunters, travelling gangs of artisans,
and the like within the limits of their block. Such encampments are always known to the villagers, as the
squatters come into the village to buy food and make money.
883. T h e Census of Teri and Tanawal.—The Census of feudal Tanawal in Hazara, held bv the Naw ab
of Amb and the Khan of Phiilra, and of the Teri jagir of the Khattak Nawab of Teri in Kohit, presented
some difficulties. In both cases the Chiefs were semi-independent, exercising all functions but those of
police and criminal justice within their territories ; in neither had any village survey been made, while
men able to read and write were few and far between. In Teri the Nawab was ready enough to do all
that was possible, and only needed assistance, no revenue staff existing in his country. Accordingly a
supervising agency at the cost of Rs. 900 was sanctioned, the cost of which in view of the unfavourable
seasons which had preceded the Census it was thought advisable to throw upon Government. The actual
expenditure was only Rs. 561, and was reallya political rather thin a Census charge.
In Tanawal there was more difficulty. The Nawab had raised objections in 1868, stating that Census
operations would involve him in difficulties with his border villages. His objections had, however, been
overruled. On this occasion he confined himself to inactivity, which was almost as formidable a difficulty
as active resistance, as much of his Territory had never been visited by Europeans. The matter was
neglected more than it should have been in the first instance. But eventually pressure was brought to
bear by the Commissioner, and the Deputy Commissioner summoned the Nawab's Tahsildar for instructions
to Abbotabad and sent his own Superintendent to supervise and inspect the work, while he himself marched
through the territory. The Deputy Commissioner had the preliminary record sent him for examination
before the final Census, and he reports that the work was in the end fairly well done.
884. Montane and sub-montane tracts.—I have already noted that the rule by which the division
into blocks was made one of area, the collective blocks comprising the whole area of the district, was laid
aside in the more mountainous portions of the Province. It would have been absurd to divide the snowclad
and inaccessible sides of uninhabited mountains into blocks; but all the villages, hamlets, and outlying farms
or huts were carefully allotted among the several supervisors and enumerators, so that there might be no
doubt as to who was responsible for the Census of each. It was here that, as noted by Mr. Alex. Anderson,
the Assistant Commissioner in charge of Kulu, the block maps showing the position of homesteads and
homes were specially valuable ; indeed without them it would have been almost impossible to exercise
any effective check, or even to ensure that the enumerator knew what he had to enumerate. I would insist
upon these maps being prepared in hill-tracts at least, even if nowhere else.
The scattered nature of the hamlets made it in some cases impossible to confine the blocks within the
limits of village boundaries. If, for instance, three outlying hamlets belonging to three distinct villages
layclose together and at a distance from the parent villages, it was necessary to give them to one and
the same enumerator. But in this case the books of schedules for the various villages or parts of villages
were kept distinct, so that no confusion resulted. This plan was only had recourse to in a very few villages
of Kiilu, Murree, and Shahpur Kandi.
885. A still more serious difficulty was the snow, which in many parts of the Pan j.lb lies thick on tinground in the middle of February; even where the snow was not aii obstacle, the scattered nature of the
habitations in the hills, coupled with the special difficulty of obtaining competent agency in these backward tracts, made the distances which each enumerator had to travel necessarily great ; while the spots
which he had to visit were often difficult of access even in the daytime. Owing to these causes it was
decided that in certain mountainous tracts it was impossible to take a Census at nisrht, and the final
enumeration was conducted by daylight on the 17th, and, when that did not suffice, continued to the
18th. The tracts to which this was extended were, Kagan in Hazara, including the vallev of the Kunhar
itself, Bhogarmang, Upper Konsh, and the snow-clad parts of Agror ; the Tanawal hills, also in Hazara .
the whole Tahsil of Murree in Rawalpindi and 50 villages of the Kahiita Tahsil in the same district,

jvttrtffTng all north of the river Ling and some villages in the east of the Tahsil in the mountainous
country adjoining the river Jahlam ; the Shahpur KandL tract in the Gurdaspur district; some few villages
in Kulu; that part of Seoraj included in the Mandi S t a t e ; and nearly the whole of the Chamba State.
In these cases the question asked by the enumerator was, on the 17th, " Who will sleep at your house
" t o - n i g h t ? " and on the 18th" Who slept at your house last n i g h t ? " In these parts, and at that time of year,
the population is very stationary; and the departure from an absolutely synchronous Census which circumstances compelled us to make was one in name rather than as regards the facts recorded. In Chamba
some few remote farms on different mountains were not visited at all by the enumerator, the heads of
the families being summoned and made to give an account of their households.
In K6gan indeed, in Kothis Swar and Kohr in Chhota or Bir Bangahal of Killu, in Mandi-Seoraj, and
in Pangi of Chamba, it had been feared that the country would be wholly inaccessible during February ;
and orders had been issued to take the Census at the latest safe date before the passes were closed by the
snow, and after visitors who did not intend to winter there might be presumed to have left, and to revise
the schedules immediately they opened again in the spring, and before people could have had time to enter; recording in the first instance only those who declared their intention of wintering in the villages, and
adding in the second instance only those whom local inquiry showed to have been present in them on the
17th February. But the season was exceptionally mild, and the Census was taken as in the other hill.tracts specified above, the only exception being that in Kagdn and Tanawal the houses were not numbered.
The Deputy Commissioner reports that there was no time for this operation—why I know not. There was
as much time here as in similar tracts in other districts; though no doubt the difficulties of procuring
"agency, and in other respects also, were very great—perhaps greater than anywhere else in the Panjab.
In Kulu the Census was particularly well taken under the superintendence of Mr. Alex. Anderson.
He found it impossible to prepare maps of circles and charges, as indeed no village survey was in existence.
But, as he re'marks, the village boundaries being strictly adhered to, it was easier to describe the boundaries
of large areas in this mountainous country in writing than on a map. The block-maps however showing
the position of homesteads and houses were prepared, and proved most invaluable. One village, Mulana,
was isolated by snow before the Census ; so a good enumerator was sent to it before the pass closed, and remained there till it opened again. The Census of the great mass of the tract was taken at night on the
17th without difficulty, as was that of the Simla Hill States, including even the wild villages of Bashahr;
and this fact makes me doubt whether the license to take the enumeration by day was really necessary in
all the tracts in which it was granted. However, as already remarked, the tracts were precisely those
in which the point was most immaterial owing to the fixed nature of their population in February.
886. Census of Lahul and Spiti.—The Tibetan valleys of Lahul and Spiti, lying north of the great
chain of the Mid-Himalayas, and approached by passes the lowest of which is more than 13,000 feet high,
are wholly inaccessible during the winter months ; the valleys themselves contained no sufficient number of
educated men to take the Census; while, even if our establishment could have been induced to winter
there, travelling would have been impossible. The Census was therefore, as in 1868, postponed till the
passes opened in the spring. As soon as access was possible a portion of the Kulu establishment was
moved into the valleys, and the Census was taken in the ordinary manner, only those being enumerated
whom local inquiry showed to have been in the valley on the 17th of February, and the work being pushed
on vigorously so as to anticipate as far as possible the influx of summer visitors. The Lahul enumeration
was finished by the 9th of June, having been supervised by seven men from Kulu, but all the 34 enumerators
having been Lahulis; on the 16th of the same month the supervisors and 14 of the enumerators moved
on into Spiti, where no local agency whatever was available ; and they completed their work by the 4th
July. The season appears to have been an unfavourable one. One great difficulty was the prevalence of
the Tibetan language, many of the schedules being of necessity written in that language and character, and
having to be translated afterwards.
887. Other special cases.—Political considerations required that the Census of the village of Garhi
Mian Zaid Gul in the Peshawar district should be taken by two different agencies, the Census of the
Mohmand population of the village being taken by the Deputy Commissioner of the district, while the
Kohat authorities enumerated the Kalla Khel Afridis. The schedules of the latter were however transferred to the Peshawar office and incorporated with the other schedules of the village. The Census of the
Biloch tribes of Derah Ghazi Khan was taken through their tumandars; while the trans-border Waziri
nomads who were present in Kohat at the time of the Census were enumerated, more or less roughly
T-Fancy, through their headmen. More elaborate arrangements would, it was feared, have alarmed them.
As it was the Durkani section of the Gurchani Biloches in Derah Ghazi Khan threatened to leave the district and return to their homes before the Census night, though they eventually thought better of it. A
small . outlying hamlet called Dhok Pathan lies within the confines of the Rawalpindi district, while
the main village of the same name, with the greater portion of its lands, is situated in the Talagang Tahsil
of the Jahlam.district. Both in 1868 and now, the outlying hamlet was included in the Census of the main
village. Strict adherence to territorial limits would have caused inconvenience; and the population in
question was inappreciably small. On the Pathan frontier it was found necessary to obtain from the
Commissioner a ruling as to whether certain villages were or were not to be considered as within British
Territory for the purposes of the Census, as " t h e border round this district (Peshawar) has never been
. "defined." I have been unable to obtain particulars of the line eventually adopted; but any possible
variation at future Censuses would be too small to materially affect the comparison with the present results.

PART I.-ABSTRACTING THE FIGURES.
888. Village Abstracts.—The first step after the complete testing and correction of the schedule;
was the preparation of the preliminary abstracts. These were generally prepared for villages by tin
patwaris, and in towns by the enumerators. Railway schedules were dealt with by the district stall,
and military blocks by the cantonment office. A separate abstract was prepared for each block, and
for each village included in the block. The utility of these abstracts is discussed in section 900.
Their form is given in general letter E. of Appendix D. It consists essentially of three parts. In tinfirst are shown the number of families and of occupied and unoccupied houses, of males, females, married.
unmarried, and widowed, of persons of each sex whose occupation is recorded, of persons who can read and
write, persons under instruction, and persons suffering from infirmities. The numbers of houses and families
thus arrived at furnished the statistics under these heads, as the figures were not again abstracted. The
remaining numbers furnished a check upon the abstracting carried on in the divisional offices, the tot.iU
of which should correspond with the figures of the preliminary abstract. The figures for males and
females also enabled us to publish the preliminary results of the Census far sooner than could have been
done in any other way. The second part of the abstract showed no figures, but gave the names of tin
religions, sects, languages, birthplaces, and occupations entered in the schedules. These names were useil
to fill into the registers used by the abstracting staff of the divisional offices, so that they should find headings ready written for them when they came to abstract the figures into the registers.
The third part consisted of a classified abstract of all tribes and castes and their sub-divisions entered
in the schedules, together w-ith the number of persons in each. This was the most important part of the
abstract. The other two parts could have been dispensed with, as all the figures with the exception ol those
for houses and families were got out again by an independent operation. I Jut we accepted the- figures ot
the third part as they stood, and thus saved an immense deal of labour. These figures, too, were prepare d
by men who, in the villages at least, knew every person entered in the schedules, and were iutimalelv
acquainted with the clans they belonged to. The importance of this has already been pointed out. In niv
instructions I left it to the supervisor to arrange whether lie- or the enumerator should prepare the
preliminary abstracts, making him however sign each in attestation of its accuracy, and pointing out to
district officers that in the villages the patwaris would be the proper agency. This »j» in ae c ordanee with
the general tenour of the Pan jab scheme (see section 85 1). I believe that, like the [ire I i mi nary record, these
abstracts were as a rule prepared by the patwaris; but I think that on another occasion this should I..made an invariable rule in the case of villages, each man doing his own circle; partly be-causc the- patw.in
could be held responsible for inaccuracies detected later cm, which is impossible in the- ease- of the nonofficial enumerator; but especially because intimate knowledge of the people enumerated is so important in
the preparation of at least the third part of the abstract.
889. Transliteration and Collection of schedules.—All schedulers and abstracts that were written in
any other character than English, Persian or legible Nagri were, save in the case of K nigra, whereas already noted an exception was made, transliterated into the second of those- characters unless
an interlinear or other transliteration already existed, in which case it was carefully compared and checked.
The enumerator himself in all cases read out his schedules to the transliterator. The schedules and their
copies, if any, were then tied together ; and as soon as the schedules and abstracts for his c in le were complete the supervisor brought them, together with the house-lists, maps, and other papers, to (he tahsil,
where they were compared with the index-register to see that they represented all the bloc ks and villager
of the circle, and were examined to ensure that the series of pages were complete-, that all the- privateschedules were stitched in, and that the total number of pages corresponded with the entry em (fie labe-i
This work was intended to be completed by the end of February, and I believe that, except in the ease ot
towns where there were difficulties, it was generally finished by then.
890. Tahsil abstracts.—Meanwhile the Tahsildar had (or should have) prepared a tahsil abstract in
the form given in General Letter E. of Appendix D. This consisted of a list of all the bloc ks in tin tahsil,
but arranged not by circles but by villages, the villages again being arranged in the- fixed order of tinrevenue office according to which all village records are kept and village statements pre panel. The names
of the villages were entered, and opposite each the letters of the block in which it was included or Unblocks into which it was divided, with their circle numbers, were filled in from the- index-register. After
the detail of villages followed similar details for all towns which, not being estates annexed to lan.lrevenue, had no revenue number, all cantonments and civil lines, and all Railway stations. The- forme-c
population of each village and town was entered in a separate column from the Census papers of iXos.
As the schedules and abstracts for each circle were received in the tahsil, the present population for eae 1,
block was entered up from them and totals given for each town and village.
In Karnal all the schedules were examined as they were received " b y the Tahsild.ir and a few of bis

wa$ TTs"e^cce'llent plan and ought to be generally adopted on a future occasion; it being distinctly laid down
