891. Despatch of schedules to the Divisional office.—As soon as the figures for a circle had been
entered up in the tahsil abstract, the village abstracts and house-lists were put inside each book and the
books were tied up together with the circle copy of the index-register. They were then packed up by
complete circles and sent off to the divisional office, which all schedules should have reached by the 15th
of March. The tahsil abstract also was totalled and sent to the divisional office, together with the
index register of the tahsil.
This work, or at least so much of it as was done in the tahsils, was worse done than any other portion
of the Census work. The figures of the preliminary abstracts were fairly correct; quite sufficiently so for
practical purposes. In only eight districts was the difference between the preliminary and final results as
large as one-half per cent., and even this error was more often due to mistakes in the tahsil than to errors in
the abstracts. But the tahsil part of the work was often carelessly and sometimes exceedingly badly done.
The lists of blocks and villages were incomplete, the entries were wrongly made and totalled, the books
of schedules were sent off anyhow, and paper after paper had to be returned for correction and completion.
This carelessness did not affect the ultimate results ; but it immensely increased the labour of arriving at
them ; and I allude to it only in order that it may be guarded against in future. I do not think it was
unnatural. I have in a former chapter remarked that the Census operations were, to speak plainly, an
unmitigated nuisance to everybody concerned. They threw a very great deal of additional labour of a
monotonous and uninteresting nature on an already overworked staff, without any hope of additional
remuneration ; they took men away from their Ordinary work, which naturally fell into arrears, to be cleared
off afterwards ; and they disturbed the whole working of district routine. But they were exceptional
in their nature, Government attached great importance to them, and the whole Panjab staff laid aside all
other duties that could be temporarily neglected with safety, threw their whole heart into the Census
work, and willingly and zealously strove to ensure a successful result. I do not think that the manner in
which the work was done can be praised too highly. But, the Census once taken, the tension suddenly
relaxed ; the Tahsildars thanked Heaven it was over, and turned their attention to working off the arrears
which had accumulated. They knew that they would be in no way responsible for the tabulation of the
results ; and it is hardly to be wondered at that they neglected subsequent operations of a purely routine
nature, the only object of which was to save trouble to another Department.
The allowing the Tahsildars to send up the schedules and returns direct was a great mistake. It was
done to save time ; for Mr. Elliott allowed no margin in his estimate of the time necessary for the completion of the work. But in the end it would have saved time to have had all the papers sent through and
checked in the district office; and I strongly recommend that this course be adopted in future.
892. Machinery for abstracting and tabulating the figures.—At the Census of 1868 the figures for
each district were tabulated by patwaris in the district itself, and a single clerk compiled the tables for the
Province from the returns sent up in English by the Deputy Commissioners. Sir Robert Egerton had
intended to follow the same general plan, so far at least as tabulation was concerned, on the present occasion. But Mr. Elliott was strongly opposed to it, as he was of opinion that it would be almost impossible
to secure uniformity of method, that a really effective supervision could only be ensured by the deputation
of an Extra Assistant Commissioner for each district, which would be exceedingly expensive, that there
would be great danger of the work being badly and slowly done, and that a single' dilatory Deputy Commissioner might delay almost indefinitely the results for the whole Province. He was of opinion that the
work should be done at a central office at Lahore under my immediate supervision. This, I objected, would
be next to impossible. We could not bring local officials, such as patwaris, so far from their homes ; and,
without local knowledge, no central staff could either read or understand the terms for castes, clans, and
occupations used, for instance, in Dehli and Peshawar respectively. The employment would be verv temporary ; it would be difficult to collect a sufficient staff ; we should have but little hold over the men when
collected; and much time would be lost in taking on incompetent men, trying and rejecting them, and
finding better to take their place. Even the mere matter of office accommodation and floor space would be
a difficulty. Above all, the constant references to districts which would be necessary for the explanation
of words and discrepancies would cause the greatest trouble, confusion, and delay. I verbally con.sulted
two-thirds of the district officers of the Province, when collected at the Imperial dnrbih- of 1SS1 ; and
though it was to their interest to have the labour shifted off their shoulders to those of another, all but one
were of opinion that the work would be done better, more quickly, and at less trouble to all parties in the
districts than at a Central office.
Eventually a sort of compromise between the two schemes was agreed on, and accepted by the Panjab
Government. A separate office for tabulation was constituted for each division, at which the figures of the
whole of that division were dealt with. Each office was put under the charge of a picked Extra Assistant
Commissioner, Superintendent of Settlement, or Tahsildar, the qualities taken into consideration in selecting them being familiarity with figures, experience in the management of large establishments, and drivingpower. The offices were manned by patwaris and qinungos selected rateablv from the staff of each
district in the division, a smaller number however being drawn from districts under settlement, or havino
large areas under fluctuating assessment or subject to river action. The whole staff thus selected numbered 92 out of the 389 qanungos and 935 out of the 7,668 patwaris of the Panjab. A Sadr qam'mgo was
also selected in each division as assistant to the divisional officer. The divisional officers were seconded
in the lowest grade of their respective ranks ; while arrangements were made, where necessary, to provide
substitutes for patwaris and qanungos at as low a rate as possible. District officers were directed to select
the very pick of the revenue staff for this work ; and most of them honestly complied with the demand,
though here and there the election was left to Tahailddr ji, who shot his rubbish into my hands. The

" "^
893. I am inclined to think that, on the whole, the plan adopted was the best. The single central
office is, I am convinced, quite out of the question for the Panjab. The only staff that could hope to deal
successfully with the infinite variety of local terms is a local staff such as we employed. Take a single
instance—that of occupation. Faqir means a tenant in Peshawar, a beggar elsewhere.
Daftari
means an office menial save in Peshawar, where it is a landed proprietor, /'(///and Banjan't. mean respectively a herdsman and a cattle merchant in the east, an oil-prcsser and a pedlar in the west, and so 011
ad infinitum ; while the local terms which are absolutely unintelligible to any outsider are simply innumerable. So, too, we found local knowledge invaluable in the grouping of blocks and hamlets under the proper
villages and towns, and of villages in their proper places ; though if the tahsil abstracts had been properly
prepared this should have presented no difficulty. But the necessity for local knowledge is felt at every
stage of this portion of the work.
The choice then lies between district and divisional tabulation. I do not attach much weight to
the objection that uniformity would be impossible if each district tabulated its own figures. So Ion;* as
the operations are confined to tabulating, not classifying, the various entries in the schedules, the process is
purely mechanical, and uniformity is only convenient because it is well that the easiest and best method
should be followed everywhere. A scheme would be drawn out beforehand ; and a good Tahsildar would
do the work more quickly, better, with a smaller expenditure of power, and with infinitely less hindrance to
current work than would be possible were it done in a divisional office. But there are bad Tahsildars, and
those not a few. It would be difficult for the district staff, already burdened with arrears of work accumulated during Census operations, to exercise the continual supervision which alone would be effective ; it
would be impossible to put a superior officer on special duty for each district; and though in a large
portion of the Province the work would be admirably done, yet bad work would be an absolute certaint)
from some tahsils.
The two dangers are delay and fudging. The first should be easily avoidable. We took three and a
half months abstracting and tabulating with one-eighth of the Provincial revenue establishment , and if wc
had tabulated" for tahsils only instead of for districts and divisions also, we should have saved three weeks
out of that. The work could be done in the tahsils in a month or six weeks with the greatest ease. But
the danger of fudging would be infinitely greater. As I have noticed in section 891, the strain of Census
operations once relaxed, there is a tendency to scamp further work done in the districts ; while in the di\isional offices the staff take a fresh departure. It is true that the inconvenience and discomfort entailed upon
the patwaris by living away from their district for three months is so great that there is a strong temptation
to make the figures agree by any means, and so have done with them. But native clerks will fudge under
all circumstances if they can ; and everything depends upon the supervision. Now it is possible to select
ten first-class supervisors for the^ divisional offices; but it is not possible to procure thirty-two such
men for the districts. Thus, on the ground that there are some Tahsildars under whom the work would
certainly be fudged, and on that ground alone, I am inclined to think that the divisional offices offer tinbest practicable solution of the problem. The consideration that weighs most strongly with me on the
other side of the question is, that if the tabulation were done in the districts, errors detected in the tallies
would be sent to the districts to be put right; and the small compiling staff would not be, as it now was,
overburdened with the labour of correction. But I think that the burden might in future be largely reduced
by measures to be described later on, and in any case the difficulty could be met by increasing the compiling establishment.
894. Preliminary examination of schedules.—When the schedules reached the divisional offices,
they were examined in order to make sure—
(1) that we had a book of schedules for every block in the Province ;
(2) that the series of pages in each book was complete, and that their number was not less than
the number of pages entered on the outside label ;
(3) that the series of house-numbers was complete, and that each house had an entry of some sort
against it, even if only to state that it was empty ;
(4) that the private schedule for every house which was noted as being allowed such a schedule
was stitched into the book.
Where the schedules were found defective in any of these respects the Tahsildar was referred to for an
explanation or requested to send the missing papers. This examination assured us that we had a Census
entry for every house entered in any house-list throughout the Province. Having this assurance, we assumed that those entries were correct save in the very few cases noted in section 80,0,. Some of the
offices began making a more minute examination; for instance, to ascertain whether ail the families and
heads of families shown in the house-list w-ere entered in the schedules. But I put a stop to this. The
house-list was prepared some months before the final Census ; it was quite possible that the apparent omission, where it occurred, was correct ; and if we had examined every entry to discover whether it afforded
room for suspicion of inaccuracy, our labour would have been endless while the errors corrected by it
would have been inappreciable in number and importance. I was astonished to find in how extraordinarily
few instances did this preliminary examination afford any grounds for inquiry, and how hardly ewer did
that inquiry disclose any real omission. In tahsil after tahsil not a single apparent flaw could be detected ; and the schedule record of houses, at any rate, was practically perfect.
895. T h e process of abstracting.—The abstracting was done by abstractors who took out the figures

Mr. Elliott permitted either of these two plans to be followed, and, availing myself of this license, I

o a the ground that it afforded no complete check. This is true. If there were t^vo Shiahs in the block

if he made a tick for each entry, it is less probable that he would make a tick under Sunni for the second

separately for each age-period used in the age-register; not because the details of age were needed in

again.
896. T h e system of check,-^And this leads to the whole question of check on the abstracting. The
checking staff was, of course, kept quite separate from the abstracting staff, the work done one day by the
latter being handed over next morning to the former to be checked. Here the totals and details of the different registers were compared with one another and with the figures of the village abstracts (which last
were never allowed to pass into the hands of the abstracting department at all), and if any differences were
detected the figures which were doubtful were re-abstracted; while even if there were no discrepancies, a
Certain proportion of the figures were tested by being got out again by the checkers, the proportion varying with the'degree of accuracy with which the men whose work was under examination were found to
.abstract* Now my first rule was that under no circumstances whatever was any preliminary or intermediate check to be allowed; that the registers were to be totalled in the checking, and not in the abstracting
department; and that till they passed into the hands of the checker, no examination of any sort was to be
Ttiade. This practically amounted to saying to four clerks who sat down together to abstract the figures
Elf a single book: " You must g e t out your figures independently of each other, and must not examine
^*thetiJ to see if they agree with o t e another., I will do that for you; and if they do not agree you will
*'>aye to do them again, and will-be pufrlshed besfdes.'* The plan was admirable in theory; but I ought

Chap. XII. J

THE PREPARATION OF THE FIGURES.

Part I.—Abstracting the Figures.
The first end I attempted to attain by making them compare the figures for each opening of the..
book—say 25 to 50 souls—as soon as it was abstracted. If any detail did not agree, it was almost as easy
to run the eye up the column and get the right figures for the detail in question, as to fudge the corrections
so as not in the act of doing so to produce a second discrepancy. For the detection of fudging (or of
honest error) there were three means. First the comparison of the figures of the registers one with
another. This only availed when the fudging was clumsily done, and was really nothing more than a very
necessary check upon and repetition of the comparison already made by the abstractors. Secondly the
comparison of the abstracted figures with the figures of the village abstracts. This was certainly a check
in that it often led to re-examination of the figures ; but it extended to only a few totals and really gave us
much unnecessary labour, for in the enormous majority of the cases of difference, the village abstracts were
wrong and the register right. Thirdly the testing the work by taking a certain number of items from
each register and getting out the figures for them from the schedules independently. This was the real
safeguard ; and upon the efficiency with which this test was applied and the extent to which it was carried
depended the measure of confidence which could be placed in the accuracy of the results.
But it is evident that the allowing the abstractors to compare the figures as they went along very
greatly reduced the value of any inter-check between the registers, for they were practically no longer
independent. If the clerk who left out a Shiah in sectiorr 895 found his Musalmins short by one, he
might look at the book again and detect his omitted Shiah, or he might save himself the trouble by adding
on a Sunni; but in no case would the register show that there had been a discrepancy. Thus it was
almost as safe to allow him to pick out his Shiah at once, and take the rest as Sunnis without counting them.
So, too, it made the combination of age details with education of very small value as a check ; lor though
the existence of the two registers showing age would perhaps disclose an error if it had occurred, yet it
disclosed it to the abstractor, and the only way of finding out whether he had corrected it or fudged it was
to re-abstract the figures. And it made the retention of the working party of four independent abstractors unnecessary, if any speedier method could be devised.
897. Proposed system of abstracting.—We tried many ways of combining our clerks and registers,
for I allowed considerable license in this matter within the limits which I considered necessary to ensure
accuracy, and which I insisted upon being strictly observed ; as for instance, when I directed that the a«e
registers were in all cases to be filled up from dictation and not by a single clerk. I found that men got
accustomed some to one method and some to another, each preferring his own ; and above all that certain
men worked well together in parties of two, three, or four as the case might be, and gave the largest and best
outturn when working in their own fashion. A slow or stupid man was a terrible drag upon sharp companions, who could really turn out more work without him. But on the whole the following is the plan
which I believe to be the best, and which I would recommend for future adoption. Two clerks take two
books of schedules; one reads out the entries while the other fills up the register for age, religion, and
civil condition. Then each takes a book and fills up the entries for education, infirmities, sect, language,
and birthplace, only counting those who are educated or infirm, or who belong to a strange sect, speak
a strange language, or were born out of the district. In the same way each man fills up the registers for
caste and occupation. It is true that in these two last registers every caste and occupation has to be
entered. But they are nevertheless better filled up by one man than from dictation, as the caste and
occupation is very generally constant for each family, and often for a house or even a group of house's.
The clerk notes how far down the page the entry is the same, counts up the items he wants within those
limits, and enters them at once. The education register we had to prepare from dictation because we
were compelled to take out age statistics in it. But this I think was unnecessary.
The system I have sketched above lessens the value of the check afforded by comparison of registers
one with another, because it does away with the working party of four independent abstractors. Hut as
I have already pointed out, the value of that check is almost destroyed by intermediate comparison, which
as I have explained above it is quite impossible to prevent. I am convinced that the onlv reallv effective
check is the examination of a percentage of all work done by doing it over again ; and during the earlier
stages of the work, before the men become expert, the percentage so examined should be large. After
a time it is marvellous how accurate the work becomes, and how rarely an error occurs. There is, of course,
one way in which absolute accuracy can be secured. That is, by having each register prepared perfectly
independently and by a separate staff of abstractors for all the blocks, and not allowing the staff who artabstracting one set of figures to have any knowledge of the results obtained by those who have abstracted
another set. The registers would then be compared ; and if two sets of figures did not agree they would again
be abstracted by two new sets of men who had no knowledge of the figures obtained in the first abstraction. There are many practical objections to this system. There would, I think, be danger of confusion,
delay, loss of papers, and omission of portions of the process. And I doubt whether, with native agency,
it would be possible to prevent the results being communicated for the purpose of fudging.
But an
absolute fatal objection, to my mind, is the enormous expense attending it. I understand that it has
been followed at Madras, and has been so costly that the Imperial Government have declined to pay
for it.
808. Cost versus accuracy.—And here I would fain deliver my soul as to the ethics of fudging. The
practical question that has to be considered in all operations of this sort is, what is the attainment of any
particular degree of accuracy worth ; given that a certain amount of extra labour and extra check will
secure a certain enhanced measure.of accuracy, is the increase in accuracy worth the cost of attaining it.
And I think one very obvious limit is afforded by the consideration, that under no circumstances can it be
worth while to tabulate material with a degree of accuracy greater than that which the material itself may
reasonably be supposed to possess in the first instance. I have heard that in a certain Census the wholestaff was engaged for six weeks in examining and removing a discrepancy of four in the total population
of a Province. There were special circumstances which justified the delay in this case ; but under ordinary
circumstances I should look upon it as waste of time, and should consider conscientious fudoini/
452

THE PREPARATION OF THE FIGURES.

("Para. 900.

L Chap. XII.

Part I.—Abstracting the Figures.
}0*rfC& duty. Now I fully admit that it is quite out of the question to allow such agency as we employed in
our offices any license whatever in this direction. The rule must be stringent that every error, however
small, is to be removed by retabulation ; though however strict it may be, it is perhaps hopeless to expect
that it will be observed in all cases. Fudging is like cribs, which are invaluable in the hands of a man who
knows how and when to use them, but must not be given to school-boys. But it is possible to carry refinements of check so far that they cost more than the extra accuracy which they secure is worth ; and this is
certainly the case when the errors which they prevent are smaller than the original error in the material
under treatment, and when the system of check can be relaxed without allowing any discretion or license to
the establishment, either directly or by implication.
Now he must be a very sanguine man who imagines that the mean probable error in the enumeration of souls at the Census of 1881 was less than, say a quarter per cent., and the error in the entry
of details will have been larger; in some respects much larger. I do not however wish to be understood to
think that all errors of not more than one quarter per cent, may be safely neglected in the tabulation of
our Census statistics. If this is the logical conclusion from my argument, 1 am quite content to be illogical
on the subject. But I do think that time and labour are both wasted in removing minute discrepancies which
bear no appreciable proportion to the figures involved, or to the much larger errors inherent in those figures
During the months of April, May, and June I spent some 60 days in inspecting my divisional offices, the
greater part of which were devoted-to checking abstracted figures by personal reference to the schedules
themselves. Knowing the want of accuracy which often characterises native work. I had expected to
rind errors in great numbers. .But I was utterly astonished at the rarity with which I detected even a single
error in the items I examined. And when I did find errors they were, as far as I remember almost without
a single exception, errors of one single unit in excess or defect So far as I saw, the errors in tabulation
by sex alone certainly did not reach anything approaching 1 per 1,000. The errors in other details
were proportionally larger, because the figures in which they occurred were necessarily smaller. But I do
not believe that the errors i n t h e entries of age even, the most difficult item to abstract, ever amounted to
one quarter per cent., even if their sign be neglected. And the errors in the totals tor tahsils and districts
must be of course many times smaller than the errors in individual blocks, for errors of excess and defect
correct each other to a very large extent.
It is a question of opinion and judgment, and not capable of calculation or proof; for wc know exactly
neither the amount of error in the original material nor the degree of inaccuracy which each relaxation of
the check will introduce. But it is my deliberate opinion, founded upon a very intimate knowledge of
the working of our divisional offices, that the system described in section 897, if supplemented by an
examination of a proper proportion of the figures, will give us figures corresponding more nearly with the
Census entries than those entries correspond with the actual facts, and therefore possessing all the
accuracy which it is worth while to aim at. What proportion of the figures should be examined it is
impossible to say exactly: but it should be large, probably something like 25 per cent., at the beginning of
the work, may be very much reduced as the men gain skill and certainty, should be smaller for the sharper
and larger for the inferior workers, and should never fall lower than two or three items in each register.
The smaller items are as a rule those most liable to error, while they can be examined most easily; and
ficrures which have beeu altered and corrected are especially open to suspicion. I have not hesitated
to"dwell at some length on this point, for it is important as largely determining the duration and cost of
the work of abstracting; and I understand that Mr. Plowden is of opinion that at a future Census far more
elaborate registers should be used with the sole purpose of rendering the check more stringent. I have
therefore thought it right to record my opinion, based upon my personal experience, to be taken for what
it is worth.
899- Departure from the entries in t h e schedules.—A question which had to be decided at an
early stage in the operations, and one with regard to which it was most difficult to lay down any satisfactory rule, was, how far it was allowable to depart from the facts entered in the schedules when they appeared to'be contradicted by other entries. The easiest, and perhaps the commonest case was that of sex
When we found " Musammat Faridan, married woman, midwife,'' with a " o n e " opposite her name in
the male column, we had no hesitation in entering her as a female. But when we had '' Mahamdu, caste
"Mughal, tribe Machhi, occupation fisherman," or " Bhagwana, caste Rajput, tribe Mali, occupation gar" dener,"' it was more difficult to decide whether their caste should be taken as Mughal and Rajput or as
Machhi'and Mali. In the Eastern Panjab, where these cases were chiefly reported, it was practically certain that the latter was the true entry, and that the men had entered themselves as of the higher caste (as
several Deputy Commissioners have reported to have often been the case) either as a joke or in accordance with the traditional origin of their present inferior castes. In the Western Panj.lb such cases were
not as a rule reported ; and the reason doubtless was that, though such false entries were probably at leasi
as common there as in the east, yet it was by no means safe to assume that in the Mahomedan portion of
the Province a man was not a Mughal or a Rajput merely because he worked as a fisherman or a gardener.
In some of these cases the village patwari, who knew the individual well, had in preparing the village
abstract of castes and clans entered him as a Machhi or Mali; and here I had no hesitation in following
the village abstract. But where this guide was wanting I decided that, however probably wrong, the entry
'must be assumed to be right unless the patwari of the village was present in the office, or other similar
evidence was available. I do not think any other rule would have been safe.
900. Utility Of the preliminary and village abstracts.—In section S8S I explained that the second
part of the village abstracts contained the names of the religions, sects, languages, birthplaces, and occupations occurring in the schedules. They were utilised thus. A clerk took a book of schedules with
its abstract, and filled in the headings into each register. For instance, in the occupation register
under the heading " Name of occupation," he wrote down all the occupations mentioned in the abstract one
under the other, leaving a large space in which to enter ticks opposite those occupations which a glance at
the schedules snowed him to be most prevalent in the block. Thus the abstractor found his headings reaJv

entered to his hand when he came to fill in the register. Where the working-party plan was adopted this
was aconvenience, as otherwise the other three men would have to wait while the fourth wrote in his"~-register each new heading as it occurred for the first time. But when the registers were filled up by a
single clerk and not from dictation, this inconvenience was not felt; and in some cases 1 found that the men
did not think it worth while to utilise this part of the village abstracts. There is undoubtedly one strong
objection to their use. The list of occupations and birthplaces given in the village abstract was seldom
absolutely complete. Now when a clerk took a blank register and wrote down his own headings as they
occurred, he naturally expected to have to fill in each new one as he came across it: moreover, he knew
exactly where each one was to be found in his lists, no unimportant point so far as speed is concerned with
a list of 30 or 40 occupations. But when he had his headings ready filled in for him, he not only had to
search for each the first few times it occurred, but when he came to one which had been omitted from tin
list and his search failed to discover it, he was more likely to make a tick opposite some other occupation
already entered, instead of adding the new name to the list as he should have done. On the whole I should
be inclined to omit this portion of the village abstracts as useless ; and if the plan of abstracting described
in section 897 is substituted for the working-party system, 1 should most certainly do so.
With regard to the first part of the village abstracts, it was of but little use as an independent check.
The figures for sex and civil condition were very generally accurate, probably because they checked each
other ; but the details of education and infirmities were often slightly wrong, while the number of persons
whose occupation was recorded was often very incorrectly stated. So far as the figures are needed for
the purpflse of publishing preliminary results as soon after the Census as possible, it is absolutely necessary
to prepare them ; but the remaining figures must be abstracted with much greater accuracy than at the law
Census if they are to be more help than hindrance.

Part II.—Tabulation of the Figures.

PART II.-TABULATION OF THE FIGURES.
901. T h e village-tables.—The abstracting finished, the next step was to collect the figures into villagetables, the top headings of which were so arranged as to exhibit the details needed for the final tables,
while on the left-hand side of each table was printed a list of all the villages or towns of the tahsil with
their component blocks, arranged in the same order as in the tahsil abstract (see section 8<jo), opposite
each of which the figures found in the register were entered in the appropriate column. Two separatetables were prepared, one for villages and one for towns, in each tahsil ; and each of the larger tables consisted of many parts or volumes, the number of columns being far too great to be shown in a single opening
These tables were 17 in number, and exhibited the following details, sex being distinguished in all :—
A.—Houses, families, and population—
X.—Occupation, over and under 15 years ol
I.—Civil condition by religion and age.

No. XVI was merely a check table, while No. Ill was added for convenience only, and contained no
fresh information.
902. Mode of filling up the tables.—The work of filling up the tables for age, civil condition, Musalman sects, education, and infirmities, was simple enough. The columns, if numerous, were at any rate fixed
in number; and all that was needed was care that the figures were not entered in tin; wrong column. This
was not the case with the tables for language and Christian sects ; but even here the number of different
entries in the register was so comparatively small that there was no difficulty in finding the appropriate
column for each. But in the case of birthplace, of castes, and of occupations, the number of entries in the
register was so enormous that the greatest difficulty was experienced in tabulating them.
The first step taken towards meeting the difficulty was to sub-divide the birthplace table into separatetables for Panjab districts (putting those of the division under tabulation first), Panjab Native States, other
parts of India, and foreign countries. Similarly the occupation tables were separate for agriculturists,
traders, service, artisans, and others. And I think it would have been well to have split up the castes in a
similar manner into some such classes as priestly and ascetic, land-owning, mercantile, artisan, menial, and
vagrant and outcast. Of course this classification was adopted purely to render the tabulation easier; it
was exceedingly rough, by no means uniform in the various offices, and was wholly disregarded in the final
compilation. The plan then adopted was as follows. In each tahsil, and especially in the rural portions,
there were a certain number of castes and occupations which recurred in almost every village. These were
known to the local staff, and were entered at the head of the columns of the first part of each table. The
entries which occurred less frequently were similarly entered in a second part, and so on till all but the
very rare entries were disposed of, the entries in the later parts of the table being often alphabetical '. A
tabulating clerk was in charge of each part of the table, and the registers were passed from one to the
other ; each one posting up the entries belonging to his headings, a numbered list of which lay by him, and
ticking them off on the register. When the register had passed through the hands of these clerks, the remaining entries if any, which consisted wholly of those of rare occurrence, were entered on rough slips
framed on the pattern of the village-table; and when the tahsil was completed, these were arranged and
entered into a final part of the table. After the entries for a whole circle or group of circles had been tabulated, the tables were taken and the entries in them read out to clerks who sat with the schedules in their
hand and ticked off each as it was read. Every entry was thus gone over twice in opposite directions, first
from the schedule to the table, and afterwards from the table to the schedule.
903. System of check.—Every table or part of a table had a last column headed total males and females,
and as each page was entered up the figures were totalled horizontally and vertically, and the results compared. When the table was finished, the page totals were collected at the end and added together to get the
final totals. These last were then arranged side by side in a check-abstract devised for the purpose, such
that all figures which, derived from different tables, ought to correspond, were exhibited one under the other,
and any discrepancy could at once be detected. The points in which the figures disagreed were then examined page by page and, within the pages which did not agree, block by block, till the error was run to
ground. In the larger tables errors were in some offices so frequent, that it was found simpler to compare all
entries in the tables page by page as they were filjed in, and while the papers were in hand and the entries
fresh in the memory. But I cannot help thinking that this was really waste of time ; and that if sufficient
care had been taken in posting, it would have been enough to examine only such details as a comparison of the
final totals showed not to agree. The other method involved examination of figures which need not have
been examined in detail at all, since their totals agreed already (see further section 904).
Here the absence of a precaution of which I ought to have thought caused a good deal of trouble. I
had ordered the lists of villages and blocks which formed the side-headings of the tables to be printed and
cut up into slips, which slips were pasted on to the side of each table. But I did not direct that the pages
' These " parts " were, as it were, separate volumes of the same table.

Para. 904."!

Part II.—Tabulation of the Figures.
of all the tables should be made of one uniform depth, so that, if the third page of any one table contained-.
blocks numbering 41 to 60, the third page of every other table throughout should contain the figures for
precisely those same blocks. This plan should be adopted in future, as it immensely facilitates the page
by page comparison. Exceptions, however, will be necessary, as I proceed to show. I had at first
directed that every single table or part of a table should contain all the blocks (rural or urban, as the case
might be) of the tahsil. But I found that this increased so enormously the bulk of the tables, and consequently the labour of filling them up and the risk of error, that I had to modify my orders. For instance,
in tahsil Bhiwani there are 3 Sikhs in a population of 103,556. Every one of the ten tables which distinguished religion had therefore to include a part headed "Sikh ; " but the entries in each were confined to
a single block, and it would have been absurd to exhibit in each all the remaining blocks of the tahsil.
So on the Pathan frontier there are hundreds of villages without a single Hindu ; while the entries for Christian sect and race and for infirmities are everywhere few and far between. Thus it became necessary tn
lay down a rule for each division as to which tables and parts of tables should exhibit all the blocks, and
which only those blocks in which there were figures to be entered.
904. The tabulation is a most important portion of the work, and one which it is exceedingly difficult to
supervise or examine effectively. It is easy enough to take a book of schedules with its abstracting registers
and to compare the one with the other ; but to find out whether the items in any given one of those registers
have been honestly tabulated, it is necessary to pick them out one by one in some 30 or 40 tables, some ot
which contain columns by the hundred. At the same time if the figures are honestly totalled, the check is
almost automatic. The comparison of totals is an absolute check against omission ; while a comparison ot
total males and females is an absolute check against what the baboo calls " miscolumning " where, as is almost always the case, the entry is misplaced by one column only ; for an entry shown one column to the right
or left of its proper place will fall under the wrong sex 1 . Thus the only errors which a mere comparison of
totals does not at once bring to light are those in which an entry, while falling under the proper religion, age,
or other head occurring in two or more tables, is misplaced by an even number of columns, two or more.
And with the most ordinary care such errors should seldom or never occur.
The real danger to be apprehended is that the Clerks, when they find totals disagree, should arbitrarily alter them to correspond with each other; or, in the larger tables, should save themselves the trouble
of searching for the proper column or of adding a new one, by entering the item in a column to which it
does not properly belong. The sub-division of tables into parts described in section 902 is a valuable precaution against this last practice. But I see no way of detecting or preventing dishonest work at this stage
beyond careful selection of the tabulating clerks, and incessant and minute supervision. Re-examination
of the work is, as I have said, infinitely difficult in the larger tables.
And this is one reason why 1 object to a page by page comparison of totals. If the final totals onh
are compared, the comparison is made by a separate staff; and the correcting department has to bring tinerror when found to the supervisor, and to satisfy him that it really has been detected and accounts lor tindiscrepancy in the totals. But if the tabulators compare the totals page by page as they go along, this iimpossible. On the other hand, the comparison of final totals does not reveal errors which are neutralised
by other errors of equal amount but opposite sign ; while the page by page comparison will bring to light
all such errors as do not balance one another within the limits of a single page, and thus affords greater
security against internal mistakes. The latter is the better plan if the agency can be trusted ; the former
if it cannot.
As a rule I believe that this work w-as honestly and thoroughly done. In some cases a grieve uiamount of labour and time was expended in removing errors absolutely trivial and insignificant in amount,
but which it would not have been safe to allow the staff to disregard.
905. Classification of entries.—It is in this stage of the work that the question of classification hafirst to be considered; a question by far the most difficult and important of all that present themselves
during the operations subsequent to enumeration. In the abstracting stage it hardly arises ; lor the strict
rule was that the abstractors should in all cases show the entries exactly as they were entered in the schedules, and the only case in which that rule was waived was where the difference between two entries ot
one and the same occupation consisted merely in the omission in one ot them of an insignificant word.
Thus, "spinning," "spinning raw cotton" and "spinning (cotton) thread" [ki'itim, nii knfiiii. and s.-ir
katna) were allowed to be taken together. But even this license was seldom needed, for the entries in
each book of schedules being made by a single enumerator, the term used for any given occupation was
generally constant.
But when the entries came to be arranged in the village tables, the number of birth-places, castes.
and occupations described in different words"but bearing the same meaning was found to be so enormous,
that some degree of classification was absolutely necessary if the tables were to be filled up at all. h
would have been absurd to make the Kangra patwaris enter Jawalamukhi and Kangra, both within tin
district, as separate birthplaces ; to have separate caste columns for Punwir and Tt'mwar, both w ell-know n
Raipiit clans ; or to show nai, hajjam, hajamat karna, -and bal katna as distinct occupations. 1 lie rules
adopted in each of these three cases were as follows :
For birthplaces, lists of the districts in the North-West Provinces, Bengal, and Simlh, and of the
Native States of Rajputana were sent to the Divisional offices ; and any entry of birthplace corresponding
with any one of these names was assumed to refer to that district or state, and was shown in the corresponding column. So again, any entry corresponding with the name of a Panjdb district or state was assumed to mean that district or state. 'This was probably wrong in some cases. Many people showed the
I'illaw of birth, instead of the district, as they should have done under the rules; and probably some "Shahpurs " for instance entered in the schedules, really referred to some one or other of the countless villages ot
1
For this reason the occupation tables should not be prepared separately for males and females as s i s done on this occasion, because in relinquishing your double sex-columns you lose an invaluable check.

to assume that the instructions had been carried out. I myself went through the European schedules, in
which, too, unknown British villages were constantly entered in the birthplace column ; referred such as I
could to England, Ireland, or Scotland, and classed the rest as British Isles. Where a birthplace not falling

006. In the matter of castes, difficulty was caused by the innumerable instances in which, especially in
the Western Panjab, tribes or clans had been returned instead of castes. In the Multin and Derajat divisions the so-called castes entered in the schedules were literally numbered by thousands. Here I was
compelled to leave much to the discretion and local knowledge of my Divisional officers. They had been
selected with special reference to their acquaintance with the divisions the figures of which they were respectively tabulating, and the number of cases was so enormous that reference to the people themselx es through
the district authorities would have practically stopped our work for months. Accordingly I permitted the
Divisional officers, when they were satisfied beyond all reasonable possibility of doubt, either from their
own knowledge or from the local knowledge of their staff or by examination of the entries in other
schedules of the neighbourhood, that a certain tribe or clan belonged to a given caste, to include that clan
under that caste. But where there was reasonable room for doubt they were to show the clan separately ;
and it was strongly impressed upon them that it was always better to show too much than too little detail.
Further, they were to furnish me with lists showing all the clans or other entries which the)- had included
in each caste-column.
With regard to occupation, the rule laid down for the Divisional officers was that, where the occupations were identical, the difference being purely one of language, they were to be taken together; but that
different occupations, however nearly related, were to be separated. Certain rules were issued for the
classification of Government servants, the number of whose occupations would otherwise have been unmanageable. Here, too, lists were ordered to be kept showing all the different terms which had been taken as
one and the same occupation and included under each heading of the table. The instructions to enumerators directed them to enter one occupation only for each person, save where agriculture was combined
with another calling. As a fact, however, double occupations were recorded in very many instances, and
one of them had to be selected for tabulation. The rules by which the tabulators were to be guided in
these cases were—
(1) where one occupation was permanent and the other temporary, the former was to be taken ;
thus a man who was entered as reaper and water-carrier was tabulated as water-carrier:
(2) where the more important occupation could be identified, it was to be selected ; thus a man
entered as oilman and cctton scutcher was counted as an oilman :
(3) in all other cases the occupation first mentioned was to be taken, in the absence of any guide
to selection.

far larger than I or anybody else had conceived possible. Unfortunately the detailed figures have been
destroyed ; and I dare not speak from memory, for I should be accused of absurd exaggeration. But some
idea of their number may be gained from the fact that, after the Divisional officers had done all thev could
or might do to reduce the number by classifying them under the rules given above, the number of separate
headinos still remaining, and returned by them in their village tables, was as follows :—

Para. ga."\
Chap. XII. J

THE PREPARATION OF THE FIGURES.

Part IL—Tabulation of the Figures.
At a future Census the plan of operations I would recommend would be something of this sort. The
urban limits would be governed in all cases by the considerations just discussed. They would then be split
up into sections and blocks in the manner described in section 846. The Deputy Commissioner
and one of his Assistants would naturally visit every town in the district during Census operations, and
would then examine the manner in which this work had been done on the spot. If he found reason to
think that municipal limits should be departed from, he would report his proposals ; but I think very strong
grounds should be shown before this was allowed. When it was finally settled what was to be included in
each town, special labels of a different colour from those used for rural books of schedules would be pasted
on the urban books, in which, instead of the name of the village, the names of the (1) town (2) suburb or
other sub-division should be shown separately. And it would help the compiling offices if the bindings of
urban books were also covered with cloth of a different colour from that used for the rural blocks. The
index-register of towns would be kept separate from that of villages ; and in it care would be taken to show
the name of the town in column 3 and that of the suburb in column 6 only ; while in the tahsil abstract
for towns, which should be separate from that of villages, there would be two columns, one for the name
of the town and another for that of subsidiary parts. These arrangements, however, would apply in general to municipalities, civil lines, and cantonments only. Village areas including a population of 5,000 or
more cannot in all cases be picked out till at any rate the preliminary abstracts have been prepared and tinpopulation thus ascertained; they seldom possess any suburbs worth separating; the village boundary is
followed as a matter of course ; and all that is necessary is to exclude cases in which the population is scattered.
912. Floating population.—There was a certain floating population to be dealt with, generally but
not always inconsiderable in amount, consisting of passengers by rail and boat, and of large gangs of workmen collected upon canals, railroads under construction, and the like. These were not included in the
population of any village or town, but were entered in the tables at the end of the village population of the
tahsil in which they were enumerated. The resident Railway population was of course treated exactly
like other residents.
913. European schedules.—The mass of the work being done in the vernacular, I was in considerable doubt as to how to deal with schedules filled up in English. I had at first thought of collecting them
all at Lahore and abstracting and tabulating them by a separate English staff. But the divisional officers
objected that this would involve two difficulties. In the first place we did not know where all the English
schedules were, a considerable number being scattered about through the books of the Province ; they
would have been constantly turning up in small numbers which would have been very difficult to deal with,
while there would have been some danger of loss. And it would have been very difficult to arrange lor tluincorporation in the figures of the village tables prepared by the Di\isional offices, of the details ol the
English schedules tabulated by a central staff. In almost all the Divisional offices there were one or two
men who knew English; and where this was not the case I sanctioned the entertainment of a couple o!
clerks for the purpose. These men made translations of the English schedules into Hindustani, and read
them out to the abstractors. On my visits of inspection I paid special attention to the subject, and especially to the translation of names of occupations, of which I caused lists to be shown me together with the translations used. But the result was not satisfactory. The age and sex were of course tabulated with ease ,
but there were difficulties in respect of religion, sect, language, nationality, and birthplace ; while the tabulation of occupations was exceedingly unsatisfactory. At a future Census I should preler to keep tinpopulation enumerated in English wholly separate. The great mass of it is European ; and so long as the
numbers of each sex were communicated to the Divisional offices to be incorporated in the village tables,
the exclusion .of the other details from those tables would not impair the utility ol the figures lor general
district purposes ; while supplementary tables could be supplied to the district office where large European
populations existed. I would then have all the English schedules for each district collected together by
the Deputy Commissioner in the first instance, each bearing plainly the number ol its circle and block and
the name of the village or town to which it belonged, and send them all to the centra! office to lie tabulated
by English clerks under the direction of the Deputy Superintendent. The figures would lie added to those
furnished by the Divisional office for each district ; while only the number ol each sex in each village or
town would be communicated to the district offices for incorporation by separate entry in their tables.

"THE PREPARATION OF THE FIGURES.

L Chap. XI I.

Part II.—Tabulation of the Figures.
Jje-TSrote the figure 5 opposite the name of that sub-division : if 7 more males occurred in the second
abstract, he cut out the 5 and wrote 12, and so on, not entering the individual numbers, but always adding
on the new number to the total of all previous entries. When the space opposite any entry was filled
up, the entry was cut out and re-entered at the bottom of the slip, the last total of the first entry bein°made the starting point of the new one. The castes with very numerous sub-divisions were worked through
first one by one for the whole tahsil, the sub-divisional slips for each being kept in alphabetical bundles.
T h e minor castes were then taken out from each village abstract, the slips for each caste being tied up
together. For each of the smaller castes the figures for a whole tahsil were worked out on only two or three
sets of slips; but in some parts, and especially in the west, the number of entries in Jats and Rajputs was
so large that by the time a circle or two had been finished the slips had become unmanageable, and a
new set was begun, the whole being totalled up for the tahsil at the end.
No exact check upon these figures was possible. Even the total population would not agree exactly, as
there were small errors in the figures of the village abstracts. And, as before noted, the enumerator had
occasionally departed from the caste entries in the schedules, where he knew them to be wrong. But
the totals for each sex and caste were compared with the tables, and where the discrepancy was considerable the figures were re-examined. Thus the results can; only be taken as approximately accurate. But they are not needed for the Imperial tables ; and the errors will be so small in comparison
with the numbers involved, as only very slightly to impair the practical value of the information. As it was,
the Divisional officers estimate that, on an average, a month of their time was taken up by this work ;
while the compiling office spent at least six weeks more over the figures. To have abstracted them from
the schedules and brought them into perfect accordance with the other figures would have taken the offices
six months at a moderate computation.
915. P r e p a r a t i o n of divisional tables a n d tahsil summaries.—When the village tables were complete, the Divisional officers prepared from them tables in duplicate for each district and division in the form
of the final Census tables, showing all details contained in those tables separately for towns and villages in
each tahsil, together with district and divisional totals. These were filled up from the village-tables by direct
transfer of the totals for each tahsil, save where details were given by religion in the village and not in the
final tables, in which cases the figures for the various religions were added together in separate forms devised
for the purpose in order to obtain the figures for total population. Check sheets exhibiting side by side
all figures taken from separate tables that should correspond with each other, were prepared for these
tables and sent up with them. Further, as the village tables were not only very numerous but also, encumbered as they were with details for each separate block, exceedingly cumbrous, abstracts were prepared for
each tahsil exhibiting for each town and village in the tahsil the following figures, with details of sex in each
case ::—
(7) Male agriculturalists over 15 years of
(1) Total population.
age.
(2) Under 10 years of age.
(8) Under instruction.
(3) Unmarried.
(9) Able to read and write.
(4) Hindu.
(10) Numbers of each of the twelve most
(5) Sikh.
numerous castes in the tahsil.
(6) Musajman.
No. 7 showed the number of adult males who had returned themselves of any occupation which was
classed in the Divisional office as agricultural; while generally the classification of entries effected during
tabulation will cause discrepancies between the details of these abstracts and of the final tables. But the
differences will be unimportant, and the abstracts will be convenient for ordinary purposes of reference.
They are however, only extracts, and have not been subjected to any check beyond the ordinary comparison with the original; and the figures of the village tables alone can be considered as authoritative. Both
the village-tables and the abstracts have been sent to the district offices for record.
Ol6. Progress Of the work.—The schedules and staff ought to have been collected at the Divisional
offices by the 15th March at the latest. But they were really not all there till the end of the month.
Meanwhile the examination of the schedules had been pushed on, and abstracting was in full swing by the
beginning of April. By the end of the month we had abstracted the figures for ten million souls. During
May we dealt with 8 million more, leaving less than a million for June ; while all but two of the offices had
finished their abstracting before the end of that month. The Jalandhar office was short-handed owing to
the fact that two of the three districts were under settlement, while the patwaris of the third (Kangra) were
often ignorant of the Persian character. Accordingly part of the abstracting for this division was done by
other offices which had finished their own work early. We found the average number of souls for which
complete sets of figures were abstracted by each abstractor in a working day to be as follows :—
From starting work to end of April

These were, I believe, the highest averages obta.ined in any province in India. The averages in the
several offices differed considerably, being smaller in those districts which included large towns with their
numerous occupations, and in which several religions were commonly found in a single village.
During the month of June the entering up the entries in the village-tables and the tabulation of tribal
sub-divisions was completed save in Jalandhar and Peshawar, which were about a week behindhand ;
and the work left to be done in July was the final check, the preparation of Divisional tables and tahsil
summaries, and of certain tables needed for the information of the Registrar-General of Great Britain.
The patwaris were gradually drafted off to their homes from the beginning of July, and the offices linally
closed at various dates between the 3rd and 24th, except that of Jalandhar which only completed its work
on the 6th August, having had to wait for the schedules of Lahul and Spiti which were not received till
the end of July. The Divisional tables were translated in the central compiling office and issued together
with one set of the vernacular tables to Deputy Commissioners by the first week of August, and to Commissioners some ten days later. The record of progress is continued at the end of the next part of this
chapter.

rjivtsfonal tables were prepared. In the Lahore office, indeed, it was found that the totals of certain tables
did not agree, false check statements having been prepared by the supervising staff, while the men in question had been dismissed to their districts. They were at once recalled, and the tables corrected. I reported

done ; though it would have been done better had it been done gradually.

Para. $*>.~|
Chap. XII. J

THE PREPARATION OF THE FIGURES.

Part III.—Compilation of the Figures.

PART III.-COMPILATION OF THE FIGURES.
920. T h e Central compilation office.—The vernacular staff of the central office consisted of 30 patwiris, 3 qanungos, and a sadr qanungo, selected from the best men in the Divisional office staff, and as far
as possible representative of every part of the Province. These patwdris received an allowance of R 7
each in addition to their ordinary pay. The translating staff consisted of 12 clerks obtained in Lahore, and
was temporarily supplemented when necessary. I had roughly estimated that their services might be
needed for three months at the outside ; but it will presently be seen how widely I had miscalculated.
After four months the Deputy Commissioner of Karnad, who had lent his sadr qanungo at great inconvenience to himself and hindrance to his work, was compelled to recall him ; and the loss to me was most
serious. The office was superintended by an Extra Assistant Commissioner who was selected on account
of his general ability and knowledge of English.
921. Translation of the Divisional tables.—The first thing to be done was to translate and circulateto Deputy Commissioners and Commissioners the tables for their districts and divisions. The tables were
translated exactly as they were received from the Divisional offices, without examination or classification
of entries. It was important to furnish as quickly as possible the material for the district Census reports .
and the errors afterwards detected and corrected in the central office were so small as in no way to affect
the general significance of the figures.
022. Compilation of t h e figures.—The final tables may be divided into two classes. In those displaying total population, age, civil condition, Musalman sects, education, and infirmities, the number of columns
and their headings had been fixed from the first, classification was unnecessary, and all that had to be done
was to transfer the figures of the tables furnished by the Divisional offices to the Provincial tables, to add
areas, statistics of former population, and the like, to calculate such averages as were needed, and to translate the results. In the other tables we had, before this process could be begun, to classiiy the headings of
the Divisional tables, which were in all cases far more numerous than those which it was proposed to exhibit separately in the final tables. Now if the Divisional tables had been prepared with absolute accuracy
the compilation of the tables of the first class would have been simple and easy. But unfortunately this
was not always the case, and on comparing them numerous small discrepancies were detected. To remove these we had first to go back to the village tables, which gave us figures for each separate religion
only and not for total population, and gave them in 252 sets of items, one for towns and one for villages
in each tahsil. In many cases the totals of the village tables did not agree, the check having been made
page by page, and the final totals not having been sufficiently carefully given ; and in these cases we had to
examine the entries for individual blocks, of which there were 53,000 in the Province. But only in the
Amritsar division had we to go back to the schedules themselves, or rather to the registers abstracted from
them, before we could remove the errors. The same remarks apply to the second class of tables, with the
addition that in them an error in any one of our items had to be searched for among all the headings of the
village-tables which had been included in that item—in some cases a very large number.
923. Adjustment of errors.—Check sheets were kept up showing the plus or minus differences
between the various entries in each pair or more of tables that ought to agree ; and as each error was run
to ground, it was entered in a memorandum of corrigenda and the difference originally shown in the cheek
sheet reduced by its amount. The totals of the village tables were examined in all eases of discrepancv,
however small. When this had been done the check sheet showing the residual differences was brought to
me. I then decided which of these differences were sufficientlv important to necessitate our going behind
•the totals in order to remove them, and examining the figures for the individual blocks, of which there were
an average of 420 in each tahsil, and many more in some. Where the difference was minute I adjusted
(vulgice fudged) it in the largest item concerned, or, if it was capable of distribution, distributed it rateably over the few largest items. This course I have already justified in section SoS. No adjustment of
the sort was made except by me with my own hand ; and the danger that the knowledge that 1 did adjust
minute differences might lead the clerks to take a similar license, was guarded against by the invariable
rule that every error detected, corrected, and the correction taken credit for in the check sheet, was
shown to the superintendent, who was personally responsible that the error and correction were genuine.
In making these adjustments I did precisely what is daily done by even' surveyor. Xo survey closes with
absolute exactness, and the closing error, when inconsiderable, is distributed over the whole survey. It is
indeed possible, by repeating observations over and over again, to reduce the probable error to insignificance ; and this is the course adopted in great trigonometrical surveys which are to torm the basis ol further
work. But these are exceptional; and in all ordinary field work small errors occur and are adjusted without wasting further time upon them.
To give a concrete instance of my procedure. The details of age in Tables VI and \ 11 gave us
more trouble in this way, and displayed a larger number of discrepancies than any other pair of tables.
Neglecting all totals, and taking districts and separate religions onlv, Table VI contains 14.25,6 and
Table VII 8,976 separate entries which had to correspond with each other, the total single, married, and
widowed of any given age, sex, and religion in Table VI having to agree with the number of that age, sex,
and religion in Table VII. The corresponding entries in the totals of the village-tables from which these
figures had to be got numbered 109,728 and 69,080 respectively, while the corresponding number of entries
for individual blocks in the village-tables were 22,888,656 and 14,411,376. When the totals of the village
tables had been examined, the details of the differences that remained were brought to me. Of these
I directed a certain number to be further reduced by examination of the block entries. Eventually there
remained 67 differences, all of which I considered too minute to be worth further expenditure of time and
