Chap. XII. J

THE PREPARATION OF THE FIGURES.

Part III.—Compilation of the Fipres.
925. Classification of entries.—In the case of religion, birthplace, language, caste, and occupation^
before we could begin to compile the Provincial from the Divisional tables, we had to classify the headings ^
in the latter. The table on page 457 shows the number of separate headings included under each main group
in the several Divisional tables. Of course, to take a single instance, some of the 2,551 caste headings of
Multin and of the 989 similar headings of Rawalpindi were identical. But the great majority were not.
Thus, the list of separate and distinct occupations for British Territory alone numbered as many as 2,975.
The bottom line of the table giv"es the number of headings in the final Provincial table ; and shows that, for
instance, the 8,392 caste entries had to be grouped together and classed under 333 final heads.
The method adopted was as follows. Take the caste table as an example. The caste headings in
each Divisional table were numbered consecutively. An alphabetical list was then drawn up in vernacular
containing in the margin every caste shown in those tables. The body of the list contained twentvtwo columns, two for each division; and opposite each caste were entered, under each division in which it
occurred (1) the serial No. of the caste in the Divisional table, (2) the number of souls entered in that table
under the heading. The castes in this list were also severally numbered.
I then classified the headings,
and sent into the office a memorandum showing the castes which I had fixed for exhibition in the Provincial tables, and opposite each of them the several numbers borne by those caste headings of the above list
which I proposed to include in it. Thus suppose Chauhan was number 357 in the list of caste headings.
In the classification memorandum I prepared, 357 would be found under Rajput, together with the numbers
of all the other caste headings I had classed as Rajputs. The list would be referred to ; and opposite Xo.
357 would be found for each Divisional table the serial number of the heading Chauhan. The compiler had
vernacular slips for each division, showing on top the name and number of the caste to be shown in the
final tables, to the left hand the serial numbers and names of the caste-headings of the list which were to
be included in that caste, and in front of these, columns corresponding with the columns of the final table lor
each district in the division. He would enter Rajputs at the top, 357 Chauhan to the left hand, and opposite
it the figures for Chauhan in that division ; and so for each of the items classed by me under the heading Rajput. The totals of these items would be the entries for the division in the final table under the head Rajput.
These totals were totalled for the whole table to check the figures, and when the slips were correct thev
were made over to the translating branch, who filled in from them the sheets for the press. The object of
giving, in the first list, the number of souls under each heading, was to enable me to decide whether it was
worth while to show the heading separately, or, if not fully understood, to make inquiries about it from the
district.
It will be noted that almost throughout this process the entries were identified by numbers merely.
The compilers were told to class No. 357, which comprised No. 27 in Delhi, No. 98 in Hissar, and so on,
as Rajputs. I made my grouping in English from the lists read out to me in vernacular, and my grouping
had to be retranslated into vernacular. Thus although the most careful comparison and check was exercised throughout, there were many opportunities for error. For this reason the compiler's slips showed the
names as well as the numbers of the headings. When they were complete the slips for each division
were carefully examined by picked men from the division, who could hardly fail to detect any error in
classification. Besides this the numbers grouped were also compared to see if any number had been
omitted altogether, or grouped twice over in different places. Thus, if Chauhan was No. 357 and Agarwad No. 257, and under Rajput I entered 257 instead of 357 by mistake, the result would be that (1) Xo. 357
would not be classed at all, (2) No. 257 would be classed twice over, once under Banya, and once under
Rajput, (3) the slip for Rijptits would contain the entry Agarwal which would be obviously wrong. It is
exceedingly unlikely that any material errors escaped this triple check. All cases of this sort were sent
back to me for further orders, and thus I personally decided upon the classification of every single entry
in the tables.
926. Principles of classification.—This is equivalent to saying that the whole classification depended
for its value upon my personal knowledge of the subject-matter, added to such information as I could
obtain by enquiry within a very limited time. With respect to religion and sects there was no difficulty, if I except the very extraordinary forms which the names of Christian beliefs and sects sometimes assumed in the hands of vernacular writers, which often compelled us to refer to the original schedules
before we could identify them.
The number of languages was small, but my knowledge of the subject was still smaller; and I found
that very little was known about the hill languages by any body to whom I could refer. I had to class them
as best I could; and even the little knowledge I have since been able to acquire has shown me that 1 made
mistakes.
The birth-places I dealt with with the assistance of Mr. Baness's " I n d e x Geographicus," which I found
most useful. Every name which coincided with the name of an Indian district or state I assumed to
refer to that district or state. Where the name agreed with that of a considerable citv 1 assumed it to
refer to that city, and where there were several towns .of the same name I took the most important of
them. But there were still a large number of entries, especially in Derail Ghazi Khan, for the most part
including only two or three persons each, which 1 could,not identify. Where the form of the name was
distinctly Indian, as Xnagr or Ypur, I classed it as " India, part unspecified." So where the language
was obviously English, I classed the place under " British Isles." But there were still many wholly illegible or unintelligible entries which had to be consigned to the limbo of " Birthplace unspecified."
With occupations the chief difficulty was the immense number, my list showing not less than 2.Q7S
entries for British Territory alone. This large number was due more to the great diversity of local terms
than to any essential difference in meaning. The instructions of the Census Commissioner were that our
classification was to be based upon that adopted for the English Census so far as it was applicable to the
circumstances of the Panjib. I first translated or, where that was impossible, transliterated the whole
9,038 entries of the list, with the serial number of each. I then affixed to each the number ol the order and sub-

466

THE PREPARATION OF THE FIGURES.

r Para. 928.
L Chap. XII.

Part III.—Compilation of the Figures.
Qtier in which I classed it. These entries were then collected into separate slips for each sub-order; and
those I again classified into the groups which I proposed to show as separate occupations in my final tables.
A very considerable proportion of the terms used were wholly unintelligible to me. Where they related
to village life, my staff, drawn as it was from all parts of the Province, could generally supply the requisite
information. But the technical terms used in the industries of cities were often either unknown to my staff,
or my own knowledge was insufficient to enable me to understand their explanations. Dictionaries gave
but little assistance; and my Munshi used to come to office every morning with his pockets stuffed with
samples of fabrics, sweetmeats, drugs, and merchandize of sorts; while I spent many hours in the bazaar
consulting with friendly traders and artificers. The number of terms I had eventually to refer to the
district for explanation was not large. In the case of double occupations, of which a large number were
shown in the tables, especially for mercantile men, I followed the same rules which I had laid down for the
guidance of the tabulators, and which are given in section 906.
927. Classification of caste entries.—But the classification of castes was the one, not only which
presented the greatest difficulty, but the results of which must be regarded with the greatest uncertainty.
As already stated, the name of the tribe or clan had been in numberless instances entered in the
schedules, instead of the name of the caste. These had been to a certain extent classified under the
castes to which they belonged during the process of tabulation in the Divisional offices. But, as explained
fully in the Chapter on Castes, the tribe has almost superseded the caste in the Western Panjab; and many
tribes are held to belong to one caste in one district, and to another in a second and adjoining district. In
still more instances the tribal name is one common to many castes, and therefore tells us little or nothing
as to the place which the people should occupy in the caste tables. The plan followed was this. I first
went right through the caste lists and classed all the names with which my own knowledge, limited unfortunately to the people of the Dehli Territory, and a careful annotation of every Panjab Settlement Report
in print and of such other authorities as I had access to, enabled me to treat with any confidence. I then
collected my whole staff and went through the remaining names in committee; not acting, however, upon
the statement of any patwdri or q^nungo save in the case of general consensus as to a well-known and numerous tribe, unless he came from the part of the country to which the entry belonged and was personally
acquainted with members of the tribe.
The entries still remaining unclassed numbered many hundreds ; but a great part of them covered
only a few persons each, very often fewer than 50, not unfrequently even less than 10. I next circulated to
all Deputy Commissioners in the Province a table containing 84 of the largest of these entries, and supplementary tables of 293 smaller entries in the Multdn, Rawalpindi, Peshiwar, and Ambala divisions, especially the three first, asking for their opinion as to their classification. At the same time I circulated to all
districts a list of 201 castes which I proposed to distinguish in my tables, together with a detail of the principal headings which I had included under each. With respect to these I asked (1) whether I had joined
together what ought to be separated ; (2) whether I had separated what ought to be joined together ; (3)
whether any caste was omitted from the list. I further gave a list of cases about which I felt doubtful, and
of castes about which I especially needed information. I distributed these circulars widely throughout the
Province to both officials and non-officials who I thought might help me ; and the replies and criticisms 1
received were both numerous and bulky, and in many cases of the greatest possible value. In this manner
I referred for local opinion the classification of every entry numbering not less than 50 souls, and of many
under that number, respecting which I felt any doubt. As for the remaining small entries, still very
numerous, we again went through them one by one in committee ; but this time I accepted any opinion
based on reasonable grounds. But even so the entries which we had in the end to leave unclassified were
still very numerous, though their totals formed a quite insignificant fraction of the population. In the
cases referred to above, where people had returned themselves under a tribal name belonging to several
castes, they were assumed, in the absence of any indication or information to the contrary, to belong to
the landowning caste to which the clan belonged. For instance, almost every menial caste has a Bhatti
clan. But while a Bhatti Rajput would ordinarily describe himself as a Bhatti, leaving you to supply the
conclusion that he was also a Rijput, no menial would ever dream of so returning himself. Consequently.
we were fairly safe in assuming that persons returned as Bhattis were also RAjpiits, and in classing them
as such.
It will be seen that I spared no personal pains in classing my castes. The amount of labour involved in
reading through and making notes of some sixty printed Settlement Reports, in comparing and digesting some
100 replies, many of them very lengthy, in referring to standard works on the subject, and in then coming
to a decision in each case, was greater than will readily be believed. And a perusal of the Chapter on
Castes will show that I am still only too doubtful as to the justness of many of my conclusions. My
guiding principle here, as throughout the Census, has been to exhibit separately all items regarding the propriety of uniting which under a common heading there was room for reasonable doubt. Anyone who cares
to do so can at any time add the figures together ; but once joined in my tables, it would have been impossible to separate them again.
928. Tribal sub-divisions.—In section 914 I have described the method in which the figures for
tribal sub-divisions were tabulated. The number of such sub-divisions for Jats and Rajputs respectively
were 52,380 and 23,925. Of course it was wholly out of the question to exhibit even a small portion of
these clans in our clan tables. Nor indeed was it in any way advisable ; for a large proportion of them
included a quite insignificant number of persons. The first thing to be done, therefore, was to select the
clans which were worthy of separate tabulation. Clerks went through the clan tables for each caste
in each district, and prepared lists showing all clans which reached the number of 1,000 souls in any one
tahsfl of the Province, and opposite each of these the total number for each district in which this condition
was satisfied. Clans not attaining the above standard were neglected. I went through the tables thus
prepared, with a list in my hand, drawn out by myself, of all clans mentioned as being important in any
Panjab Settlement Report ; and selected the clans for which I thought it was worth while to obtain com.

(3) details of over and under 15 years of age, and of simple and combined with agriculture, for all
occupations :
(4) details of resident families :
(5) details of occupied houses, of Jains and Sikhs, and of suburbs, for towns :
(6) tables of sub-divisions of tribes and castes.
The labour involved in Nos. (2) and (6) especially was immense. The sub-divisional tables occupied
the whole compilation office for at the very least six weeks. I was, moreover, unfortunate in my selection
of an Assistant to superintend the Central office. A- man of exceptional mental power and possessing an •
unusually perfect knowledge of English, which was of the greatest possible use to me, he had had no
experience in managing men, and was not well suited for the charge of an effice. And this not only threw
much work which 1 ought to have been spared upon my shoulders, but seriously hindered our general progress. At a future Census the compiling establishment should be trebled at least; and there should be
two officers to assist the Deputy Superintendent, one a first-rate Tahsi'ldar or Superintendent of Settlement
to superintend the vernacular office, and the other an English-speaking Extra Assistant Commissioner who
would act as Personal Assistant and take charge of the translating department.
930. Classification at the next Census.—But at the next Census I hope that the whole work of both
tabulation and compilation, so far at least as it depends upon classification of entries, will be rendered
infinitely easier. The Panjab Government has approved of a proposal that I should edit a Dictionary of
Paniab Occupations. This will include at least all the terms used to describe occupations in the Census
schedules, and will explain the precise meaning of each. I understand that it is proposed to hold a committee after the reports on this Census are complete, in order to draw up a scheme for the classification of
Indian occupations for use on future occasions. But whatever scheme be adopted, the Dictionary will
enable the authorities to class all terms that may be entered in it at once, before the tabulation begins.
All that will then be necessary will be for the tabulating clerk to look out in the classified list each
occupation entered in the abstracts, and to enter against it the serial number of the main occupation-heading
under which it is to be included. The columns of the village tables will be numbered to correspond ; and
the transfer of the figures will be easy. The entries not found in the classified list will be tabulated
separately in supplementary tables, and the Deputy Superintendent will have to treat them as 1 had to
treat the whole of the occupations of this Census. At each Census the unclassed residuum will grow
smaller and smaller ; while the successive editions of the Dictionary will form storehouses of valuable information on a subject about which there is at present very little available in print.
As for castes, Government has sanctioned a small expenditure on the complete tabulation of all the
entries of tribal sub-divisions found in the schedules of the present Census. When the work is complete
the results will be published, together with such information about the several castes and clans as 1 have
meanwhile been able to collect from existing sources, and as I may have received in response to a letter
asking for assistance in the matter which I have circulated throughout the Panjab. The results can hardly
fail to°throw much light upon Panjab Ethnology ; and even if they do not afford material for an ethnical
classification of the peoples of the Province, they will at least render easy the preparation of a classified
list of clans showing to what caste each should be referred, which would be used by the tabulator exactly
in the same manner as the list of occupations referred to in the last paragraph. Whatever form the caste
columns mav take in the schedule of future Censuses, it is quite certain that in numberless instances the
clan will be returned instead of the caste, and that such a list will very largely diminish the labour and
uncertainty which have attended the caste-classification of the present Census.
931. Progress and completion of the work.—The figures were received from the Divisional offices
about the end of July ; and after they had been translated and the Divisional tables issued, and the selected
staff allowed a short rest, the work of compilation was fairly begun about the middle of August. In September and October 1 had to move the office to Amritsar, as the cholera then raging at Lahore so
demoralised the men that they could not work. And here our mistortunes began. In escaping Scylla we
had fallen into Charybdis, for the terrible fever epidemic which decimated the inhabitants of Amritsar laid
the whole office prostrate, and during these two months very little way was made. The compilation in the
vernacular was finished by the end of April, and all the tables save those tor castes and occupations translated and checked. Thus the compilation itself took us more than six months of unremitting toil, besides
two months of slack work. I have already explained in section 929 how enormous was the mass of work
to be got through, how utterly everybody had under-estimated its amount, and how I was in a way unlortunate in the matter of officers. But here again our work was unexpectedly interrupted when nearly over.
W e had, as I have stated, received instructions that the English system of classification of occupations was
to be followed as closely as possible in our tables; and I had accordingly followed it, completed my occupation tables in vernacular, and submitted Indices showing my classification in detail to the Census Commissioner. But just as those tables were about to be translated and checked, in the end of March 1SS2, came a
completely new classification from the Census Commissioner, and I had to group the whole of my 2.975 different occupation headings anew. I spent a week's incessant labour on the new lists, and almost my whole
office was occupied during all April (final orders were not received till 13th April, but we did not wait for
them) in compiling the new Imperial table. Thus 'I was left at the beginning of May with the two heaviest
tables those for castes and occupations, untranslated, and unchecked in English. My vernacular office was
composed of patwSris and qanungos who were urgently needed in their districts, from wliich they had been
absent for a year, and was superintended by an Extra Assistant whose services were required by Government.
I could not keep them waiting till the tables were translated and the English copies checked, in order to
correct any error that might be detected after the translation and check were complete ; and I let them go.
Thus I was left with only a small translating office, and with the whole translation and final check of the
two largest tables on my hands. Moreover they were precisely the two tables in which I found the largest
number of errors due to the uncertainty of vernacular writing. Shoe-makers had been entered as lime4','y

™ E P R E P A B A T I O N ° F ™ E FIGURES.

burners (juta, ckuna), cotton scutchers as cotton merchants (pinjna, bechna), Rors as Aroras or Roras,
Arabs as Banyas [mahajan, muhajarin), Maliirs as Maniars, Barhdisas Bhariis, andathousand similar errors
had been committed. The whole examination of the tables and detection of these errors had to be done
by myself personally, since they were not errors which any mechanical checking of figures would bring to
light, but required some knowledge of the subject to detect them. And when detected they had to be
corrected by a small staff of six clerks. Thus some three months were spent over the examination and
completion of these two tables, and till they were ready my clerks could make but little way with the preparation of the abstracts for the final Report, nor indeed could I make any satisfactory progress with it; and
this because at the very last moment, when the work was done and the establishment about to be broken
up, orders to recompile from the beginning our heaviest table were received from head-quarters.
932. Another reason why my Report has been so long delayed is the extraordinary dilatoriness of the
Lahore Central Gaol Press in printing the final tables. These tables were begun in December 1881 :
a constant supply of copy has been kept up, proofs have never been delayed, and now at the end of February
1883, half the heaviest table, that of occupations, is still unprinted. With such bulky tables as those in
question nothing can be done till they are in print. The number of separate items to be discussed, for which
the figures are given in section 929, added much to my labours; while the various information which we collected in the Panjib only for specially Provincial purposes, and which are detailed in the same section,
greatly increased the whole work of the Census in every stage. During the whole time that I was engaged
on the report I had other matters connected with the Gazetteer to attend to as well. And finally, one main
cause of the delay in finishing my Report has been the utter absence of anything worthy the name of a
Report on any previous Census in the Panjab. Had there been a good Census Report to start with, such as
exists in almost every other province in India, all I should have had to do would have been to compare my
figures, add new facts, and modify previous conclusions. But there was nothing of the sort in existence, and I
had to lay the very foundations as well as build the superstructure. It must not be forgotten in comparing
the Panjib Census with that of other provinces, that in hardly any other part of India was the record of castemade or tabulated in such great detail as here, while in no other province was any attempt made to collect
information regarding tribal sub-divisions. The figures given on page 460 will show how greatly this added
to the labour of tabulation and compilation. I do not think I exaggerate when I say that these figures
alone added five months to the time which elapsed between the taking of the Census and the completion
of the Report.

CHAPTER XIII.

MINOR P O I N T S AND SUGGESTIONS.
PART I.-SUBSIDIARY MATTERS.
933. Legislation; and proceedings under the Act.—The Census was taken under Act XIV of 1880,
the Indian Census Act, the third, fourth, and eleventh sections of which empowered all authorised by
the Local Government in that behalf to appoint any person whomsoever to take the Census of any
specified local area, and rendered him punishable if he refused or neglected to do so. The Act also
provided for punishing householders who did not fill up correctly and return private schedules left at their
nouses for that purpose, and made persons, whether official or otherwise, who were in charge or
command of or had control over bodies of men, or on whose premises considerable numbers were lodged
or employed, responsible for their enumeration. Under this Act the Punjab Government issued
notifications 149, 150, 151, 152, dated n t h January, and No. 396 of 31st January 1881.
These notifications extended the whole Act to the Panjab, appointed myself, all Deputy Commissioners, and all officers in executive charge of sub-divisions and the Agents and Superintendents of the
various Railways in the Province, to be Census officers, delegated to them the power of appointing other
Census officers for the local areas under each respectively, and authorised them to sign declarations
which should be conclusive proof of such appointments. They also empowered all Cantonment
Magistrates and Tahsildars to sign similar declarations for enumerators, supervisors, and superintendents
of Census. They further provided for the hearing of certain classes of cases before Magistrates of the
1st class, and that certain prosecutions under the Act should not be instituted without the sanction of
specified authorities. The declarations of appointment for all subordinate Census officers usually took
the form of a declaration made at the foot of the index-register under the signature of the Tahsildar.
1 think that some such legislative provisions were very necessary. No doubt the mass of the people
did not even know that a Census Act had been passed ; though in some Districts its main provisions
appear to have been publicly notified, while in Gurgaon " its penal provisions were explained to the
" people where necessary, and they were shown that they must answer." But the educated classes who
alone were called on to assist, were probably very generally aware of its existence ; and the Deputy
Commissioner of Sialkot mentions its having had " a most salutary effect," while the Deputy Commissioner of Hushyarpur writes, " the fact of its existence was a salutary stimulant to the staff, though,
" with regard to the outside public, I do not think there was a single instance in which it had even to be
" warned." But it is with respect to the European and Eurasian population that such an Act was especially needed. During the discussion prior to the passing of the Act the Deputy Commissioner of Simla
wrote:—
" In October 1878. when a Census of Simla was taken, the instances were not few in which tho European residents filled
41
nonsense into tlieiv schedules, or where these, though frequently called for, were not filled in and returned to the Census officers,
*' who could only obtain the information after great delay and trouble. Again many European hous.-holders absolutely refused to
" fill in the information for their own servants. There was also great ditficulty in getting proper returns from p»rsonsliving in single
" rooms in Clubs, Hotels, and Boarding Houses, who refused to give the necessary information to the Secretary or Manager tilling
*'in the schedule. There were several instances in which enumerators were assaulted by European or Eurasian clerks and persons
" of that class, "
At the present Census a comparatively small number of private schedules were issued in Simla ; but
in no case was it found necessary to adopt coercive or punitive measures of any sort ; a fact which
seems to show that the provisions of the Act, which were prominently printed on the back of all private
schedules, had a wholesome effect.

in schedules for persons living in their premises or under their control.

enumerator in the city was found to have exacted a fee of two pice (three
farthings) per house, and was sent to prison for three months.

Peshawar (Mr. Lawrence).—" It was fully expected that the Census in this district would be attended with certain diilienlties,
" Following quickly on the close of the war and preceded by three years' continuous drought, the Census found t be j pie in a
" very distressed and anxious condition. Tales were rife as to ttie objects of the Census; ir, wus to collect a tax for paving the
" expenses of the war, or it was to ascertain how man v marringe-ible women there were for the army, Theseantv 11 iudu |>opu" lat.ion in the villages and the large Hindu community in the city generally believed that a poll-lax was impending. These were
"the views of the pessimists: others more sanguine went about saying that the Government intended to provide grain for its
" subjects, and wanted to know how many mouths per family were to be fed.
"The minute nature of the house-lists and the maps of homesteads (which did not previously exisf). and the careful numbering
" of the houses, convinced all that something affecting their interests was about to be done. But when the preliminary leeor 1
"commenced, thePathans saw at once that no tax was to be levied. When they observed that the points most carefully gore- into

" mind has a leaning to historical details of caste, clan, and family, and they soon realised some of the objects of the Census. No
" Patbaii ever gave the name of a woman, and great sati faction was felt at the order forbidding enumerators to ask for women's

936. Correctness Of results,—There is an almost unanimous consensus of all concerned that the
enumeration was performed with remarkable accuracv. Of course there were mistakes and omissions
there must be such in every Census. But I asked Deputy Commissioners to report especially upon th •

475

Paxa. 937. "I
Chap. XIII. J

MINOR P O I N T S AND

SUGGESTIONS.

Part I.—Subsidiary Matters.
extent to which the work had been examined by European or gazetted officers, and from the result of t h e examination I find that an extraordinarily large proportion of the work was tested by superior officers, and
that the general impression left was what I may almost call surprise at its unexpected accuracy. In some
Districts the schedules of nearly every village were inspected by a gazetted officer ; in many the Tahsild£rs examined nearly the whole of the work. Several Deputy Commissioners report that they examined the
work in the most backward and difficult parts of their Districts, in which it had been least supervised,
with especial care, and found it singularly good. Of course the errors in detailed entries, and especially in
respect of age, caste, and occupation, were infinitely more numerous than errors in enumeration. But
even in these respects Deputy Commissioners as a whole seem well satisfied with the work. Accuracy as
to age'is of course unattainable in India, In the matter of occupation the fault of the entries was want of
precision, seldom absolute error: a term was used which was not wrong, but was more vague and indefinite than it need have been. .
The entries of caste, and more especially of sub-divisions of caste, are the ones which are generally
reported to be more unsatisfactory than any others. But I think that this is very largely because Deputy
Commissioners misunderstood the object of the three caste columns. They seem not to have been
satisfied unless all three columns were filled up correctly, whereas in a very large number of cases only
two were intended to be used ; and they apparently did not understand that all we really wanted or
cared for was, the main sub-divisions of each caste, not the secondary branches of those sub-divisions ;
though to make sure of getting the one we had to provide for the entry of the other also. Thus, though
there is no doubt that there are errors in the caste entries, yet I believe that, for our purpose, they were
far more correct than Deputy Commissioners seem to think, and at any rate as correct as could be
expected at a first experiment.
937. As for the mere numbering of the people, I believe it to be marvellously accurate; in the
villages, which include some seven-eighths of the population, probably almost perfect. In the towns,
where the population is more fluctuating, and often more difficult to deal with, errors were no doubt
more frequent. But taking into consideration the fact that in England there is no preliminary record,
that the schedules are in all cases filled in in the first instance by the householders, that more than half
the entire population live in towns of more than 5,000 inhabitants, and that the Englishman is far less
willino- to submit to an inquiry of this sort, and far more likely to show an obstructive temper than the
Indian, I believe it probable that the enumeration of population in the Punjab is at the least as accurate
as that of the English Census. To give in full the opinion of each Deputy Commissioner as to the
correctness of the results would take too much space. I extract the opinions ot a few officers well able to
judge from personal inspection of the nature of the work done.
M r . T . W . S m y t h e of Dehli, who takes one of the least sanguine views, writes :—" There is every reason to believe t h a t the
" r e t u r n s are fairly accurate ; and t h a t t h e errors and omissions are comparatively leu-. The general impression of t h e inspecting
"officers was, that the work was carefully done and t h a t few names were omitted from the schedules. I n the columns showing the
" c a s t e , got, elan ov tribe, and the occupations, some mistakes and e n o r s were diseovere'l. These were owing mure to defective or
'•erroneous information supplied by the heads of families t h a n to neglect or carelessness on the part of the enumerators. Incorrect
" r e p l i e s were not as a rule given for the purpose of misleading, but chiefly t h r o u g h ignorance."
M r . B e n t o n of K a r n a l s a y s : " I think t h a t the Census results may be regarded as a close approximation to the t r u t h . Much
" trouble was t a k e n for a length of time beforehand to impress on all engaged on the work t h e necessity for obtainin<>" correct results. There was hearty co-operation on all hands. The agency wa* t h r o u g h o u t qualified for the task assigned to it,

and was satisfactorily supervised, admirably so I should say, by the tahsil officials who h;td been employed in settlement. I

Part I.—Subsidiary Matters.
" M y own opinion is that generally speaking, considering tbe numb;r of women perhaps not enumerated at all, tlv.se deceased or
"emigrated not struck out, aud those new names not in the preliminary record.errors in escalation n[' units of population must have
" crept in to at least 3 per cent., having regard also to the agency procurable." .Mr. Fox bis not had much administrative experience; and I believe this estimate of error to bean absurdly high one. Excluding the liillv parts of Hazara and the Kanirra
jagirs, where the Census was probably less accurate than in other parts of the Province. I do riot believe the error in tni'mti-ativu
approaches one pereent.; and I believe that even this error is in great measure conlined to females. The errors in details such as
age, caste, occupation, and the like, have already been discussed together with tbe results under each heading.

938. As for the subsequent operation of compiling the figures. I have already described at length the
processes followed and the checks adopted. I believe the error to be considerably less than tin error
inherent in the material. The total numbers have been compiled with almost absoli'tc accuracy. And ir:
the details I believe the errors to be wholly insignificant, save in so far as my judgment may have erred
in classifying the entries. At the same time 1 have only just come across an instance of how a very large
and important error may be honestly made and yet escape detection. In correcting the proof.-- of the
language table I was surprised to find that 1,507 females and only 397 males were shown in the Kiingra
district as speaking Tibeti. On examining the compilation slip. 1 found that the original entries were
correct ; but in adding up the Pahari-speaking males the total had been made 1,000 too large. e\ identh hv
a perfectly genuine mistake ; while the 1 of the 1.35S males speaking Tibeti in Spiti had been written over
the line of the column, and so escaped notice. Thus the total lor Tibeti-speaking males was as much too
small as that the PahAri-speaking males was too large ; and the total males added up rightly, and so the error
escaped detection till it caught my eye after it had actually got into type. I can only say that no labour habeen spared to avoid such mistakes, and that 1 believe very few to have escaped uncorrected. There ione sort of error, however, which can hardly have been otherwise than frequent; and that is error due to
the extraordinary uncertainty of the vernacular cursive. As far as was possible we compiled all our figures
by men well acquainted with the district to which they referred; and they were always examined alter
compilation bv such men. But there must have been hundreds of cases in which an unfamiliar term wamistaken for a familiar term and wrongly included under it. Some of these I have noticed in mv discussion of the figures, and many of them I detected in my personal examination of the tables. At the -ame
time this sort of error will have affected two tables only, those of caste ami occupation ; and the error will
be almost wholly confined to small items, the larger and more important castes and occupation.- being
sufficiently well known to the staff. In one case my Native Extra Assistant Commissioner, who was readino- over a classified list of occupations with me, was obliged to confess with shame that by some groscarelessness boot-makers had been included in the priestly class ; until a reference to the statement shoved
that the word that both he and I had read bootmaker in the vernacular, was really a local name for a class of
Brahmans. And even when the final occupation tables came up to me for examination before going to
press, I found that many thousands of lime-burners had been returned for a certain district, who were on
examination found to be entered as leather-workers and not lime-burners at all.
939. Progress reports,—Weekly progress reports were submitted to the Deputy Commissioners
by the Census staff throughout each stage of the operations ; and similar reports were sent to me
from each district every fortnight. In order to enable them to prepare these reports, Census officials
were instructed to keep up diaries in which the details of each day's work, notes of inspection, instructions
given on the spot by supervising officers, and the like, were to be entered (see Appendix D, General
Letter B).
On this subject Mr. Stogdon writes :—
" I t h i n k returns of the n a t u r e of Census diaries should not be required, They are of no practical value, and the probability is
" t h a t the majority of the entries in t h e m are fudged. I do not sec much use in the weekly progress vcpoit*. T h e ,nv not triist" worthy and do not conduce to the progress of the work-. Every one knows t h a t the Census lai< to be taken 011 a c a ; , , m ,tav, a i d all
" parties concerned will do their u t m o s t to have everything in readiness by that day, whether they keep Cetu-iis diaucs and submit
" weekly progress reports or not."

I think perhaps the diaries were unnecessary.
The idea was taken from settlement experience ;
but the Census work is of so temporary a nature that possibly the diaries give mere trouble than
they are worth. But the progress reports seem to me to be absolutely necessary. The Deputy Superintendent is responsible that the work is done up to time, and that all preparations are complete
before the night of the final Census. If Government is of opinion that this may safely lie left to
the local officers, it would save the Deputy Superintendent an infinity of harassing anxiety.
Hut 1
think that it would not be safe in all cases to leave matters so, and I believe that the neces-ilv for
reporting progress weekly stimulates subordinates by creating a rivalry among them ; while 1 cannot conceive how a Deputy Commissioner is to know what is doing in his District, or to be- answerable lor the
proper progress of the work, unless he receives something of the sort trom all parts of his Di-trict.
I would circulate to all tahsils of the district a lorm showing the progress made in each, so as to encourage this rivalry. This plan I adopted with my Divisional offices, and found it bail a wonderful effect
on the work.
940. Inspection.— I have already noted (section 936! how- very large a portion of the work was aclii.illv
exatnined on the spot by superior officers. The gazetted sta.t of most districts wa- in camp almost
throughout the operations, inspecting, instructing, explaining, and encouraging. 1 mv-elf made lours of
inspection through the Province, in the course of which I examined the work in the following Districts;
Dehli, Gurgaon, Karnal, Ambada, Liidhianah, J.ilandhar, Husky.irpur, Amril-ar, Gurda-pur. Si.ilkot, Lahore.
GujrSnwala, Rdwal Pindi, Jahlam, Gtijr.it, Multaii, Montgomery, Muzaffargarh, Pcsh.iwar. Mr. Kan-haw e.
Revenue Under-Secretary to Government, while on tour with His Honour, inspected riiavpur.Sir-a,I liss.ir,
and Rohtak. At each place I had the index-register and a lew hou-e-lists and completed schedules
brought in from each tahsil, examined them, and pointed out errors. A large part of the supervising staff
was collected to meet me at head-quarters, and I briefly explained to them all points, and specially those
regarding the filling-up of the schedules, on which it seemed to me that doubt was possible, and eticouraeed
them to ask questions and propound difficulties for solution. I confess that I had been very sceptical as to

It will be seen that the number used per 1,000 of population varies enormously in different districts.
This is partly d^e^to the fact that, while in most Districts the more incompetent enumerators made a
rough preliminary record and had it corrected by the supervisors before they copied it into their books,
i n some Districts this was written on schedules, and in others on paper ruled by hand. But there is no
doubt whatever that in some Districts the indents were absurdly large and there was great waste.
Many of the enumerators' books'had 10. or 20 unused blank schedules at the end, and thousands of spare
schedules were in some cases sent to the Divisional o85ce after the Census to be used as blank paper.
In many Districts, however^ the Deputy Commissioners complain that the supply was insuQBcient. I think
that in future a very liberal margin should be allowed for waste; far more liberal than was generally
provided by Deputy Commissioners in their indents for this Census. I think that the supply should be
nearly if not quite double tha!t which would b e needed if all enumerators wrote closely and neatly, and
there was no w^aste- It is better that rough copies, if they are to be made, should be made on the printed
fechifedule, as the enumerators • thus grow accustomed to its use, and are saved both time and trouble,
while the cost of the extra schedules is quite insignificant; and above all things the schedules should be
distributed as early as possible, and every effort should be made to estimate and supply the deficiency,
if any, at as early d a t e ' a s possible^. At this Census District officers seemed to think that schedules
grew at Lahore; and I received emergent indents for thousands of schedules up to within a few days
of the actual Census,
947* Mr-. Steedman has the fpUoWing retnarks on the subject:—
*' I^ative suboi'dimates sejkd m the Vildfes^t indents. The oiily way to keep them in check is to require explanations of how the
" forms previously supplied liave beea expeiided- A short time before the final enumeration I scut a circular round the t^^hsiU
,** iiskiiig the BuperiutendeBts |i<^ se^d i^^ iii all surplus fprrtis, and pointing out that according to the number supplied, the population
*^'«0OOi?^
surplus. I believe this went along way
towards stopping unreasonable eniergent indents at the last/*

This is very t r u e ; but care must be taken not to make the officers feel that they may not use
schedules for such purposes as rough copies and the like, or that there need be any hesitation about re*
placing a scheclule which has been badly cut up'by corrections. T h e relative competence of enumerators
is almost as important a factor in deciding the number of schedules required as is the population to be
etlumerated. Where other characters than Persian are. used, two copies, or an interlinear biglot copy
occupying double space, must be made, and the practice of preparing a rough copy seems to have been
very general. Mr. Tolbort of Ambala says : '* At the commencement of this stage of the Census proceed'* ings I endeavoured to check the pr^cticfe of preparing a rough record first and fairing it afterwards, but
/**tbe general consensus of opinion in favour of the said practice was so strong that I considered it unwise
**to persist in opposing^ it, I do not think that it led to ^farzV procedure;'' and most Deputy Comimssioners agree with hini,^ though Mr, Wilson of Sirsa pirfefers the original as being more trustworthy.

r Para. 951.
L Chap. XIII.

Part II.—Census in Native States

PART II.-CENSUS IN NATIVE STATES.
950. Form of schedule for Native States.—The Government of India, when determining to extend
the Census operations to the Feudatory States with the exception of Kashmir, proposed to "draw the
" broadest distinction between the enumeration in British and Foreign Territory. The results in the
" latter case will'necessarily possess mnch less completeness and accuracy, and we should not expec t
" them to conform to any uniform standard." The Committee of 1877 had recommended that nothing
more than an enumeration by sex should be attempted, and the Government of India proposed a simplified form of schedule.
But the Panjab Government preferred to adopt the same schedule in the Native States subordinate
to it as had been prescribed for British Territory. It was of opinion that they were probably more advanced than the majority of similar States in India; that they had always imitated and adopted our
administrative system in its main outlines ; that they were fully competent to conduct their Census on the
same lines as our own ; and that they would feel it as a slight if a doubt were cast upon their capacitv to
do so by the suggestion of a simpler schedule for their use. The schedule prescribed for British Territory
was sent to the various Chiefs, and they were asked if they thought that difficulty would be experienced in
collecting any of the information therein detailed. They one and all professed their readiness to take a
Census of their territories in precisely the same manner as that of British India, the only doubt expressed
being by the Bahawalpur Darbar, which questioned the advisability of recording the age of females.
Accordingly the general schedule and all instructions issued by me for the guidance of British officers were
supplied to the Governments of Native States, who took their Census precisely as we took ours.
951. N a t u r e of w o r k in Native States.—The result has most fully justified the decision of the
Panjdb Government, and I believe that, as regards the greater number of the States at anv rate, tlieir
results are quite as accurate as our own. In most of them a special officer was appointed to superintend
the operations: in some of them, as Pataudi, Loharu, and Dujana, officials were sent to our Districts to
study the system ; and all the larger ones, with the single exception of Bahawalpur, remained in constant
correspondence with me, sent me specimens of their papers to examine and crillcise, referred doubtful
points for my decision, constantly kept me informed of their progress, and even sent men to Lahore to
consult me personally on difficulties which had arisen. Some peculiarities there doubtless were. In
Patiala a third column for eunuchs was added to the columns for males and females ; and in this and
perhaps in other States the Maharaja and his immediate family were excepted altogether from enumeration. But on the whole 1 believe the work was almost, and in most of the States of the Eastern Plains
quite as well done as in British Territory.
In Loharu and DujAna great difficulty was experienced in obtaining competent agency for the final
enumeration; but the Commissioner Colonel Minchin, wdio took great interest in the work, satisfied
himself that the preliminary record, which after all is the backbone of the Census, was thoroughly satisfactory. In Pataudi Major Young sent an official to be trained at Gurgaon, and exercised an active
supervision throughout; and the Census papers which were sent to me to examine from this State were
admirably prepared. In Faridkot Mr. Fanshawe examined the work on the spot, and the papers which
were sent me were very well done: Mr. Macnabb saw some of the work in Kalsia, and expressed himself
well satisfied with it. Of the work in Bahawalpur and Maler Kotla I know nothing. But of.Patiala, Nabha,
and Jind I can say from personal knowledge that the very greatest interest was taken throughout in the
operations, that no pains were spared to secure accuracy, and that, so far as it is possible to judge from
outside, the results were in every way admirable. The officials to whom the Census of these States was
entrusted were men of great intelligence and ability; they took the greatest interest in the work, spared
themselves no labour, and were in constant communication with myself on all sorts of difficult or doubtful
questions.
In the Hill States greater difficulties were experienced, as might indeed have been expected ; and
in some of the wilder tracts the same special license had to be allowed as was taken of necessity in similar
portions of our own territory, and has been described in sections 884, SS5. In Chamba the operations were
superintended throughout by Major Marshall, who tested much of the work on the spot and found it
thoroughly and accurately done. In Suket the earlier work was capitally done under the superintendence
of the Superintendent Munshi Hardial Singh, an official of our Government, Colonel Davies the Commissioner exercising an active supervision. In Mandi Colonel Davies had practically to take the whole
matter into his own hands, as the Raja, while only too glad to have the work done for him, and offering
no' sort of obstruction, would not himself move in the matter. Accordingly Colonel Davies obtained a
trained staff of supervisors from Jalandhar and Hushyarpur and put Munshi Nathu Ram, one of our
Superintendents of Settlement, in charge of the Mandi Census, with the result that the work was well
done in the end
But it was done quite at the- last moment ; and it is wholly due to Colonel Davies'
energy that a Census was taken at all in Mandi. In the Simla Hill States the Superintendent Major Nisbet
summoned an official from each State to be trained at Simla, and throughout exercised the most active
supervision! It was found impossible, both here and in the higher portions of Chamba and .Mandi,
to divide the country into blocks, as indeed it had been found in Kulu ; but enumerators were appointed
to each hamlet or village, and Major Nisbet, who marched through a great portion of the country
under his superintendence while operations were in progress, found that the enumeration was accurately
done.
There is no doubt that, in the Hill States, as in the hilly parts of British Territory, more than
ordinary difficulty was experienced in obtaining competent agency ; and if they alone were to be consi40 I

S S D SHll'l

MINOR POINTS AND SUGGESTIONS,

Part II,—Census in Native States.
dered, a m o r e simple schedule would, I think, be advisable. But they include but a small fraction of the
population of the Panjdb Native States, and it would be a pity to destroy the uniformity of the whole
Census by making an exception in their favour.
952. Kapurthala Census incomplete.—In one State only, and that a State under the superintendence
of an English officer, was the scheme of enumeration which was followed without other exception throughout the length and breadth of the Panjdb abandoned. In Kapurthala the Superintendent directed that no
religious sects should be recorded in the column provided for that purpose, that one of the two columns
for tribal sub-divisions should be omitted altogether from the schedule, and that the other should be filled
up for four castes only. The consequence of this action, which was not discovered till long after the
Census was over, when the final tables were sent to my office, has been to destroy the value and completeness of our Tables in two not unimportant points. In all other respects I believe the Kapurthala Census
work to have been admirable.
953. Tabulation in Native States.—The abstracting and tabulating the figures was done by each
State independently, with the exception of the Simla Hill States, which sent clerks to Simla where they
abstracted the figures under the supervision of the Superintendent. Nahan, however, was excepted from
this arrangement, and allowed to tabulate its own figures. Immediately after the Census was over I invited
the Native States to send their principal Census officers to meet me at Lahore. There I fully described to
them in Committee the scheme to be followed in the tabulation of the figures. I think that, on a lutiire
occasion, it would be well to collect these officers at a similar meeting before work is commenced, to
explain to them the general plan of Census operations. A little verbal explanation is better than much
written precept; and I am sure that the States would respond cordially to such an invitation.

Part III.—Difficulties and Suggestions.

PART III.—DIFFICULTIES AND SUGGESTIONS.
954. Difficulties and suggestions.—In writing this Report I have attempted throughout to keep
clearly in view the fact that this is not the last Census which will be taken in the Pan jab, an< I to leave on
record as far as possible all experience that we have gained on this occasion for guidance in future
enumerations. It can hardly be conceived by those who have never attempted the task, how much careful
though was needed and what countless minute points had to be considered in order to makt the different
parts o the machinery work smoothly and without jar or hitch, and to secure the measure of S t r e s s which
has attended the present operations. So far as the Census itself and the subsequent abstr actino of the
figures are concerned, all went far more smoothly than I had ventured to hope for. But in he hnal compilation and tabulation we broke down lamentably, for reasons which I have already stated in lull. Still,
even \v lere things went best the arrangements might have improved. Many suggestions for t heir iniprcA cmerit will be found scattered through the preceding chapters of the Report ; and 1 wish her • to add .such
further suggestions as may occur to me, and to collect the references to the scattered sugges lions alrcad\

Part III.—Difficulties and Suggestions.
T h e importance of t h e accurate e n t r y of subsidiary p a r t s has already been insisted on (section 835). Walls, however, should n o t
be entered (section 842). I t is essential to get the column defining the subsidiary parts next to the columns defining Hie block, or
i t will never be known in which block each subsidiary p a r t is included. When this form is complete, I still t h i n k copies should be
sent t o t h e s u p e r i n t e n d e n t and tahsildai', and t h a t a general index-register for the tahsil should be drawn u p in this modified form. N o t
only will t h e Tahsilddr require some such index to the a r r a n g e m e n t s , but, what is fur more i m p o r t a n t , the whole preparation of the
t a h s i l abstracts (section 890), the whole grouping of the blocks in the village tables prepared by the Divisional offices, and the whole
check upon the completeness of t h e Census schedules as sent to those offices depends upon the correctness of t i n s index-register.
T h e greatest difficulty was caused by alterations made at the last moment in t h e a r r a n g e m e n t of the blocks not having always been
incorporated in t h e index-register. T h i s form must be sent in an accurate a n d complete state to t h e Divisional offices, tor they
h a v e no other guide in tabulation. I t m i g h t perhaps be worth while to p r i n t blank forms of index-register on a future occasion.
Preparation
of house-maps.—The
importance of those maps and the best way of preparing them are discussed in section 8.^7.
I n hill t r a c t s and also in t h e M u l t a n and I)eraja*t Divisions —wherever in fact scattered hamlets are common—they are absolutely
essential. The following suggestion of Mr. Gardiner seems a good one : " I think every Municipality should We made to ptnvide
' ' i t s e l f with a m a p of its town a n d to sub-divide the whole area of the latter into muhallas or streets and give a name to ea<di locality
" a n d a n u m b e r to each house. This would greatly facilitate any future enumeration, and is required for t h e purposes of municipal
" a r r a n g e m e n t s , the delivery of letters, and the general administration. The country is quite civilised enough to appreciate such an
" i m p r o v e m e n t , a n d the sooner it is p u t into force t h e b e t t e r . " (See also Mr. Stogdon's opinion quoted in section 837).
Souse-numbering—should
be down-hill in billy tracts (section 836). T h e n u m b e r s are best affixed as indicated in section 856.
The series of n u m b e r s should be continuous throughout the circle or vilhige, whichever is larger (section 83O). Wells and ouil\ iug
h u t s in t h e held should n o t be indiscriminately entered (sections 8 4 1 , 84.2).
Definition of house and family.—This
is fully discussed in sections 838, 839, and proposals made in section S.p>. The existence of a hearth is perhaps the best test of whether a building is a house or not (section 841). A family should be defined and
translated as suggested in section 843. A difficulty was felt in H u s h y a r p u r in w h a t was said to he the not uncommon case of one
family occupying two houses. W h o then was to be shown as the head of the second house? I think ihe column m i ^ h t he filled np

and all families so entered omitted in the statistics for families, which should (section ^44)
be taken from t h e house-lists, not from t h e schedules. The following suggestion by Mr. Gardiner will I think he met by my proposals
in section 8 4 0 : " The definitions of a house a n d - o f a family are now understood, and any alteration of these on a future oera^ion
" w o u l d only complicate m a t t e r s more. B u t w h a t I t h i n k is required is the localisation of ihe families in a house ; under the present
" a r r a n g e m e n t as the families in a house are entered in no sort of order, there is no way of t e s t i n g readily whether a family has been
" o m i t t e d . Roams m i g h t be numbered in some system from left to r i g h t as a bullock walks at a Persian wheel, ami from the bottom
'* t o the top of a house ; and enumerators should b - encouraged t o enter buildings of any sort in their schedules (as after ;tll the

of urban limits.—This
should be made most carefully on the lines indicated in sections $^(> and <)i 1.
Forms for house-lists,—I
r a t h e r t h i n k 1 would p r i n t and distribute blank forms for house-lists ••** a future Census.
It is. well
t o leave as. little avoidable work to be done in the districts and to rendei the duties of the stall' a- simple and easy as possible.
956. A g e n c y . — T h e matter of agency is t h e real difficulty in a F a n j a b C e n s u s ; and though with the spread of education the
difficulty will gradually decrease, yet I fear several enumerations will he made before it wholly disappears. The greatest difficulty
was" experienced in the backward tracts, such as the Men country in Gurgaon. and the Sirsa district, in the hills generally, on the
frontier, and in the g r e a t scantily peopled areas of t h e Derajat and ihe bar districts. T h e question of agency is tally discussed in
sections 849 to 855.
I t is always questionable whether it is not better to work with an interior agency which knows the people, than with an inipnrte l agency to whom they and their ways are unfamiliar. T h e increase in the aveiage size of blocks suggested above will partly
diminish the difficulty. I do not think the general scheme of agency could be improved- The supervisors are the backbone ••[' the
.whole scheme, and should be most carefully selected from the very best men available. Many officers are -of opinion I hat the superint e n d e n t s are useless and unnecessary, while others attach the greatest importance to t h e m . I quote below opinions I Venn Peshawar
and L a h o r e : —
P e s h a w a r . — " T h e supervisor was the m a n who really did all the work. Tin' superintendent was too often a mere name.
" I would suggest t h a t a t a future Census, the duties of t h e superintendent be entrusted to the Tah-tldar and Qanungo.
Tne
" patwaris of the district, aided by the lain bar da rs, could perform the duties of enumerators and of supei visors, as they he.ee done
" i n this Census,"
L a h o r e . — " I n m y oninion t h e post of superintendent is not a very i m p o r t a n t one. The important point is to secure ^uod
" s u p e r v i s o r s with manageable charges. If sufficient lime is given for Cersus operation-, the Tuhsildar. X>;.ih Tab-iMar, and As-isiant
"•Commissioner or E x t r a Assistant Commissioner in charge of the Tahsil can effectually control and check their uperatinns. If the
'" best men are appointed superintendents, it is difficult to obtain a sufficient number of efficient supervisors, and a Census cannot, he
" p r o p e r l y conducted. I think t h a t t h e post of superintendent m i ^ h t be considered an honorary one to which /.aildais and men
" o f note in t h e district should be appointed. They would always be able to render very effective aid in m a k i n g a r r a n g e m e n t s and
"collecting the people, and impressing upon t h e m the necessity of g i v i n g correct information and of t i m b e r i n g the w-rk of
" supervisors and enumerators to the utmost of their power ; h u t the actual control of the work of the supervisors can best be |>ci formed
" bv responsible officials of experience, who are more likely t o work in an intelligent and uniform manner than inexperienced
" landholders, however influential and well-meaning they may be. The duties of a, supervisor are of a most important nature, and
" it is on them t h a t t h e success or otherwise of a Census principally depends."
On t h e other hand Mr. Coldstream in H u s h y a r p u r added intermediate officers between supervisors and superintendents for the
fiial Census, employing for the purpose every man of position and available person 111 the district, in both towns and village-. Some
of t h e m rode 20 01-30 miles on t h e Census night. The mere fact of their appointment stirred up the enumerators and m u l e them
afraid to shirk their work ; and for this purpose a zaihhir who could not read was almost as useful as one who could. I should be
inclined at a future Census to appoint all the best educated men I could get as supervisors ; and \<< appoint, as superintendents all
t h e leading headmen, whether illiterate or otherwise, m a k i n g it their d u t y t o constantly visit their circles, stir up t h e enumerators,
listen to complaints, and smooth away difficulties, rather t h a n to supervise the actual entries, which tusk m i - h t be left to the supervisors.
M r . Coldstream points o u t the admirable effect of associating the n a t u r a l leaders of the people with the operations, both in m a k i n g
t h e people careful and willing t o give correct information, and in allaying their fear and suspicion-*. I think the supervising stall'
should be slightly larger than it was at this Census, especially if t h e patwaris are to make the preliminary record ; and mo-t of all
i n t h e sparsely inhabited tracts, where t h e distances to he traversed arc g r e a t and where t h e intelligence of the enumerators is
g-enerally small. B u t if illiterate men are utilised as superintendents this will increase the number of educated men available as
supervisors. I t h i n k (sections 49 to 52 and 854) t h a t non-official agency should not he paid ; hut ivhciievrr they are sent to a
distance from t h e i r homes, I would give t h e m a small sum to cover actual expenses. I would avoid the use of \ a ' g r i and Mnhaj.mi
writers as much as possible, even if the only alternative were to utilise school-hoys as amanuenses (sections S55. S.-;(>). Where their
use cannot he avoided, t h e best plan of transliteration is iudicaicd in secti in 856. I would issue a second (government, circular to
heatU-of departments shortly before the Census as suggested in section S50, and would impress upon Deputy Commissioners the
importance of m e e t i n g the convenience of the start lent t h e m by arrangements such as are described in section S50. I would make
t h e preliminary record of villages entirely by patwaris (sectious 8 5 1 , 852).
957. T h e form of and entries in t h e S c h e d u l e s and the e n u m e r a t o r s ' b o o k s . — I would put. the name of the village on the label
(section 858), omit the name of the "tahsil from the schedule (section 858), and add a column for the number and kind of house
instead of writing it across the page (section 859). T h e bindings for the books should he made in t h e district, sample books
being sent them to copy (section 860]. Each village or urban suburb should have a separate book, even if it form only part of
a block (section 860). Many officers point out that, the printed character used is almost illegible to the people, and t h a t the headings
should be lithographed. This is impossible if the headings are to be repeated on every schedule. I t is suggested however t h a t a
single lithographed heading should be supplied for each book, and the schedules consist of blank paper ruled by hand by ihe enumer-

Column 2.—I would add the relationship of the woman or child to the head of the house where the name was not given

Column 3.—I would give instructions that unmarried prostitutes are to be entered as single (kwdri, section 859). The
question whether marriage or cohabitation (mukldioa) should be taken as the point to be recoried is an important one (section 681).

Column 5.—I would enter the age at the next, not at the last birthday (section 859).

nearer the native idiom.
Column g.—I would direct in so many words that the name of a village should never be entered (section 905).
Column 10.~\ would have the heading of the column " Occupation " only (section 859}, and would enter the occupation <>f
nobody under 15 jearB old (section 718). I would direct that under no circumstances should the name of a caste be shown in
this column, but that if for instance the man was a Teli by caste and an oilman by trade, the words tel nifcdfna should be entered.
Also that a woman should not be entered as of the same occupation as her husband unless she actually helped him in his work. I
would I think distinguish between proprietors who cultivated with their own hands and those who did not, and 1 would direct that
the sons of landowners were to be shown as landowners, not merely as cultivators (sections 726 and 774).
Column Ji.—Very many officers have suggested that the character which the man can read and write should be shown. If
so, two columns would be needed, oue for " learning " and the other for " educated," and the name of the character would be
entered in the proper column. The information would certainly be valuable (section 807). ft' a man wrote two characters he migl.t
be left to select the one to be entered. The point that children who are learning and men who have learnt to read only and not to
write are not to be entered, should be emphasized (section 807).
958. Preliminary Record and final enumeration.—The preliminary record of the villages should be made almost wholly by the
patw&is (sections 851, 852). The date of beginning should be as late as is safe (section 862). I would forbid a rough Census being
made beforehand a8 practice for the enumerators (section 861). In fixing the date of the Census I would avoid lagans (section
863), and if fairs cannot bo avoided, would take the day of the fair instead of the day before or after (section 863). If statistics for
families are to be taken from the schedules, and not from the house-lists, which I should prefer (section 844), I would separate
families entered for the first time on the final night (section 864). In no case would I enter temporary visitors in the preliminary
record (section 862), and I would perhaps show people who were temporarily absent in order to arrive at the permanent population
(section 865). A more difficult point is the question where people temporarily sleeping out of their houses at night are to be enumerated. It was found that some men absent for the night only at their shops or in their fields were cut out at the final Census.
though this was contrary to the instructions. I do not see how to improve the rule. Men absent at their shops could easily be enumerated there, but that would be impossible in the case of men watching in the fields ; and yet we must I think have one general
rule for all similar cases. I think the rule must be left as it is ; but the point might be emphasized thus : " But you must not cut
" out those who are merely absent, &c." And the special attention of enumerators and supervisors should be directed to this point
before the final Census.
959. Special measures.—The responsibility of Cantonment Magistrates for the Census of cantonments should be not only
defined but enforced (section 867). Military lines should be treated as blocks (section 869). The lists of houses to which private
schedules are to be granted should be made early in the operations (section 872). But there seems no necessity for giving them to
natives at all (section 873). The European form of schedule should be recast, and separate columns substituted for foot-notes
(section 875); and they should all be examined by a capable official in the district, and made really correct and complete before
being Bent to the compiling offices (section 875). Railway premises should be treated as circles (section 876) and house-lists
prepared for them in large Railway centres (section 876). River turnpikes should be instituted (section 879). Tickets fur
travellers appear to be unnecessary (section 881). It is mosMmpnrtant to avoid transfer of officers, and especially of Tahsildars.
when the Census work has once been begun, till it is completely finished. c*ome remarks on the difficulties of a synchronous
Census in mountain tracts will be found in section 885.

960. Discretion to be allowed in enumeration.—One great difficulty is the degree of discretion to
be allowed to the enumerators and supervising staff in rejecting the answers given by the people and
recording what, they believe to be the truth. The subject has been noticed already in sections 32 and 37
of Chapter I. About age there can I think be no doubt. Discretion must here be exercised, for the man
himself often does not know his own age. The really difficult columns are those for caste and occupation.
Here I think that where the enumerator is the village accountant, who is personally acquainted with every
man,woman, and child in the village, he may be trusted to make the right entries. What is wanted is the
caste to which the man is ordinarily reputed to belong; not any theory of ancestral origin which he or his
fellow castemen may have formed. One thing I feel quite positive about, and that is that there must be
no "aiming at uniformity." I have already quoted in section 37 an example of the pernicious effect? of such
attempts. The same officer, a highly educated Native Extra Assistant Commissioner, writes: " T o avoid
" a description of the same occupation by a variety of names, a general list of occupations was issued, and
" t h e enumerators directed to make a special report of every other occupation they had to enter. Similar
" lists of the tribes and clans met with in the tahsil were issued, and everything practicable done to secure
"uniformity of results." No possible action coujd have been so eminently adapted as this to render the
results of the Census absolutely worthless. There is no officer in the Panjab, English or Native, who could
draw up even a tolerably complete list of the occupations, tribes, and clans of a district or even of a single
tahsil; and the result of issuing such a list authoritatively naturally was, as it always would be, that some one
of the entries in the list was made to fit every soul enumerated. Besides, it is useless securing one kind of
uniformity in one district and another kind of uniformity in the next. What we want is variety.
The greater
diversity of entry, the larger our stock of information. Variations and discrepancies will set us thinking
and inquiring. Such uniformity as is possible must be arrived at by a careful classification of the entries on one
system in the central office, and not by forcing the entries into a predetermined mould. Instructions on the
subject of what is and what is not to be entered cannot be too general; for not only are we without even a hundredth part of the knowledge necessary to enable us to enter into details effectively, but bv attempting to do
so we deprive ourselves of the very possibility of acquiring that knowjedge.
If we show the slightest preference for one entry rather than another, the natives will give us that entry, right or wrong. W e cannot

962. T h e t a b u l a t i o n of t h e figures.—The subject is fully discussed in Part I I . of Chapter X I I . I do not think the general

the suggestions conveyed in section 904, a n d t o tabulate male a n d female occupations in t h e same t a b l e ; for though their occupations differ somewhat, y e t it is surprising how little dilference there is on the whole, and the loss of the check afforded hv the double
columns for sex is irreparable. I n deciding w h a t places to class as towns I think I would follow the strict numerical l i m i t ; that
is t o say, I would class no places of less t h a n 5,000 souls as towns unless they weie either Municipalities or Ci\il or Militarv
Stations (section 909). Of course I would exclude, as has been done in this Census, populations widely scattered over Luge village
areas. A r r a n g e m e n t s for urban areas are suggested in section 9 1 1 . The question of the village units in the Krilu sub-division
should be considered (section 910) ; b u t I fear a n y further sub-division would be impracticable, as Mr. Anderson tells me that t h e

All t h a t is wanted is t h e total population of each hamlet, and that perhaps might be taken out separately (see section 9K1). T h e
English schedules should certainly be wholly dealt with a t a central office (section 913J. Indeed they should, after examination at
t h e district head-quarters (section 875), be sent s t r a i g h t t o t h a t office. T h e Census once t a k e n , t h e European figures should be kepi
quite distinct t h r o u g h o u t . I t h i n k t h a t t h e totals for each village should not be filled i.ito the vi'lage tables till the check is
complete. A t t h e late Census we filled t h e m in a t once in red ink. I doubt if they are really necessary at all except for the t o w n s ; for
few villages can contain more t h a n five or six blocks, the total of which can easily be given when required. At any rate the having
two independent sets of figures on the same page, one only of which is to bo added up in order to get the page totals, is very
confusing ; a n d t h e second set should not be added until t h e table is completely cheeked, totalled, and corrected.

district and send them into t h e central office before almost they begin another. I am not sure t h a t I would not make them send
