I n d i a m i g h t perhaps be begged to fix t h e forms finally in good time. Changes in the middle of the woik not only lead to great
delay b u t make error and fudging much more probable. I t takes t h e heart out of an office to have to do work over again from m.
fault of its own (section 9 1 8 ) .
963. Compilation of t h e figures.—At a future Census t h e existence of t h e classified lists of castes and occupations mentioned
in section 930 should immensely reduce t h e work of compilation, as the great mass of the entries will then he classified during
tabulation instead of during compilation (section 930). Still, the great error I made was to take too small a staff for this work, and
it should n o t be repeated. Above all, a really good active Superintendent of Settlement should be appointed to superintend the office,
even if this prevents a n y English-speaking Assistant being given t o t h e Deputy Superintendent
T h e real work is done in the
vernacular office, a n d i t is there t h a t driving power a n d system are wanted (section 920). Above all things the vernacular office should
never be finally broken u p till every single table has been translated, checked, and panned for the jircss in English.
For tinconsequences of neglecting t h i s rule see section 931. I think t h e compiling staff must be patwaris selected as on this occasion
(section 920). N o other men can deal with the local terms.
964. I s s u e of instructions a n d s c h e d u l e s . —Suggestions regarding t h e supply of schedules will he found in section 946. The
discussion of arrangements a n d instructions by Deputy Commissioners in Committee with all their Tahsfldars, mid as many of the
supervising staff as they can collect, is exceedingly
important (section 040). A t the same time it should be explained that
" uniformity " is n o t wished for in the entries (section 960). I think t h a t the Deputy Superintendent's tour of inspection is most
valuable (section 940). By next Census he will be able to reach almost every district in the Province by rail. I would issue
translations of all instructions with t h e instructions on a future occasion (section 943b The schedules must I think he printed at
Calcutta unless lithographed headings for each book he adopted (sections 944 and 957). If printed at Calcutta they might be distributed from there by rail (section 945). Above all t h i n g s both schedules and instructions should be distributed at as early a date as
possible. T h e complaints regarding late receipt of these papers a t t h e present Census were numerous. B u t we did not fix on our
form of schedule till t h e end of A u g u s t , and did not receive t h e whole supply ordered from Calcutta till J a n u a r y . The schedule form
should be fixed upon long before a n y sort of-Census work is really begun in the districts, or even anv instructions issued ; and t h e
schedules should be printed a t once at Calcutta. T h e stock of schedules should be complete and ready for distribution t o districts,

distributed, hy the middle of November at the very latest. I t must be remembered that it takes a long time to
reach parts of the Province from Lahore. Parts of Kulu are 16 stages from Dharmsala over passes 10,000 feet high and often
closed in winter. So with the instructions. On the present occasion the great delay in the issue of orders by the Government of
India regarding the Railway and Military Census, and the alteration of the instructions regarding occupations in the middle of
November, caused much trouble, delay, and confusion. But on a future occasion it ought to be possible to issue complete instructions
for the whole operations at once, before work is begun at all. This would be the greatest possible boon to district officers, for men work
at once more wiUingly and more intelligently if they know exactly what their work is to lead to. The complete instructions ought to be
in the hands of Deputy Commissioners by the heginning of September if possible; for the first arrangements for demarcation of
circles, and especially the selection of agency, give a great deal of trouble and take a great deal of time. The house-maps and houselists could be prepared in November and December, and the preliminary record in January. Done in this way at leisure, I do
not see why the Census work should anywhere prevent the preparation of the usual annual papers ; and it certainly would be
possible to test and check it more effectually than if the work were all crowded into the last tew weeks. The only difficulty that
I see arises from the fact that the issue of complete instructions by the 1st of September, and the completion of the printing of
the schedules by the beginning of November, involve the appointment of a Deputy Superintendent of Census from the beginning
of July at the latest. But when he had once got his forms and instructions complete, that is to say from the end of August, he
would have little or nothing to do till about the middle of November, when he would begin his tour of inspection. During
these two and a half months however, I think he might be employed in preparing classified lists of clans, tribes, castes, and occupations for the use of his tabulating offices as suggested in section 930.
965. Census in Native States.—I think that at a future Census the same schedule as is used for British Territory should be,
as now, adopted throughout the Native States, not even excepting Hill States ; though active superintendence on the part of the
Superintendent of Simla Hill States and the Commissioners in political charge of the other Hill States will again be needed,
(section 951). And I would, when the first instructions are issued, and before any operations are actually commenced, invite the
Stales to send representatives to meet the Deputy Superintendent at Lahore, and there to discuss verbally the whole scheme
(section 953).
966. Form of Final Tables.—I would, I think, show figures for towns and villages separately in all the tables. The urban
population is exceedingly different from the rural in all respects ; and the comparisou would be most instructive. If it be thought
unwise to double the bulk of the tables by showing separate details for towns and villages in each district, those details might be
given for totals only. "Where separate figures are not given for urban, rural, and total population, it would I think be better to
ehow figures for urban and total population, rather than for rural and total ns I have done. It is true that the rural population is
more important and more normal than urbau ; but it is also so much larger that the variations in urban population are quite lost
in the total figures, which may almost be taken as identical with those for villages so far as proportions and percentages arc
concerned.
Table I.—I would omit at auy rate unoccupied houses. The figures are even more meaningless than those for occupied
houses (section 841).
Table II.—For "Increase and decrease per mille" I would substitute " number living in 1891 per mille living in i3Si," and
so save half the columns at the end of the table.
Table IIIA.—See discussions of these figures in section 291. The table as it stands is useless. The European schedule
should be remodelled (section 875); and I would separate race from sect, giving Native Christians in a separate table if the details
are required.
Table IIIB.—I hope, for the reasons given in section 196, that at the next Census all sects, both Mahomedan and Hindu,
will be both recorded and tabulated.
Table VI.—1 would tabulate the civil condition of the earlier ages in greatei detail, thus 0—5, 5—10 (section 687).
Table VII—I would take only ten-yearly periods after 35, thus 35—45, 45—55. 55 — 65, and so on (section 666).
Table VIIIA.—I hope the classification of castes will have been greatly facilitated before next Census (section 930). I think
all castes and all important tribes should be shown as now. But the '' total" column in VIIIA. should have " both yeses " last
instead of first, so as to allow of more ready comparison with the details for religion. I would omit details of Christians. I had
hoped to arrive at the composition of Native Christians by caste, but they frustrated iny desire by very gouerally returning Kristan as their caste.
Table XII.—The whole system of classification must, I think, he recast. The English system is utterly unsnited to the state
of society in India (sections 715, 716), and I would suggest the adoption of the proposals set forth in section 720. I hope the lists
of occupations to be published in connection with this Census will greatly facilitate the work in 1891 (section 930).
Tables XIV to XVII.—I doubt whether the details by age are of any value, unless the Sanitary authorities want them.
Table XIX.—The population by the present Census should be put first, and then that by the preceding enumeration. As now
arranged it is difficult to find a town of any given population in the table.
967. Final counsel to my successor.—I would commend to the careful consideration of the next Deputy Superintendent u!
Census the experience described in sections 918, 919 and 931, I hope that he will find his task facilitated by my labours ; and especially I hope that the detailed tables of caste divisions and occupational terms which it "is intended to publish will do an immense deal
to render the classification of these two entries simpler, more speedy, and more accurate. But I would once more earnestly recommend
him to insist upon a sufficient compiling establishment, to have a first-rate driving man to superintend his office, and not to let his
establishment go till the last table is passed in English and ready fur press. If I had known what was before me and hud acted on
these principles, I should have had my work finished six months earlier. I have left him a complete set of my circulars bound up ; and
this volume, together with all files that can possibly be of any use either to him or for purposes of office reference, have been made
over to the record department of the Panjab Civil Secretariat with my No. 3928, dated 18th April 1883, to the address of the
Secretary to the Panj£b Government, Home (General) Proceedings No. 119B of April 1883. I had intended also to leave him a
complete set of the registers and other forms used in abstracting, and the vernacular instructions issued. But the surplus copies
have been sold as waste paper, and I cannot complete a set. With the details given in this report be will probably be able u>
devise far better ones for his own use.

TherejgflfTjg n 0 a o u h t that the Census was carried out on a well-conceived plan, and that tho instructions were full and

" The instructions to enumerators were simple enough and left nothing to be desired. The great dilficulty was the Index-register
